SELECTED READINGS.

Or WOMEN SCOTS IND ENG-ram—The English- woman is wanting in physical and mental angles. She fulfils (in a Swinburnian sense of the word) her raiment, and she has the comeliness of a good digestion and an easy mind. The nature of the land that gave her being—its rich pastures, its fertile spread of tilth, its centuries of prosperous s--- peace—is innate in and impressed upon lam Her • Ithersio#, so fat as . alas has any, is optimistic and material.-rfitie •Toveat itt• an • easy, good-tempered, respectful way the duties of life, and in eld she • may well turn either Nick:eby or Gamp. Her voice is soft and pleasing to the ear; she talks with fluency, and with little difficulty of expression has Belden), apything ,to. -pay. She has no ideas and not many different- words; but her presentation of both is -endless,- as if the action gave her ininitetploasure.- Her mind, in short, is naked and not ashamed. She is not given to introspec- tion. She is not shy, nor is she impudent. She is honest, ylemant, wholesome, fond of sentimental fiction, the' verse of Weatherly and Sirs, the minstrelsy of Maybrick, and the burnt-cork melo- dists who never perform out of London, the art of Millais and the Christmas number generally. Physically and mentally the Scotswoman is more addicted to angles than her- southern sister. She Is not so comely; very often she is not comely at all. A gloomy'', sky and a still gloomier theology have statalsed• their mark on her, body and soul. Her high cheek bones are one result of many centuries of east wind, and the lines about her • mouth of a period less prolonged but more acute of John Knox. She is somewhat shy and reserved and awkward, speaks little, thinks much, and is com- paratively wanting in grace of manner. The Scotswoman is strong in mind and body, is truly - self-reliant, is welTilttcd to cope with adverse cir- cumstances—in a word, is really mistress of her fate. She is less neat and winning than trustworthy and capable. Her largeness and strength are not without a certain dignity. She shows to best advantage in the heroic moments of life, with whose petty and trivial worries her angularity refuses reconcilement. Exertion and the adversity of work are necessaries of life to her. The great increase of wealth in Scotland during the present century has shut out the female members of many districts from the need of tail; and the change has been by no means an unmixed good, for they have less to do than will keep them in perfect health. Although as regards looks the average Scotswoman is not equal to the average Englishwoman, yet are there faces in the north of higher and more spiritual type than any- thing you find across the Border. Indeed, the social averages are like the elimates ; the run in England is better, but in Scotland there are such days of brilliant light and exquisite shade and heartening and exhilarating air as England never knew. Also, in matters intellectual, the palm is with the north; and this is due a little to the scenery—for the Scots landscape, always touched by the inspiration of the mcnntain Air, is often rugged and is never common-place—and something

, to the Shorter C ltechism, which is an admirable training in logic, induces hhit of exact thinking, and is a complete systeal ut philosophy. The Scots 058erver. • A DROLL IDEA.—A correspondent has taken up the droll idea of there being a pleasure in tbe want of a character, or, more properly speaking, in being unknown. He is glad that he has no character to support—that nobody knows him. Hear how this happy fellow congratulates himself on his poverty —the blessed obscurity of his condition "I have just been thinking what a privilege it is to be poor and unknown, and what a blessing it is to be without a character. Nine-tenths of my enjoyments are such as are not attained by the wealthy or great. They are such as are not permitted to those who have character and reputation and station to sustain. The great pass through life on a high horse. They sit erect. Theft heads are elevated, and they move-.prondly on to their graves, without knowing' wr feeling a thousandth part of the beaut,es of the world i±i which they live. I, on the other band, with my characterless, poverty-stricken brethren, make the journey of life on foot. We

hasten not on our way ; we take it easy; we cull - - the flowers which grow along our paths; we avoid the thorns which obstruct them; aud when we come to a sunny or a pleasant spot, we sit down and enjoy its beauties, and take the refreshment and rest that our necessities may require. My occupations and amusements are such its the man of character and the proud man know not. They are engrossed with themselves, and see not and care not what

the world is doing further than it affects their Immediate, interests. Their natural ta sts are curbed, their impulses are restrained, and their feelings are concealed. Their whole life is a mask. They are ' star' actors on the world's stage, while we poor, unwashed gentlemen are tEe 'supernumeraries.' They have an arduous and difficult character to sustahr ;' while we have only Ici, 'hear their ranting, sing chorous M,thcir so,crack a joke, and have a little fun ambrigrititletv'ect" A SAFE noflt— btl ...pt obe an expen- , sive palfrey. THE EXAMINER. SATURDAY, MAY 27, 1893.

" CAPITA.L" CHAT. , , LADIES' LETTER _ Madame Patey has, it is said, requested • Mr. Vert, her agent, not to accept any fur% enter - i pr DEAREST NELLIE, ments on her behalf after next month an .1 is ; ' glance at the'postpmes of those ladies % generally understood that this step 's sit 'ny rte 1„. ivd4he fashionahle • uarters kif London nt preliminary to the retirement oft di ' did 4 , 't thi season has 'ç enced wiliprove that ' 4 kt contralto from public life. That a er it I ng, 6n. ''VfiSO skirt and the shor 4iitsted bolllC'e ourable,and doubtless renumerative caper,during at i revers or Victorian pelerine, are all but ir. ....daif least a) years of which she was our leading English . although a few women noted for their stylish contralto,the popular vocalist should desire to be re- toilettes affect the very short slightly-pointed lieved of the hard work absolutely necessary for a )basque in preference to a quite round waist. A prominent concert singer to undertake,of constantly 'good example of one of the styles most prevalent travelling in the provinces, often during the incle- Its seen in a shot green and fawn crepon, made merit winter season, may readily be imagined. But I with a double skirt, each of the two deep flqunces, that Madame Patey will, without a formal farewell lof which it is composed being edged with a very! and possibly a final tour of the provinces,take leave I narrow band of green velvet headed by a tiny of her numerous admirers is, we should imagine, 'beaded trimming. The bodice has double revers.. unlikely. The under ones, of velvet, are wide enough to i touch the sleeves, while the outer ones, which are; iof crepon bordered with velvet and beaded galon, are much narrower. There are, however, several new methods of cutting skirts, although the effect when finished' is apparently the same. Some are shaped on the principle of the circular cape—that is to say, with; 3ust a hole in the centre, of an enormously wide' round of cloth, for the waist. Another and far prettier style for thin material is that of using only two widths of double-width stuff. The front seam is straight, and the back a good deal gored. The extra fulness is gathered lightly in to the waistband all round, the greater part, of course, to the back. The fashion is most suitable for crepons, zephyrs, grenadines, and such thin mate- rials as are worn over an underskirt fitted tightly round the hips.

'ii

Meanwhile, it may be of interest to point out that Madame Patey, who is 51 years of age, is still in the prime of life, and that her magnificent voice is very little affected by many years of constant use in public. Janet Monarch Whytock was born on May 1, 1842,in London, but a few doors, as we have been told, from "Dolby's Chop House, where her great predecessor, Madame Sainton, 21 years previously, first saw the light. She was a pupil of Signor Pinsuti and of Mrs. Sims Reeves. She made her dant as a child at the Town Hall, Birmingham,and she first had her chance when, as a member of Mr. Henry Leslie's once celebrated choir, she was in- vited from the ranks of the chorus to replace an artist who was indisposed. In 1866,the year of her marriage to Mr. J. G. Patey, Miss Whytock first sang at the Three Choirs Festivals, and in 1670, on the retirement of Madame Sainton Dolby, she be- came nemine contradicente our leading concert contralto. In 1871 she toured with Mr. Santley and other British artists in the United States ; four years later she was invited by M. Lamoureux to sing the contralto part in the revival—which French musicans accepted in the spiritof interested curiosity—of Handel's " Messiah " in Paris; and only a year or so since she returned from a succes- ful tour in the Antipodes.

One of the most startling passages of Mr.:Glad- stone's speech on the second reading of tho Home Rule Bill was his quotation from a letter of the lion. J. E. Fitzgerald, Auditor-General of New Zea- land. Mr. Fitzgerald now in his seventy-sixth year, is the president of the Public Service Associa- tion of New Zealand, and at the last meeting of that body he intimated his intention of resigning on an early day and retiring into private life. The an- nouncement was received with general expressions of regiet. Mr. Fitzgerald comes next to Sir George Grey in widespread popularity throughout New Zealand. He was for years the most accomplished orator of the colony, and was on the high road to the Premiership when he politically effaced himself by accepting the Auditor Generalship. He was a close friend of Mr. Gladstone in 1842, and was on the staff of the British Museum when he resolved to join the first-batch of " Canterbury Pilgrims to New Zealand.

With the high coils and Grecian knot there was only room for a strip of bonnet, but the low chignon leaves a bare expanse of smooth hair between it and the curled frip4e, and the bonnet has expanded to Ztlie-rfre-deffcienck. Amongst the many and varied shapes of hat one with a black chip brim, a crown of cream Tuscan, and trimmed at one side with an accordion-pleated bow of lace, and a bunch of many-coloured ane- mones, has a very pretty effect. Many of the wide straw hats are lined with velvet, or velvet hats covered perfectly fiat are frequently made in two colours—black for the outside, and coloured velvet inside the brim. Amongst the new straws some have two or three rows of colour inserted, others show lines of coloured straw at the edge of the brim, and with some the .crown is of one colour, the brim contrasts, and for this style a light- coloured velvet to match the crown is used for lining the brim. One of the prettiest garden party toilettes which I have seen amongst this season's Paris fashions is one having a draped bodice, with small lapels in cardinal-red satin, enhanced with white guipure, which veils the neckband, and stripes the puffed sleeves to the elbow. Black silk net covers the skirt, and insertions of the lower part of the sleeves, which are divided with satin rouleaux, as well as those at the foot, further finished off with plain box-pleated niches. An elegant tea gown, was made of turquoise Surah, with three cross-cut frills around the skirt. The sleeves were full to the elbow only. Flounce of lace formed a basque, and there was also a double cape. The front was open, and orna• mented with bows of satin ribbon and bands of the lace.

Skirts that require lengthening when they are altered can be added to at the bottom, and the fact of such addition is hidden by bands or tucks of the material, or of a contrasting odour in velvet. According to an American paper the New York dressmakers are adding a yoke of velvet to such gowns, and putting the skirt on to it with a stand- up ruffle. This yoke is long enough to come over the hips. A very pretty addition to some of the new bodices is a narrow-pleated frill of satin ribbon placed round the edge of the bodice; the ribbon is about 3M. in width. Tucks of black satin are also put on over the hips, the highest about 4in. and the lowest about Din. below the waist. In this case there are no trimmings of any kind on the edge of the skirt.

Miss Olive Schreiner, the writer of" Life on an African Farm." has just arrived in this country from the Cape. She is a young lady still well under thirty i with rich clustering brown hair, and dark eyes and well chiselled features. She is low-sized and of very pale complexion. Miss Schreiner was born at the Cape. Her father was a German mis- sionary, and her mother who is still living, was an English lady, whose maiden name was Tindell—the nime of the principal character in the " African Farm." Miss Schreiner'd brother, Mr. William Schreiner, has just been appointed Attorney-Gen- eral at the Cape. Her elder sister is a zealous and eloquent advocate of the temperance cause in South lAfrica, and attracts enormous audiences to her lectures.

Miss Schreiner's, house is in a little settlement called Matiistontein, in the Karoo desert about one hundred and fifty miles from Cape Town. It is , named Schreiner House and consists of a sitting- room, a bedroom, and a pantry. Miss Schreiner is ot an asthmatic tendency, and finds the pure brac- ing air of the desert very beneficial to her health, and the solitude suitable for study and writing. She is a famoue horsewoman, and at the conclusion of her work, to which the morning is devoted, takes a brisk ride on her fast-trotting Cape pony. She is very foud of animals, and is always accompanied by a terrier called" Dick." "1 have chosen Dick;' , she will say with a pleasant smile, " for his ugliness. He is so ugly no one will take him from me." Miss Schreiner is a very keen politician, and is an en- thusiastic Rome Ruler. When in England a few years ago she silts frequently in the Ladies' Gallery in the House ot Commons. She has been heard to say that she could never understand the fascina- tion Mr. Gladstone exercised over the mind and heart until she had seen and heard him.

I wonder how many people would guess that the Queen does not spend more than ten pounds a year on gloves. She is " careful " with all her wearing apparel, and often wears a dress till it is quite " shiny ;" but in the matter of gloves it would be really difficult for her to be otherwise than econo- mical. She is so seldom in town that her stock of gloves mostly consists of those which ladies usually' wear for driving in the country, and which are con- sequently inexpensive.

Lentil Cakes.

_ Ingredients—lb. flour, 2 ounces butter, a pinch of salt (or more if preferred), ilb. cooked lentils and vege- tables mixed, frying oil,halt teaspoonful baking powder. Mode —Mix the flour, butter, salt, and baking-powder well together, then work in the lentils and vegetables, which should have been previously minced. Mix all thoroughly, and roll out about half-an-inch thick stamp into rounds with a pastry cutter or any fancy shape,and fry in boiling oil until quite brown. This is a very good way of using up lentils and vegetables which have been used for making gravy. These cakes .sre specially re- commended to travellers.

A Dv In Bed.

A French mother has hit upon a plan to preserve the health and beauty of her daughter, and has given it to her friends with the strength of her endorsement as to its efficacy. She declares that " the young lady went to a ball six days a week, but on Sundays stayed in bed, rising only for five o'clock tea and retiring again at an early hour. The result of this system was very happy. When the time came for going to the seaside, she was the only one of her companions who did not need its benefits. She was like a country girl, as fresh as a rose." This experiment, by the way, has been tried elsewhere, but not in the unchristian Gallic manner, and has been found efficacious. Two sisters, with arduous household duties, made a practice of spending one day each week— say Tuesday or Thursday—in bed, with the result that they were never ill, and for that matter never tired— never required a vacation or a change ef air. The sug- gestion, therefore, is not a new one, but it is at all times worth considering.

Why Milk Should be Boiled. The question of the habitual use of uncooked milk which prevails in this kingdom is, says an old number of the Lancet, one which demands serious attention. Again and again have milk epidemics of typhoid fever, scarlet fever, and diphtheria shown conclusively how severely the incidence of the disease in question has been felt upon those using uncooked milk, as opposed to those taking milk only after it has been boiled or other- wise cooked. At one time it was thought that the mat- ter involved only the question of cleanliness of the dairy, and the freedom of those engaged in its management from communicable disease ; and the mere fact of milk coming from country dairies has been held to be a suffi- cient guarantee of its wholesomeness. As a matter of fact our experience goes to show that the danger is at its greatest, according as dairies are situated in the mere remote and rural localities ; for it is precisely in scat- tered rural areas thst first cases of ill-defined sore throat and other affection in the families of those hav- ing to do with cows and milk are most likely to pass unnoticed. But experience has shown that disease in the human subject, whether unrecognised or concealed, is not the only—perhaps not the greatest—danger that has to be contended with in connection with our milk services. It has now been shown that the cow herself may suffer from a disease which at present is rarely, if ever, regarded as of any importance by dairymen, and which may give to the milk, at the actual moment of entering the pail, the power of producing scarlet fever in those who consume it in its raw state. Just as the excellent arrangements which have been made for the medical supervision of dairies must at times fail in cer- tain cases to prevent scarlatina producing milk from being despatched from the dairy. We would, therefore, urge once again the importance of using milk only after it has been boiled or otherwise cooked. And for those who have not habituated themselves to the somewhat altered taste of boiled milk as a beverage, we may note that if milk be boiled immediately after its delivery by the milkman, and be then Set aside in a cool place for some six hours, the taste which is sometimes objected to will be found to be almost entirely removed ; and not only so, but milk so treated keeps good and wholesome for a longer period than does unbolted milk.

SOME BIG CREATU RES.

The biggest fish is no doubt a shark, though I should not like to take the responsibility of saying which. The basking-shark and several other species attain a length of 40 feet ; the Rhinodon of the Indian Ocean grows to over 50. But the Challenger dredged up from the bottom of the sea, at a great depth, shark's teeth which were five inches long by four broad ; and if the owner of these bore the same proportion to his teeth as do those that are known, he must have been as big as the biggest whale.

Contrast with this the" mackerel midge," about one and one-quarter inch long when full grown. The sturgeon occasionally runs to 27 feet, with a weight cf 3,0001b5. •

Among invertebrates the biggeat of all are the terrible cuttle-fish or Equids, of winch small specimens are seen in aquariums under the name octopus. . .

But what would you say to such monsters as have been captured or cast ashore at Newfoundland, with bodies as big as those ot hippopotami, huge, horny, parrot-like beaks, tentacles 40 or 50 feet loin, and stud- ded all over with powerful suckers, weighing something like four tons ?

The biggest crab—in fact, the biggest of all the crus- taceans—is 011i3 which is caught off the coast of Japan. His scientific title is" Inachus kvempferi." Inachus has a claw four feet long (fancy that for supper—four feet of solid indigestion) ; the other legs are proportionately long ; so that it covers altogether about 25 square feet of ground. Other crabs, like the " Porcellandee," measure scarcely more than a quarter sf an inch in the diameter of their shell when full grown. Crabs present a curious exception to a general rule. Most of them got bigger in cold regions than they do in warmer seas. This is very noticeable with some European species which are widely distributed, much finer specimens being obtained around the Orkneys than are found in the Mediterranean. The biggest shell is the tradaena of the Indian Ocean, seen often enough serving some ornamental purpose, such as forming the basin at a gar- den fountain.

I think the great hairy, venomous spider which the London Zoological Society named after me, must be the biggest of its race. I brought home the first speci- men seen in Europe and sent it to the Zoo, where he created an immense sensation. _

Then, when the popular interest began to flag a little, this public-spirited spider revived it by shedding his skin in a most wonderful manner, like a snake, so that the cast off slough presented a perfect model of himself, down to every hair—in fact, when the keeper saw it in the case one morning bethought it was the spider him- self dead. And they examined the skin a long time before they found how he got out of it, it was so com- plete and unbroken, A sort of flap could be lifted up at the fore part underneath ; through this opening he must have put his legs out one by one, lifting and with- drawing them from their trousers, and then have shaken the entire envelope off his back. All sorts of food was given him, but his great weak- ness was dead mice ; these at first he used to suck dry, as a house-spider does a blue-bottle, but after a' time finding he could have as many as he liked, he used to eat their brains only, and the rascal absolutely refused to accept any. other diet. He would clip the back of the skull out with his pincers as cleanly as though it had been made of paper, and then settle down to enjoy his meal.

In his hems in the Brazilian forests this giant spider most likely lives on birds, but if so he must pounce down on them, as the web ird spins would hazily hold a cockroach. Once in contact with them, his bite would kill much larger animals.. 3

Six inches-would be a length that any garden worm might well be proud to claim, and so it would be in most countries ; but what do you thick of a worm—a real, true earthworm—six feet in length and proportionately thick ? They are not uncommon in South Africa, where they are easily caught after the rains, their muscles being too feeble to urge their huge and clumsy bodies at any great speed. Perhaps the aptly-named. Goliath beetle may be placed at the top Of the 350,000 species of in3eas—a huge monster, with black and white stripes, arranged in a fantastic pattern, like a very " loud " tennis- blazer. .

There is another beetle which is very common in the West Indies, and is there called the Dominica Woodcutter though it occurs on other islands as well as the one from which it derives its name. This is not so bulky as the Goliath, but it is a good deal longer, and would, in any case, rank second. Its length is due to the extra- ordinary prolongation which it bears in front, like the two nippers of a lobster's claw; and if the natives of Dominica and Martinique speak truly, it puts this apparatus to a curious use. Catching hold of the topmost shoots of growing plants it whizzes round and round at a great rate by means of its wings, until the stem is sawn through and the shoot falls to the ground ; then, not eating or in any way utilising this, it proceeds to another and repeats the same process—and so on, working as hard as wssible all day long all its life though. It is said, by this wanton des- truction, to do much damage in the cultivated parts of those countries.—Dr. Arthur Stradling,, in " Golden Days."

DARK WOOD. A TALE OF LONEMOOR. , CHAPTER XXX Tu. A BRIEF CALM. Upon the morning following the tragic events in the preceding chapter Lord Da.rkwood presented himself in the breakfast room at the usual hour, without a trace of uneasiness or anxiety in his manner.

His fat, full-moon face looked yellower than usual, it might have been noticed, however, and his thick eyelids were heavy with sleeplessness. He ate and drank as usual. No remorse of his crime came to dull his appetite After breakfast he rode as usual. He returned home, gave an interview to his land agent, and afterwards strolled about the gardens an park. Now he was master of Dunholm Castle, and none could wrest his title and honours from him.

After his solitary luncheon he paid a visit to the school room, in the east wing of the castle. He had not been wont to bestow thought upon his (laughter's education or welfare, but he desired to see again the beautiful young governess, who had so singularly interested him.

As he knocked upon the door of the school room, he heard music within. Gwen was playing the piano with a masterly touch. His knock was not heard, and he paused and listened. "That girl has a fortune in her fingers," he thought. "I wonder she doesn't give concerts. With her musical genius, and her beauty and presence, she would create a furore." He waited until the music ceased, and then opened the door. The two girls were at the piano. Gwen arose and received him courteously, quite as if she feltlerself mistress of the place. That princess-like air, for which she had been distinguished even in her baby- hood, gave her a peculiar charm. Lord Darkwood seated himself,. and watched as if fascinated. . _

He noticed that the Lady Georgina had greatly improved during the past few days. The ostentatious vulgarity of her manners had been subdued. She was dressed more quietly and in better taste, and her voice and her movements were more gentle. Yet she looked to him more than ever coarse beside her beautiful, high-bred companion. Affecting paternal interest, the marquis ques- tioned his daughter in regard to her studies. She replied to him flippantly and half resentfully. There had never been any affection between the father and daughter, and the Lady Georgina was not inclined to accept its pretence now. "Georgina improves rapidly. I foresee that she will do you credit, Miss Myner," he said. "I am greatly pleased with her progress so far. You must have had rare advantages in your own edu- cation. Were your parents wealthy 1" Gwen flushed painfully. "I have been an orphan from my childhood, my lord," she answered, in a low, troubled voice. "But I had kind friends, who spared no expense in my education." " That is easily seen," responded his lordship. "The expense was well bestowed," he added, gal- lantly. "But, pardon me, you seem better fitted for the drawing-room than the schoolroom. You do not look as if you had been educated for a governess." "I was not, my lord." The marquis did not push the subject further. He saw that it was unpleasant to the girl, and sur- mised that some reverse of fortune had plunged her from a state of affluence to her present dependence. He made a brief call and departed. As he entered the lower rooms Gwen's image haunted him. Her mysterious resemblance to the Charteris family was not so apparent now to him as on the occasion of his first interview with her, but her wonderful beauty, in its tender freshness and bright promise, had made a deep impression upon him. "If she were an heiress, I'd try to win her for my wife," he thought. "She is young enough to be my daughter, true—but I intend to marry a young woman. lam rich and can afford to marry a poor girl. If these Myners are of good family, the alliance might not be utterly unworthy of my con- sideration. I would prefer some proud county dame of influence and position, but this girl charms me. I'll inquire further into her family:connections, with a view to marriage." He re-entered the library to meditate upon his new scheme.

No trace of the previous night's tragedy remained. The blood-stain upon the hearthrug had been too large and deep for obliteration, but Pietro, with ready wit, had dropped several live coals upon, completely burning it out. The ser- vant had found the rug in the morning, had won- dered at the "accident," and had replaced the rug with another. . .

Lord Darkwood dropped into a chair, and gave himself up to thought. An hour later he went out to drive, returning in time to dress for dinner.

Throughout the day he had thought often of the previous night's tragedy, but no pang of remorse had pierced his stolid soul, It had occurred to him that, although Lord Darkwood had not stopped in London, and had not revealed the fact of his rescue from drowning to Mr. Sutton, or to anyone in Eng- land, andalthough he bad journeyed home under his family name of Charteris, yet he must have declared his identity to the Corsicans who had rescued him from the sea, and nursed him back to health, and very possibly he might have 'revealed his identity to others. Lord Darkwood communicated his ideas upon this point to Pietro as he dressed for dinner. "Without doubt," said the Maltese, "his lordship told the Corsicans just who he was; but we have nothing to fear from them. In the first place, they were ignorant sailors or fishers, and would not half comprehend his English name. In the second place, his lordship had a belt of money on his person, He paid them liberally for their care of him before .he left them. There was no occasion for him to write to them or send them money, so they will never appear here. The devil has helped you as usual, signor. You have always the devil's own luck." The marquis smiled as if he had received a com- pliment. He dined alone, and retired early to bed. He slept as sweetly that night as if no stain of bloodguilti- ness lay upon his soul, and no dreams marred his repose. The next raorainv he was seated at the breakfast- table wnen iue !cutler, alter Ins custom, brought in the postbag. Lord Darkwood unlocked it and glanced over the contents, sending the butler away upon some trivial errand. ' -

There were letters for the Lady Georgina, and one for "Miss Myner." The marquis turned this over in his hand. It appeared to enclose another letter, and he was tempted to open it. Here was a chance to learn something about his daughter's governess! but he laid the letter aside, promising himself to satsify his curiosity at some future time. There were letters for himself, and one thin blue missive for Pietro, addressed in a foreigr! hand.

Lod Darkwood slipped this thin envelope between two of his own, gathered his pile together, and Rushed the rest from him. "You may sort them oht," he said, as the butler returned. "Send them where they belong. I seem to have a larger number of begging letters and tradesmen's circulars than usual:"

He slipped his letters into his pocket, and went to the library. gaving locked the door, he pro- ceeded to open the letter addressed to his valet, takibg the same precautions he had employed on the former occasions.

!,!I earl easily slip this letter into the bag to- morrow when I look over the letters." he thought, "unless I decide to suppress it altogether. Well, what does Tomaso Tocino have to say ?" He soon discovered that Tomaso Tecino had very little to say. He informed his brother, in Italian of decidedly low class order, that the second adver- tisement had been inserted in the newspapers men- tioned by Pietro' but that no answer of any sort had been received. He said also, that appreciating his brother's anxiety to discover Miss Winter, he had sent a trustworthy man to Penistone and to Lone. moor to make investigations, and that the man had returned without having accomplished anything. The Quillets had caused him to be ignominiously ejected from the premises, declaring that Lonemoor was troubled beyond endurance with disreputable- looking men who came continually to make inquiries concerning her. "No special mention is made of my visit," thought Lord Darkwood. "I am included among the dis- reputable - looking' men, I dare say. Pietro— Tomaso's messenger—the girl's two lovers—whom else? Is any one else upon the track? Has any one else a clue to the mystery of her birth ?" The idea was too unpleasant to be long enter- tained. His lordship re-sealed the letter, and placed it in his notebook, which was not likely to undergo examination by his valet. "Where can the girl be ?" he asked himself. "Gone to the bad, the land bailiff of old Squire Markham declared. I must find her—l will find her. Yet, as I dare not employ a detective, and as Pietro

is eagerly conducting his search, I will make him my catspaw as heretofore, and discover the truth from his letters and those of his brother."

With this resolve he made himself quite at his ease.

't He had no difficulty in slipping the letter he had' stolen back into the postbag the next morning Anseen by the butler, and Pietro received it in due course, wondering a little at the delay in its trans- mission, but not suspecting that it had remained in his master's possession a day. The days and weeks went by in swift succession. No letter cattle front Corsica to Lord Darkwood. Pietro had been right. The marquis had settled his debt to his rescuers before leaving their island, and they:had not expected to hear from him again. No one suspected his lordship's rescue from death and return to England. The preSeiit marquis had absolutely nothing to fear, it seemed, on account of his awful crime, and no foro bodings troubled him.

Pietro and his brother changed letters fru- quently—under Lord Dark wood'ssecral super visiou —but no light was obtained by any of the three upon the mystery of G wendoline Winter's fate. The marquis made a journey or two to London to search for the girl, but having no clue to her whereabouts his search was futile, Pietro went up to town once also, but he was equally unsuccessful. Both believed the object of their seurch to be ignorant, coarse in looks, vulgar, but sprightly and fond Of dress. Searching for a person such as they imagined Gwen to be in the great town was like searching for a needle in a haystack. - Lord Darkwood would have given up all thought of looking for her had he not known Pietro to be still active and hopeful of discovering her. The very possibility that Pietro might find her stimulated him into activity, and kept him in a perpetual uneasiness and discomfort.

The winter wore on, and February was come. 'Still Gwendoline Winter was not found and even Pietro began to think her dead, or beyond his reach. During this winter the object of their search dwelt under the same roof with themselves, walked in the park and gardens daily with the Lady Georgina, and rode, and came and went before their eyes. and they never once suspected her identity. Ah, if they had Ina known! Lord Darkwood had, takei his place in county society, went to halls and dinner parties t and bore his honours with ease and dignity. He had not yet seen a lady whom he wished to marry, with the exception of G‘Ven, and even she, with all her beauty,- did not fully meet his requirements in a wife. He wanted to marry money, influence, and position, for he had not by any means taken his predecessor's place in the county esteem, as he well knew.. His affairs were therefore in a state of calm, as were those of Gwen. But the calm comes before a storm and already the clouds of coming events began to shadow these different lives., , ,

CHAPTZR %XXIV. GHOST OR OWL ?

In common with most ancient houses of size and Importance in England, Dunholm Castle Was popu- larly supposectto be haunted. The servants in their hall in the long winter evenings were wont to whis- per to each other wild tales of the Darkwood spectre that " walked " the grim chambers of the ruins. A tragedy had centuries since been enacted in the old castle, in which two /nen had fought over one fair woman. The men had both perished in combat before the woman's eyes, and she had gone mad in her horror and despair, and had soon after died. The Lady Georgina Charteris had a great love of the marvellous, and within three days after her arrival at Dunholm Caiitie had learned froin her maid the story of the 4irkwood spectre. Sho repeated the tale in turn to her young governess, and her faith was not to be shaken by Gwen's smiling dis- belief.

"It is true I" asserted the Lady Georgina. "Mar- gery tells me that her grandfather saw the ghost one night, when lie was hurrying home through the park, many years ago. He was courting one of the maids at the castle in those days—she married him afterwards, and she is Margery's grandinothor —and he was a park labourer, It was about mid- night, and he was on his way home by a short cut that skirted the ruins, when all of a sudden he heard a strange cry, and he looked up, and a light flashed across one of the broken windows, and an awful spectre, larger than any giant and bloody with wounds, was seen for an instant and then vanished, and the light went out in the same instant. What do you think of that, Miss Myner ?" "I think," said G wen, "that Margery'sgrandfather had a vivid imagination." The Lady Georgina looked offended. "Other servants have seen the same sights," she declared, "and I believe them. I wouldn't go near the ruins after dark, not if;by doing so I could be queen of England. I haven't had coinage to go yet in the daytime, although there have been no end of visitors-to see them. But I'll, go with you, Miss Myner. Let us go this morning." The morning in question was bright and pleasant, late in February, with the balminess of spring. Gwen assented to the Lady Georgina's proposition, and they made ready for a stroll.

The ruins a Dunholm Castle were historical, and visitors had been to examine them from far and near. The late marquis, like his predecessors, had shown them freely on stated days, but the present marquis had refused to admit strangers to their precincts, and had caused the doors to be secured and the windows to be repaired. It had become generally known to tourists and excursionists that visitors were no longer allowed to enter Dunholm Castle ruins, and Gwen, having learned the fact, had restrained her curiosity throughout the winter, and had not even once been in the ancient portion of the grounds. "How can we gain admittance to the ruins?" asked Gwen.

"Oh, I know where the keys are kept," said Lady Georgina. "I saw them in the library the other day, and asked father where they belonged. I'll go and get them." "You must ask Lord Darkwood for permission to visit the ruins," said Gwen.

"Oh, fudge I" cried the Lady Georgina, inele- gantly, as she dancing away on her errand. "I'll aek him if you say so—but what could he care ?"

The girl- sped downstairs, and burst into the library. Lord Darkwood was not there. The Lady Georgina, with brief hesitation, rushed to the ruins. The cabinet was not locked—a mere chance, for, it was usually kept so—and the girl opened it and dis- covered the keys of which she was in search, an returned to Gwen, whom she found equipped for their walk.

"Now, Miss Myner," she exclaimed, tossing up the great bunch of massive iron keys and catching them again dexterously, "I'll put on my hat and jacket in no time/and we'll be off."

- She hurried to 'her own dressed for the intended re4nble.

They set out together without delay.. They quitted the castle by the main entrance, wandered through the gardens, which were begin- ning to put on a show of verdure, and descended to the terrace.

"The park trees grow close to the ruins," said Lady Georgina, "and they make the ruins twice as gloomy as they would be otherwise. I'd cut them down if I were father, and rout out the ghost. When I become marchioness—if ever I do—l'll have the ruins pulled down. The very idea of a ghost frightens me." They passed round the angles of the now bnild- and.entered the thick shadows around the ruins.

There were several entrances to the ruins, One only being of importance. They avoided this, halt- ing before a little sunken iron door in a deep stozie portal. The Lady Georgina tried her keys, and presently the door swung open almost noiselessly. "I expected to be frightened at the creaking of this door," said Lord Darkwnod's daughter; "but it moves as if the hinges were oiled." "Perhaps the ghost used this door," suggested Gwen, mischievously, ".It moves as if in frequent use 1"

The Lady Gporgina looked over her shoulder timidly, half feitghtenecl at Gwen's suggestion, and then advanced into the ruin.

The girls rambled through the court and wan- dered through the rooms ,that opened from it. Every door was locked; but the Lady Georgina possessed the keys, and they explored all the different apartments. "The Darkwoods of those old days must have kept armies of servants," said Gwen, thoughtfully. "It must give you strange sensations, Georgina, to be. wandering like this through rooms that were tenanted centuries ago by your ancestors. In that big drawing room your foremother entertained her lovers, or welcomed her husband home from the wars. In the banqueting hall brave knights revelled; in this din i old chapel generations worshipped. And these people, who feasted and prayed, and loved and lived, were all of your blood and name." "I care very little for ancestry," said Lady Georgina, flippantly. "We prize most what we do not have," said Gwen, a faint cloud overshadowing her proud, sweet face. " I should like to kaow that Iliad had 4 long lire of honograble ancestry I zhould li

to know that I am descended from brave men and pure women—" The Lady Georgina stared. "Why, I supposed," she exclaimed, "that the Myners were of good family," Gwen started. "So they are I" she exclaimed. "But they were not famed for courtly deeds, for brave feats, for noble lives, as were your ancestors, Georgina. I think I have born in me a love of good blood. Of course a person may be born of bad family and still be good and noble, but no one can be sensible to the pleasures and advantages of having had worthy relatives." "You ought to have been a Charteiris, Marian, and the Lady Georgina with a smile. would appreciate the family far better that I I" The girls explored the various floors and mounted to watch towers, and looked afar over the landscape, upon the park and Dark River, the village and the distant spires of Shrewsbury, which glimmered the dim far distant. Then they descended to the first floor. "We've not seen the trace of a ghost, Marian'', cried the Lady Georgina, who had quite dismissed her nervousness. "It must have been exorcised long , "Ghosts don't flourish in the nineteenth centtui," laughed Gwen. "lam in a mood for explorations, Georgina. Let us go down and look through the gloomy old dungeons where prisoners were confined in the days of war." "Should you dare ?" cried Georgina. "Do yen, know, Marian, Margery told me that men had died in these dungeons centuries ago A skeleton was found in one of them within the memory of living men. It Was buried in Dunholm churchyard. Thee° must be the dungeon keys. lam not afraid, if yna are not. Come.

They had alreadynoted the entrance to the cellars and subterranean rooms, and were about to wend there way thither, when Gwen suggested that they would need a light. "I saw a lantern in the kit.then, with a box of matches beside it," said Georgina. "Before father's time strangers were admitted to the castle on Tuesdays and Thursdays, you know and they always ; explored the dungeons. The lantern was used by the servant who showed them those dark holes. There may be a little oil in it still. I'll see." She ran to the kitchen, her heavy steps echoing and re-echoing through the lofty stone chambers. Presently she returned, bringing with her a lighted lantern.

"There was oil in it !" she exclaimed "Am I not lucky? The cellars are under the kitchen, servants' hall and offices. The dungeons are under the main castle, and are entirely separate from the cellar& with a great wall of rock and soil between, Margery told me. This is the way to the dungeons. Come Marian." ,

Gwen held the lantern over her head, and they passed:down themide stone staircase, finding them- selves in a gloomy damp passage, from which Other passages branched, and from which massive Iron doors opened. "It was very like my idea of the catacombs," said , Gwen in a hushed voice. "The air is chill and damp here. "It is like a tomb," said Georgina. "1 should • think the ghost would make this dark hole his especial haunt 1" They rambled through the long passages unloglc- , mg a doornow and then, and peeping into some bla k cell, and then hurrying on, half-scared. They turned corner after corner, losing themsel7es in the labyrinth of passages, and Gwen said at last— "We must be near the farthest tower, Georgina. The lords of Darkwood had prison room enough for all their enemies, were their enemies a hest, I should think. We must thing of returning, I believe that I can find the way batik." A great rat skurried across the passage, brushing against Georgina's anlcles. She uttered a shrill scream of terror, that rang through the vault, echoing and re-echoing." "It's—it's the ghost !" gasped 'Georgina, learning 'against the nearest wall. "It was a rat --I saw it," said Gwen. - "It was a ghost !" asserted Georgina. "oh' Heaven, what's that ?" MANY a one who in the great crisis of life bee acted the hero's part has been shamefully overcome by some little sin or some secret temptation. The sudden irritation and the stinging word carelesly spoken in the domestic circle, the chafing and try- ing episodes in the tedious routine of our dui! 'work—these are the things that prove and weig our manhood more than any other.

FIRE under water can be produced by placing small piece of phosphorus in a conically-BWe' glass filled with water, and some crystals of Chlorate of potash covering the phosphorus, .and then , Mg through a long tube funnel a few drops or Juini; phtiric acid down on the mixture at the bottom 01 the glass. Tongues of flames can be seen flashing up through the water. The intense chemical action produces sufficient heat to inflame the phosphor under the water.

WHITSUNTIDE FESTIVITIES AT

RUNCORN.

In Runcorn, Whitsuntide is the chief holiday of the year, and is made the occasion for celebrations Bitch as do not characterise any other period of the Year. Natives who have had to remove to fresh fields of labour to fight the battle of life, religiously renew their acquaintance with the ancient town, and revive bygone memories on this anniversary. Friends and relations from all parts are welcome,' at Whitsuntide in the Runcornians home, and to he young people it is a season of unalloyed rejoic- Ing• The festivities redly commence on the Sunday. On that day more people are to be observed in the streets, and in the lanes.than on an ordinary sabbatb,and all,too, decked out in their newest and sprucest attire. A kind I 1 Informal pilgrimage is made to Runcorn Hill, that once charming spot nsw so much disfigured by the wurk sof the stmecutter, and on Sunday afternoon it may be truthfully said there were a thousand people there. Whether religion is the motive for the visit to the Hill is a debateable point, bet it is a fact that on Whit Sunday the Primitive Methodists of the town,follow the example Of their first organisers of Mow Cop Hill and conduct Ulm worship iu the open air. Thus is revived the

CAMP MEETINGS Of old. On Sunday last the worshippers attending the Chapel in Greenwav-road assembled at Delph Bridge, and walked in procession through the streets to Runcorn Rill, singing hymns in stirring fashion as they went along. In the afternoon Devonshire Square was the Place of assembly, and the procession to the Hill was witnessed by large numbers of spectators. And at the Hill addresses were delivered by the Rev. C. Finch, Rev. H. lt. Didcock, Mr. Cox (St. Helens), Mr. Sage ( Widnes) and Mr. R. Peacock. In the evening a " love feast ' Was held in the chapel the proceedings being conducted by the Rev. H. R. Didcock. The weather on Sunday had the merit of variety. Sunshine and shower were blended, sometimes un- pleasantly for those whose finery lacked the protection an umbrella, and fears were felt that a repetition of ''6l6 Wet

WALKING DAY AT EARLESTOWN.

Monday was the second occasion on which the various Sunday schools in Earlestown held what has now come to be considered the annual Walking Day. The morning broke with every indication of a brilliant day, but about nine o'clock dark threat- ening clouds hovered about, which caused some misgiving in the minds of the school officials. The sun, however, asserted itself, and after ten o'clock, a more beautiful day could not have been desired. The scholars were radiantly happy, and attired in their white dresses presented a gay and cheerful appearance. It was a regrettable fact however that the inhabitants did not take the same interest in the Sun- day schools procession, as they did in that in connection with the May Queen, nor did they exert themselves to make the streets wear a festive appearance by a display of buntieg. During the May Queen festival, there were many banners exhibited from private houses which were singularly inappropriate to the occasion, but when a suitable opportunity occurred for their mottoes to have some bearing on the festival in progress, especially those of a scriptural term, they were conspicuous by their absence. As before, the Church of England schools did not take any part in the procession, but why this should be, is of course better known to those responsible. Their 'absence of course caused some unfavourable comment I from the spectators. Ten o'clock was the time fixed for the schools to assemble on the Market Place, and shortly before that time they began to muster there, and were speedily placed in position for leaving the ground with- out confusion, by Messrs. Graham and George who officiated as Marshals. When all were gathered, the profusion of banners and ban nerettes, with the varied colours of the ch ldren's dresses, made the scene a very pretty and animated one. A couple of hymns were sung and the precession afterwards filed off in the followi•g order, Earlestown, brass band, Vulcan Wesleyans,Welsk Sunday schools, Earlestown Wesleyans, Salvation Army and band, Primitive Methodists, Haydock brass band, and Baptists. The route was from the Market Ground, by Market-st. along Queen, Houghton, Derby,Hotel-streets, Cross-lane, Bridge-street, Earle-street to Earle Cottage, returning by way of Bank-street, Wood, Viaduct, Regent. Sankey, Market-streets to Market Ground, where another hymn was sung, and after the National Anthem, the children marched to their various schools and dispersed. After dinner they reassembled at the schools and proceeded to various fields for the annual treat. The whole of the arrangements in connection with the procession were carried out by a committee of representatives from the various schools, Mr. J. R. Barr performing the arduous labours of secretary very efficiently. Inspector Kelly very kindly provided a staff of polise to see that the pro- cession was not obstructed or interfered with, and his forethought will no doubt be amply appreciated by the school officials. The procession was led up by Mr. Jas. Woods, of the Primitive Methodists, as being the oldest superintendent in the town, and on either side of him were Mr. T. Clare and Mr. B. Harrison, the next oldest superintendents. The positions of the schools in the procession was drawn for, and the premier place very properly fell to the Vulcan Wesleyans, as it is the oldest- established Sunday school in the district. The follow- ing are the particulars of the schools in the order of pro- cession : VULCAN WESLEYANS. These were headed by Mr. E.Martin and Mr.R.Naylor, superintendents. They floated a fine new banner which had only been unfurled that morning. The scholars as- sembled at the school at 8 30, the Rev. T. Wynne-Jones being present, and a large assemblage of visitors wit- nessed the ceremony of unfurling the new banner. The Rev. Wynne-Jones in a few remarks said it had been the unanimous wish that Mrs. Martin (one of their fellow- workers) should undo the strings of the banner, and he called upon her to do so. After Mrs. Martin had loosed the strings, the banner was raised amidst loud cheering. Mr. Jones said it was a inost beautiful piece of work,and he hoped it would lead their school to many victories in days to come. It was the work of Mr. John Jones, a resident in the village, on whom it reflected great credit. The procession then started for the Market-place. The number of scholars on the books is 180 with 24 teachers, nearly all of whom were present. They had tea in a field belonging to Mr. Williams, of New Hay Farm, and until dusk the children amused themselves with various games. WELSH WESLEYAN'S. The number on the books is 50 with 7 teachers, Mr. R. Owen, superintendent. These also had a fine new ban- ner been used for the first time on this occasion. They had tea in a field off Wargrave-road belonging to Mr. Cawley, and games were afterwards indulged in until dusk. BRUNSWICK WESLEYAN'S. Number on the books 460 and 76 officers. They mus- tered at the field after dinner, and afterwards proceeded Ito the school, where they were regaled with a substan- tial tea, each scholar on leaving the school being pre- sented with an orange. They returned to the field where cricket, football, and racing was the order of the after- noon, the youthful portion amusing themselves in scrambling for nuts and sweets. Mr. Sanderson kindly placed his field behind Messrs. Vicars's works at their disposal. SALVATION ARMY. . -

Number on parade 70. The band was considerably augmented by, players from other corps, and they made a creditable display. After the procession they returned to the barracks where tea was partaken of and the children were dismissed. The officers and adults pro- ceeded to Runcorn to take part in a demonstration there. rftrigrrivE METHODISTS. Number on the books 330, with 38 officers and teachers. These were headed by their banner, and the Rev.T.Collins, Messrs. Dickens and Austin, being also in front. After re-assembling at the school the scholars proceeded to a field belonging to Mr. Worsley in Burton- wood, where tea was served, and various amusements were indulged in until dusk. BAPTISTS. Number on the books 280, and 20 teachers. In front were Rev. T. Cottam Messrs. Eckersley, and Grissold. Mr. Sanderson placed' one of his fields off Grow-lane at the disposal of the Baptists, and here they indulged in a football match, married v. single, and after a very exciting contest, the benedicts were vanquished by 2 goals to 1. Tea was afterwards partaken of, and the remainder of the evening was spent in various games, the children having exciting scrambles for nuts and sweets. It should be stated that the L. and N.-W. Co. very kindly lent several of their vans for the conveyance of the very young children.

FATAL COLLIERY ACCIDENT AT ST. HELENS. On Saturday, a shocking accident occurred at the Sherdley Colliery, St. Helens, belonging to the. White- cross Colliery Company, by which Thomas Molyneux, a collier, aged forty-nine years, of Grace's-row, Ell Bess- lane, Sutton, was killed, and Peter Almond, a collier, of Alma-street, Peasley Cross, had a narrow escape. The two men were working the coal face in the four-feet tunnel, and were " gaining up," when, without the slightest warning, about six feet of roof and Eix feet of dirt fall. Molyneux was completely buried, and Almond, in trying to get away, was caught by his foot. 1 He extricated himself from this dangerous position by slipping his clog off, which was soon covered by the 1 falling stuff. Mr. Robert Turner, manager, with five other officials of the colliery, and a gang of about a dozen men at once set to work. It was estimated that about 50 tons of stuff had fallen. The gang worked hard and heroically, but eight and a half nours elapsed before the lifeless body of Molyneux was reached doubled up beneath some coal.—An inquest was held on Wednes- day on the body of Molyneux,ny Mr. Brighouse,coroner. Mr. Turner, certificated manager, produced a plan of , the workings.—Peter Almond stated that he and deceased 1 were colliers, and Joseph Molyneux and Joseph Almond 1 were drawers. They were at work on Saturday getting the bottom coal. Deceased was cutting. Some coal had been liberated, when suddenly an enormous quantity of top coal and dirt fell. ,Molyneux was buried, and wit- ness escaped by leaving his clog behind. So great was the fall that eight and a half hours elapsed before the body of Molyneux could be reached.—By Mr. Hall : The working place was six yards three inches wide. There were old works on two sides, and an old level below. As a rule they did not draw timber after the coal was got, as it was not safe • the roof would fall with it.—By Mr. Turner: They had some difficulty in getting down the coal, and had made application for extra pay on that account. If they had known then what they now knew they would have had a couple more props set on the higher side. There was plenty of timber.—By a Jury- man : Witness never saw such a place since lie started to work in pits.—Edward Baldwin, fireman, said he visited the place on the morning of the accident, and after examining it thought it was safe enough. He did not give any instructions as to timbering.—Mr.Hall,Gov- ernment Inspector,said he examined the place,and was of opinion that it should have been thoroughly timbered,at from three feet to four feet apart, so as to support the roof.—The Coroner said the question was whether Bald- win should have given further instructions as to timber- ing or have told the men they were working in a danger- ous place. The jury were of opinion that the accident could have been avoided by proper timbering, and re- turned a verdict of "Accidental death."—The Coroner, addressing Baldwin,said a fireman could not do too much timbering, but could do a good deal too little. Be ad- vised firemen to be extremely careful.

, MAY 27, 1893. ATI AT OUR. LOCAL CLIURCHES, WARRINGTON.

THE P.S.A. AT WYCLIFFE.

There was a large attendance at the usual Pleasant Sunday Afternoon meeting in Wycliffe Church on Sun- day. The membership still creeps up week by week, and the total number is now nearing 600. Mr. Yonge was the chairman for the afternoon, and in a short address spoke of the large congregetions he saw leaving the public-houses when coming to the meeting, and hoped some of the P.S.A. men would go to the public- house doors each Sunday, and give out handbills and invite them to the meeting. Those were the men they wanted to get at the services. He did not want the P.S.A. to become too respectable, but was anxious that it should get hold of these brothers of theirs. The Rev. T. Ross also delivered an address on neutrality in reli- gion. He didn't think men should be neutral in politics much less in regard to religious questions. Men at the prtsent time were living three different kinds of life— the devilish life, the moral life, and the distinctly reli- gious life. Christ when on earth never talked about those who stood midway. He spoke about the wheat and the chaff, the sheep and the goats. We knew what to do with the sheep and the goats but what should we do with the neutral? Mr. G H. Yonge sang as a solo , the hymn " Tell me the old, old, story " and a duet was contributed by Mr. Sherlock and Mr. Friar entitled " The Crucifixion." Mr. T. Atkinson and Mr. H. Bar- ker also took part in the service. THE INDEPENDENT METHODISTS.

A mission room in connection with the above-named denomination was opened on Sunday. The room is situated at the corner of Brick-st. and Battersby-lane, and was at one time a workshop for the blind. Ser- vices have previously been held in the vicinity in a room in Brick-st, but owing to some difficulty arising, it was found desirable to have another mission room. The present room is to be the nucleus of further exten- sions in the future when the promoters hope they will be able to build a permanent chapel. At the opening service in the morning addresses were delivered by Mr. James Shaw, Mr. Roberts, and Mr. T. Barton. Two sermons were preached, afternoon and evening, by Mr. Berry of Swinton, and were much appreciated. There were large congregations at all the services and the col- lections realised £ll Es. 90. ST. MARY'S ROMAN CATHOLIC CHURCH.

Special sermons were pr3ached at St. Mary's Roman Catholic Church, Buttermarket-st., on Sunday, by the Rev. Luke Rivington. M.A., at one time a clergyman of the Established Church. The services were on behalf of the day schools. There was a large congregation in the morning, when the rev, gentleman took for his text, 2 Corinthians ix. 15.—" Thanks be to God for His un- speakable gift." he said that day was naturally a day of thanksgiving, and it was because gratitude was pleasing to God, that Ile wrote it upon the hearts of men. It was a gift that had come from the very heart of God himself. It was for them that day to thank God for leading them into the Holy Catholic and Roman Church, th,s home of the spirit of God. He acted in- deed outside the precincts of the church, but, not as he did within. In conclusion he urged his hearers to sub- scribe liberally to the schools so that others might receive the holy gift and preserve it unto the end.—ln the evening the Rev. Luke Rivington preached from John x 16.—" And there shall be one fold." He re- marked that in these days people sometimes asked which was the Catholic Church and which was the church of the living God. It was of the first importance that their souls should thoroughly grasp the fact, that the unity of the church was the reat note to which their Lord Himself had vouchsafed to point them. He sent the apostles forth without the New Testament; they had not the gospels to give to men. Albeit they loved the Holy Scriptures. The book of God must be kept in the ,house of God. It was not a secular and profane book to be kept anywhere. The Catholics believed that the divine Lord appointed St. Peter to be under Himself,the ' visible head of the visible body. While among the Protestants they found all kinds of beliefs, and these beliefs continually changing, in the Catholic Church they found only one faith. The same fundemental truths, mysteries, virtues, and duties were impressed alike on Catholics throughout the world. There WAS a field of theological opinion where a man might exercise with freedom, but, let him touch an article of faith and he must be extruded from the Catholic Church. They looked to the priests, the priests to the bishops, and the bishops circled round the throne of Peter; and when he spoke, lo ! it was met with respectful obedience throughout the entire world by men of intellect and piety. Here there was unity.

SERIOUS ACCIDENT ON TIIE SHIP CANAL

An accident took place on Wednesday on the Ship Canal at Stockton Heath, which in some res- pects was similar to one at Ince about two years ago, but fortunately there was no loss of life. The scene of the disaster is against the new swing bridge at Stocton Heath, at the side of which about twenty men were engaged in making a lock to join a section of the Old Quay Canal to the Ship Canal. At about 3.45 p.m.,seven waggons,attached to an engine had just been unloaded, when from some cause, the engine driver lost control of the engine. and it com- menced to go down an incline, which led to the top of the lock. It then fell over with a crash into the lock, completely filling it, the lock being about ten feet in depth. A bricklayer who was on the ' Stockton Meath-road had observed the oncoming train, and gave warning to the men working in the lock, and they had just time to escape, or otherwise they would almost certainly have been killed. The engine driver stuck to the en- gine, and, wonderful to relate, he escaped without injury. The wagons are a complete wreck, most of them being completely shattered. The engine, which is one of the most powerful of the Ship Canal engines, and called the Gloucester, sustained very slight injury, as the wagons beneath saved it from any severe concussion. Among other damage a theodolite has been broken. Mr. Jackson, the con- tractor, was quickly on the scene, and looked over the work of reparation. About thirty men were set to work at once, to repair the damage, and at about 7 a.m. on Thursday morning, rails had been laid close to the engine, and it was pulled out. The work of getting out the wagons, however, was a more serious difficulty, as they w,,re jammed in the hole together, and the work was continued during Thursday. The news of the accident quickly spread about Stockton Heath, and large numbers of people were soon on the spot and and watched the work of getting out the engine with much interest. We understand that the loss caused by the accident will fall upon the contrac- tor, Mr. Jackson, and not upon the Ship Canal Co.

REMINISCENCES ON THE BANKS OF THE DEE, AT PARKGATE.*

BY MELROSE. Oh! wonder-working Time. Thy seythe.liath inark'd this as a ruined prey ;

We charge thee with a crime, Thy wandering must have led thee far astray . .

To hurt this noble stream

Where once a county's hope and joy did beam.

Commerce received a blow,

When thou with allies one and all com.bin'd Resolv'd to overthrow A city's pride, with budding wreaths entwin'd !

- Sad was the awful day Thy myrmidons began their wretched play !

Sweet Dee, thy F hallow stream Did once well cope with Britain's proudest river ; The sun of hope did gleam In majesty to make thee fam'd for ever ; Yet unsuspecting deep, Thy bed was destin'd for the ocean's sweep !

Nor worldly sycophants Alone do honour to thee with their praise ; Aurora gently plants Her footsteps o'er thee to transfix the gaze ; The tranquil dawn of day,

Unto perfection dotfi thy form dispi;y.

Luxuriant 'neath her beams, The sun rides o'er thine ever placid form,

Each tiny wavelet gleams, So innocent of any angry storm. A gorgeous spacious wave, As still and holy as the good man's grave !

But,.dark'ning night returns,

And my fond view is vanishing apace; With grief my bosom burns . . . . _

For now the lasCiaint sion can I trace, Farewell, sweet stream, no more I tread thy verdant, quiet honour'd shore ! * The River Dee, several miles wide has been shallowed by the banks or bars of sand, which from time to time have been cast up by the sea.

On Thursda-.37-a-t-;;;;;; Coic7i-n-eir Charles Henry Laurd, R.E., a Local Government Board Inspector, held an inquiry at Widnes Town Hall, in respect of an application of the Town Council to borrow £15,516 Is. 6d. for works of private street improve- ment. There were present the Mayor (Alderman Gossage), Alderman Sadler, Councillors F. Neil, W. Christie, S. Owens, H. B, Shores, H. S. Oppenheim (town clerk), T. Craik (deputy clerk), S. Hoyle (accountant), J. S. Sinclair (surveyor), Dr. O'Keeffe (medical officer). Woodhouse ( Woodhouse and Willoughby, architects of the Technical Schools. 1 The Town Clerk said he appeared on behalf of the Corporation to support the application to borrow £15,516 Is. 6d. for works of private street improvement The notices had been duly served upon the owners and occupiers under section 150 of the Publie Health Act, 1875. The time specified in those notices had expired some time ago, and no work had been undertaken by either the owners or occupiers. The streets comprised in the application were the following :—Waterloceroad district No. I. Comprising the streets and passages from Hutchinson-st. to Milton-st., the estimated cost being £4,866 10s. Waterloo-road district No. 2. Comprising the streets and passages from Mil- ton-st. to Church-st., the estimated cost being £6,727 6s. 6d. Mill Brow district. Comprising Joseph-st., William-st., and the passages connected therewith estimated cost £3,920 ss. The object of the inquiry was to enable the Corporation to borrow the money for a term of ten years, the whole of which would be repaid by the owners of the property by in- stalments. This was the usual practice of the Corpora- tion, and it had been a most convenient and beneficial practice for the owners and occupiers.—The Inspector said he had an idea that the Local Government Board was reducing the period of these loans to seven years.— The Borough Accountant said the Corporation had one loan at seven years, and others at ten years. The loan at seven years was one of the earlier loaus.—The Inspector said he merely mentioned it because he had heard it was the wish of the Board to restrict the num- ber of years. He would mention ten years to the Boarct and they could act as they wished.—The Surveyor pro- duced plans and gave the Inspector particulars as to the manner in which it was proposed to sewer the streets. He mentioned that in all cases the gradient was very good,and they would be connected with the main sewers which emptied into the Mersey. The passages were generally nine feet wide. The Town Clerk said the Corporation also wished for sanction to borrow £520 for a public improvement in Appleton village. This was a sewer, which commences near the Baptist Chapel in Deacon-road, passes through Appleton village and terminates in Birchfield-road. The work had been nearly done and the Corporation now wished to pay for it. The work was done with-et the sanction of the Local Government Board under the fol- lowing circumstances. This sewer formed part of a scheme which.embraced the improvement and sewerage of Peelhouse-lane, and when Widnes, like other places, was threatened with considerable distress in winter, the matter was brought forcibly before the attention of the Council by the Board of Guardians, the Council took in hand what works they could with propriety for the bene• fit of the aistrict, and which must have been done sooner or later. This was one of the pieces of work they undertook, to provide work for the unemployed.—The Surveyor produced plans and gave details of the sewerage. The Town Clerk said he also desired to make a further application to borrow £10,415 required to erect schools for technical instruction and public free library, under the Free Libraries Acts and the Technical Instruc- tion Act. He was happy to say that the Council were not called upon to pay for a site, which had been given by the Trustees of the Lech estate, and comprised 2,000 square yards. A further application for permission to borrow £10,415 for the purpose of erecting schools for technical instruc- tion and a new Free Public Library. The combined income of the Technical Instruction Committee was £1,725, which was ample to provide for the repayment of the principal and interest, and for carrying out the work of the two institutions. The Corporation were at present carrying on various classes in Science and Art in buildings which they rented, but it was felt that the scope of their work should be much enlarged and greater opportunities given to those who desired to improve their education. The Free Library was a most valuable institution. It contained 5,708 volumes, and the daily average issue was 190. The daily average attendance at the reading room was 519. At present the Library was housed within a building for which they paid rent, and which it was highly probable would be utilized for other purposes whsn a contemplated extension of the Town Hall took place. The Inspector would thus see that there was every reason for providing ample accom- modation both for the teaching of technical subjects and for the numerous frequenters of the Library. In reply to the Inspectors, the Town Clerk mentioned that the application had not been advertised in the usual way, but the Corporation were wishful that the work of construction should commence as early as possible and they therefore wished him to deal with the question at that sitting.—The Inspector said it was highly probable that the Local Government Board would require another meeting to be held in order to conform with the usual regulations, and Councillor Shuttleworth said the ratepayers wh3 objected to the scheme should have an opportunity of coming forward and stating their objections.—The Inspector Quite so. —The Town Clerk said the scheme had been unani- mously approved by the Technical Instruction Commit- tee, the Free Library Committee and the Town Council and, he believed, had the unanimous approval of the burgesses of the borough.—The Mayor, Councillor F. Neil, Councillor H. B. Shores. and Dr. O'Keeffe con- firmed this statement.—ln reply to a question as to the probable date of a future inquiry, should the Local Government Board deem one necessary, the Inspector said he could only make his report to the Board and that Board was a mill which reeved very slowly and ground exceedingly small. The inquiry could not he held much within a month.—This closed the inquiry and the In- spector inspected the plans and site.

THE QUEEN'S BIRTHDAY.

Queen Victoria on Wednesday entered upon her 75th year. Her age has been exceeded by two occupants of the English throne only—George IL and George 111., who attained the ages of 77 and 82 respectively. On the 20th of June next, her Majesty will have reigned 58 years, a length of time exceeded by one predecessor only —George 111. who occupied the Throne for nearly sixty years. Henry reign 'also extended to 56 years.. The Queen has been a widow more than 31 years. The only Sovereign older than her Majesty is the King of Denmark.

Queen Victoria is writes a London correspondent, so bountifully endowed with children and grandchildren that it is a little difficult to realise the intense interest and importance attaching seventy-four years ago to the slowly disseminated fact that on the 24th of May, 1819, the Duchess of Kent had given birth to a daughter. Joy at the event was accentuated by the still vividly remem- bered grief ef the nation at the death, only eighteen months before, of George lll.'s only grandchild, the Princess Charlotte. During those months the sons of the King alone stood between the Throne and the deluge. The little Princess taken down from Kensington to her father's cottage, near Sidmouth, became, therefore, "the pillar of a people's hope, the centre of a world's desire," and the events of her infancy quickly strengthened the anxiety of the country for her life. For she was only six months old when the death of her father brought her one place nearer to the throne; while six days after that, by the death of George 111., and the accession of the Regent, another step was gained, George IV. stankihag between the baby and the succession.

PORK-PIE POISONING.

Cases of food poisoning are just now occurring with unusual frequency. Possibly (says the "British Medical Journal ") the hot dry weather is responsible for this, tempting manufacturers to hide under a pie-crust what will not bear an examination by sunlight; and unfortu- nately for the butcher, the action of sunlight or electric light will not destroy the bacteria which have found a resting place in the meat he.offers for sale. We shall ever hear of cases of meat poiscning, as sad as they are unexpected, until we have some means of control over the manufacturers of tinned .neat or the various kinds of pie. In the meantime, people should be warned, and learn that those who partake of meat pies offered for sale in shops and restaurants do so at their own risk. A I case is reported from Peckham where three persons were poisoned after eating pork-pie. The symptoms ware characteristic of what is called ptomaine poisoning, and so were the post-mortem appearances in the unfortunate woman who died. It seems that several persons in the neighbourhood who partook of pork pies derived from the same source were also taken ill. The whole case is being investigated by Dr. Stevenson. Another case of pork-pie poisoning is reported from Yorkshire. Here a whole family of five were poisoned,but fortunately with- out any fatal result. As people either will eat what ;is offered to them, or, for economic reasons, are com- pelled to do so, it seems desirable that the supervision over our abattoirs and manufacturers should be stricter , that it is. In this respect Germany, and especially Prussia, are far ahead of us. The supervision must be directed against the wholesale manufacturer or salesman to be of any use, and the latter should be induced to recognise his responsibility.

SUB-LETTING A CORPORATION CON-

TRACT AT WIDN ES.

Before Mr. John Brock and Mr. G. Eil kington at Widnes Petty Sessions on Thursday, John Martin, a bricklayer of 54 Wellington-st., sued Messrs. Sayce and Randle, contractors, under the Employers and Workman's Act for ZI O. 10d. wages alleged to be due. Mr. Peters appeared for the complainant and Mr. Knowles for the defendant. Messrs. Sayce and Randle are carrying out a sewerage contract in Lowerhouse- lane, for the Corporation and on the fifteenth February. Martin was taken on by a man who was only known as " Cockney Harry" and who was represented to be the foreman of the firm. He worked until Saturday at noon, 24 hours but when pay-time came the foreman was nooa e.gt, and the men went to Mr. Randle who said he had paid the man the wages and they would find him drunk in some publichouse. One of the workmen asked if 1 • Cockney Harry" was a sub-contractor awl Mr. Randle said it was an impertinent question. Eventually the firm paid the mei.' half their wages.—The defence was that the job was let to " Cockney Harry " at so much a yard and the complainant was his servant and not the servant of Messrs. Sayce and Randle, and it was a piece of generosity on their part to pay half the wages. In reply to Mr. Peters, Mr. Randle said the firm agreed with the Corporation in their contract not to sub-let the works. He had written perhaps about March the 2nd to the Corpsration denying that their work was sublet,and that the brick work was being done by day work.—Mr. Sayce said they had not a written contract with" Cock- ney Harry," and he declined to state at what price per yard the work was let. He did not know that the sub- contractor was a " drunken thief " ; the whole of the men knew that the work was sub-let.—A man named John McCabe corroborated the latter state ment.—Tne Bench dismissed the case. McCabe was allowed 2s. 6d.

MR. GLADSTONE'S EYESIGHT.

A Chester Correspondent says that Dr. Granger, ophthalmic surgeon to Chester Infirmary, saw Mr. Glad- stone on Wednesday at Chester, where he arrived about noon, having driven from Elawarden Castle. It will be recollected that Dr. Granger attended to the injuries in- flicted on Mr. Gladstone at Chester in June last. Dr. Granger says the mark of the injury has completely dis- appeared. Tested with the usual tests Mr. eilaclstone's sight is above the ordinary standard, even in the eye that was injured. In fact Mr. Gladstone's sight is un- usually good for his age. In referm,ce to the report in the papers that during a recent discussion in the House Mr. Gladstone appeared unable to decipher some reprint or manuscript, Dr. Granger said that after the tests to which he had subjected him, he was of opinion that this was due to the light, and not to defects of vision. The doctor added that he had advised Mr. Gladstone that ordinary work would inflict no undue strain upon his eyesight, and that he need take no special pre- cautions.
