THE INTERNATIONAL CONFE RENCE

OF MINERS.

DEBATE ON THE EIGHT HOURS QUESTION.

The International Miners' Congress assembled on Monday at Brussels. Among the English delegates pre- sent were Mr. Thomas Burt, M.P. ; Mr. Pickard, M.P. ; Mr. S. Woods, and three other members of Parliament. The prominent foreign delegates included M. Calvignal, Mayor of Carmaux, and M.M. Basley and Cortiier' Mr. Woods was elected English chairman, and M. Calvignal, French chairman, with Mr. Pickard as secretary of the Conference, and Mr. Burt as treasurer. After the usual examination of credentials, ' the first British resolution was brought forward by the Barnsley delegate in favour of an international eight hours' day. He informed the Conference that the pas- sage through the British Parliament of the Eight Hours' Bill was noW assured, and it was evident many other countries were resolved to obtain the same reform. M. Rondet strongly supported the resolution, which was also favoured by M. Pirdboenf on behalf of the full Bel- gian delegation.—M. Cinfzer (Austrian) opposed the re- solution,and moved an amendment thereto, sating forth that the conditions of labour being different in every country, each country and district should be left free to obtain an eight hours' day by the means best adapted to its local eircumstauces.—Mr. Bailey,an English miner, and Mr. Kerres. a Northumbrian delegate,made speeches against the idea of obtaining an eight hours' day by Act of Parliament.—M. Easley pointed out, in ieply to the argument of the last two named speakers, that the North of France was always flooded with English coal whenever a strike was in progress, and that proved that without international union and common legislation the objects of labour were unattainable. The International Miners' Conference at Brussels re- sumed on Tuesday morning consideration of the question of a legal eight-hours' day. The discussion was carried on intermittently for a great part of the day, but led to no definite result, the proceedings being abruptly sus- pended. At the commencement of the sitting it was announced that MM. Basley and Lamendin, two of the French delegates, had been summoned to appear before the Director of Police, and on their return M. Basley re- ported that he had been called to account for his action during the recent strike riots in France, and that he and M. Lamendin—both members of the Chamber of De- puties—had been ordered to cross the frontier before midnight. This led to an excited debate, in which threats were held out that the whole of the French dele- gates would retire as a national protest aeainst the action of the Belgian authorities. A deputation was sent to the Minister of the Interior to ask for the revoca- tion of the order of expulsion. This was refused, where- upon a resolution was passed denouncing the action of the Belgian Government as being aimed at international federation, and exhorting the general body of French representatives to remain at the Conference. The latter appeal was acceded to, and the delegates then rose and I accompanied MM. Basley and Lamendin to the railway 'station in a body, loudly cheering them as the train departed for France. There were nO such scenes of excitement at the Brus- sels Congress of Miners on Wednesday, as marked the , proceedings on Tuesday. Business went on quietly , throughout the day, and satisfactory progress was made in disposing of matters coming before the Conference for consideration. The discussion upon Mr. Bailey's resolu- tion affirming the principle of a legal eight hours' day was ' resumed, and the resolution was ultimately carried, the , number of miners represented in the voting being—For, ' 1994,000 ; against, 100,000. It was also resplved that should it become necessary owing to the various Parlia- ments refusing a legal eight hours' day, a general strike should be declared to obtain the same. Several of the Cont- inental delegates were strongly in favour of fixing a date ' on which the eight hours' system shoulti be conceded, and Mr. Burt, M.P., as strongly opposed the resolution, ' contending that a general strike was utterly impractic- able. Royalties and wayleavea and the employment of ' women in mines were also among the subjects dis- cussed. At the International Miners' Conference at Brussels on Thursday a motion by a French delegate in favour of uniform hours for all workers in or about mines elicited much diversity of views. The majority of the British delegates abstained from voting, but the seven delegates representing the Miners' Natimal Union voted against the motion. The voting—on the basis of members represented—was as follows :—For the motion, 299,000; against, 100,000 ; neutral, 585,000. A proposal of the Continental miners that inspectors should be appointed by the men, though paid by the State, was defeated by 850,000 to 424,000, but a resolution calling for a more efficient system of inspection was passed unanimously.

LOltD SALISBURY IN ULSTER.

Lord Salisbury on Tuesday entered on the political campaign in Ulster which was originally arranged for I the Easter recess, but had to be abandoned because of the illness from which his lordship then suffered. The over-night journey from Euston to Stranraer was unbroken by an incident of note; but at Stranraer, where his lordship took steamer for Lame, the Hon. H. Dalrymple, son of the Earl of Stair, presented an address from the Unionists of Stranraer and district At Larne, _ . . . _

I Lord Salisbury was received by Lord Londonderri, I Lord Ashbourne, Lord Antrim, Captain M'Calmont, and members of the Larne Unionist Club ; and later in the day was welcomed to Ulster by the chairman of the Town Commissioners, on behalf of the inhabitants of the district. In reply, the Ex-Premier said it afforded him great pleasure; on his first visit to Ireland, to visit such a prosperous district. That prosperity, he said, depended on the maintenance of the union with Great Britain; and on the vigour with which the Unionists now resisted Home Rule depended the welfare and destiny of Iriskmen, Englishmen, and Scotchmen for generations to come. During the journey from Larne to Belfast Lord Salisbury was heartily cheered at varions stations. A popular ovation awaited his lordship in Belfast. At the Northern Company's railway terminus he was pre- sented with a general address, including one from the City Council. His lordship briefly replied, expressing thanks for the hearty welcome, and his appreciation of Belfast's steadfast attachment to the maintenance of the Union. The noble marquis, in the Lord Mayor's carri- age, was then about to drive to the County Down Station, but the crowds in the street took the horses out of the traces, and themselves drew the carriage through the principal thoroughfares, which were decorated with flags. At County Down Station he took train for New- townards, en -mete for Mount Stew-art. At Newtown- ards the cordial demonstrations were repeated, and the Town Commissioners presented an address. Lord Salisbury continued his political campaign in Ulster on Wednesday night, when he spoke at a crowded meeting in Belfast. He said the Home Rule Bill could not be amended to be made acceptable, and in discussing it the Unionists were simply hoping to point out the real nature of the evils, calamities, and absurdities it involved. The talk about Imperial supremacy was only a formality, and it was the same thing with regard to the veto. The current of the world's course was not towards separation but consolidation: and while admit- ting and failing to analyse Mr. Gladstone's marvellous power, he could not lose sight of the fact that the Home Rule project was the scheme of one man. Mr. Gladstone had always yielded to pressure that which he had prev- iously declared to be inviolable and sacred. It seemed paradoxical, but that reputation was for the moment the Prime Minister's strength, every interest and fad expecting to extract concessions from this universal yielder. Time was on the side of the Unionists, and one man could not fight against the people. Resistance to the House of Lords meant resistance to England and to Ulster, and the Peers were not likely to be frightened by a Justin M'Carthy agitation. He characterised the Home Rule Bill as an " intolerable and imbecile bill," as this " accursed bill," and as " this insane eccentricity of I a single statesman," and urged Ulster to do all in its power without resorting to violence to secure its rejec- tion.

At the Ulster Hall, Belfast, on Thursday Lord Salisbury was presented with nearly ninety addresses from public and eoclesiastical bodies and political associatims. In reply, he sail the variety of the addresses showed the width and depth of the Unionist feeling in Ireland. It was not the result of One school of thought, nor the action of one party, but an outburst of the genuine feel- ing of attachment to the state of things under which they lived, and apprehension of the dangers that were lowering, which had come frbin men of all classes and creeds, who hoped that their remonstrances might over- come the influence which ignorance and optimism threatened them with. He repudiated in the strongest terms the suggestion that even if Home Rule were deferred now Unionists would adopt some similar plan. He expressed confidence as to the result of the Unionist resistance to the Home Rule proposals, paid a tribute to the loyalty of the Liberal Unionists, and said historians would- find it hard to get another instance of a party so disinterested, so faithful, and working with such pure and unmixed regard, thinking only of their country, and sacrificing many old associations, anti- 1 pathies, and preferences in order that they might be equal to the great emergency, and that their country might have an adequate band of defenders in her need. Lord Salisbury afterwards lunched privately with the Lord Mayor. After luncheon, the carriage in which the Marquis rode was dragged by working men to the Great Northern Station, from whence he left by special train for Baron's Court, the residence of the Duke of Abeicorn, on his way to fulfil his enga,,oementS at Lon- donderry.

RETURN OF UNIONISTS TO WORK.

A SAD OCCURRENCE.

This has been what in future will be known amongst the Hull dockers and Alte_PnbliC.L.aller- ally as "Resumption Day." Contrary to expectation, writes the correspondent of the "Daily News," on Tuesday,everything has been quiet and peaceful in the vicinity of the town docks. A number of men assembled at eix o'clock in the morning waiting "for something to turn up," but; as A matter of fact, there were six times more docks willing and anxious for work than the employers could engage. The selection of gangs for various ships was a matter that excited close observation and much critic- ism. Unionists and non-unionists were sent to work in the holds together. Seven weeks ago there were no non-unionists employed on the docks, and under the circumstances it was anticipated that' disturbSnces would take place. The pessimists did not, fortunately, have their expectation realised. At one time, however, it did seem as though a disturbance would take place in front of Messrs. Wilson's officies. During the forenoon about six hundred men gathered at thp en- trance' and expressed loud discontent at an order of the Hull Dock Company that before men should be ad- mitted to the dock a guarantee must be signed by which they would forfeit their pass on to the quays if they molested, intimidated, or threatened any fellow- workman. Mr. C. U. Wilson, on his arrival in town addressd the men. He pointed out that his firm, had nothing to do with the order of the Dock Company, which was simply issued to protect the dockers. There was work for 500 men on his ships, and he thou:Olt it a pity that there aheuld be this disagreement. What his firm desired was to get all their old hands whc; had wives and families, back to work as soon as possible. With that object the free labourers would be removed from the Albert Dock by the end of the week ; but he wished it to be understood that his firm brought the free labourers to Hull, and they would do so again if the dockers struck work as they did on the sth of April last. Be and his brother, added Mr. Wilson, were prepared to give a sum of money if matters now went smoothly, to alleviate the suffering and starvation existing in the town. Early in the morning Mr. Chesterfield, the district secretary of the Dockers' Union, attempted to take hisi life by cutting his throat. Mr. Chesterfield had for some weeks been suffering acutely, and the excitement and anxiety of the past few weeks have had a very depress- big effect upon Wm. About half-past five on Tues- day morning Mrs. Chesterfield was awakened by hear- ing a noise downstairs, and rapidly dressing herself she descended into the kitchen, the door of which she found to be open. Passing through to the yard, she saw her husband with blood fiowing from a gaping wound the throat. Dr. Stothard was at once summoned, and in reply to a written query by the patient as to his chances of living said " For the next forty-eight hours there is ' a danger of shock to the system, but taking into con- sideration your temperate habits I think there is really a chance of your ultimate recovery. The wound is a serious one, and it will be several months before your convalescence." Mr. Chesterfield also wrote : "I did this myself. I cannot face the misery I see before the men through the action of the Shipping Federations. They do not intend to perform their part of the agree- ment, but mean to crush our men. Buck, my cash is all right. Keys in my pocket. Fifty pound note in the houso to be seat back to Sanders, of Rotterdam. This I was a loan ; but we did not want it. Send it back. My wife knows I kept it here for safety. Why don't they send the blacklegs away,and open the docks to our men. I have no means. I have only a delicate wife. She can- not do for me. Davis gave the keys to me last night. The cash box is in the desk with his branch money 4 You will find the receipt-books in the desk, £3 I3s. 6i. On inquiry at the Royal Infirmary, I learn from the doctors that there are improved hopes of saving the life ofildr. Chesterfield, who at the commencement of the strike refused to accept his wages as district secretary, preferring rather to take strike pay.

GREAT HOME RULE DEMONSTRATION.

A great demonstration in favour of the Home Rule Bill was held in Ifyde Park on Sunday. It was estimated that nearly a hundred thousand people were present. Speaking took place from twelve platforms, the speakers including several members of Parliament. Mr. J. Dillon, M.P., speaking at one platform, referred to the retention of Irish members in the House of Commons, and said that if the English Radicals desired it the Irish members would remain at Westminster after they had got Home Rule, to carry out other reforms; but if the Radicals did not care to have them they were perfectly content to manage their own affairs. Mr. Michael Davitt, speaking on the same subject, hoped the Government would follow the drift of public opinion, and let clause 9 of the bill slide. At each platform, a resolution in favour of Home Rule was carried.

Lord Randolph Churchill has sent to the" Times " a long letter, giving his impressions of Sunday's Home Rule demonstration in London. There was, he says, much that was instructive and nothing that was ths- agreeable in the procession, which he admits was very orderly and its general appearance most respectable re- latively to that kind of procession. His lordship says he received little personal, and that mostly deferential, notice from the processionists ; nothing Of any import- ance was addressed to him, except that in Pall-Mall a group greeted him " with loud cries and exclamations which, though ironical and expressive of strong political disagreement, were not in the least abusive or otherwise than perfeetly fair." One little incident is thus re- corded :—" Just at the corner of the Army and Navy Club one man hustled me and tried to trip me up ; but I turned and looked at him ; he was a very noor little creatc”e, and shrank into the crowd." Lord Randolph Churchill concludes with an appeal to the Uzaionist leaders and active persons of authority in the Unionist party in the metropolis not te neglect, in the important work they are performing among the constituencies, the more thickly populated portions of London, because from what lie saw he had great hopes that among the various classes the procession represented there are many who would be glad to have the Unionist cause put before them, and who might even become converts to the true faith of the union between Great Britain and Ireland.

THE ROYAL WEDDING.

The royal wedding, it is practically certain, will take place on a Wednesday, but it is not yet fixed whether that Wednesday shall be sth or 12th July. Who are to be the bridesmaids? is a question society anxiously asks; but the answer (so those within the innermost Court circle whispers) will be a great surprise to everybody. The Duchess of Teck —moved by compassion for our neighbours over the water—is entrusting some of the handsomest trousseau orders to a Dublin dressmaker. She has also purchased far Princess May a large quantity of Irish lace. Concerning the bridal arrangements it may be stated that the honeymoon will certainly be spent at Sandringham, and that in the event of the royal pair driving from Buckingham Palace to Liverpool-st. Station, facilities will be offered for many Londoners to see something of the wedding pageant. The Duke of York has shown great taste in hinv.elf suggesting that the form decided upon by his bride eighteen months ago for the City pre- sent should be unaltered in any particular, and the Wedding Committee of the Corporation will be able to carry out their commission with great ease. The service of silver plate first chosen was to hare been of a stock pattern, and therefore no serious loss was sustained by the abandonment of the order. It happenedi that her Majesty possessed some pieces which t was intended to add to those made for the civic fathers, and thns enable the royal pair to set up housekeeping with an exceptionally complete silver service. It is not unlikely that the previous negotiations in this direction will in the main be , revived.

led persons " who prefer "plunder, injns- sion and tyranny to any other form of at." A little later on this" set of hun- ill-disposed persons," became a "lawless vho were nothing better than political' and political Nihilists," and then they daily described as an "Irish gang" and led Nationalist crew." In LORD RAN- opinion any man in his senses would md money to the Turk than to his own lbjects who happen to live in Ireland. to the same orator, who, by the 'way tho ' "ems to have left all his old smartness, liinsug;ll none of his verbal violence, behind is 41 the African desert, Mr. GLADSTONE an " unconscionable old statesman," Zid. his policy is " based upon despair, upon cow- Thelee_, and upon the lowest party speculation." 11 rRUMIER. is afterwards transformed into a thgt_ and old genius," and as one of the authors of treacherous policy" he is consigned to tieverlasting political perdition." These are the lee flowers of rhetoric which are to be found in wgte abort Speech Of LORD RANDOLPH CHURCHILL, iS ranked high amongst the leaders of the niat party. That such language is not merely 4,13 ailded at excited meetings, but afterwards ZRoved in cold blood is very fair evidence of the ta oralisation that has set in amongst the "party '4 gentlemen."

LTHE Welsh Land Commission is The Welsh . lad Question, doing excellent work in giving the I. publicity to the grievances of nant farmers of Wales. Some of the less dicious members of the Conservative party hate sh .wn a disposition to accuse the Liberal ernment of neglect of the agricultural in- try. Agriculture,they say, is dealining and the t nvernment refuse to propound any legislative jtiledY• It is a hardy accusation considering that the Tory party themselves held office and POwer until a few months ago, and yet did no- thing to substantiate the claim they make of be- illg the "farmer's friend." The remedy which they desire is, of course, of the nature of protec- i"--some scheme or ether which will tax the rest of the community in order to keep up the rents of the landlords. But the evidence which is being submitted to the Welsh Land Commission i)ninta to the need of a far different kind of ,Nislation. One tenant tells of his crops being destroyed by swarms of rabbits and pheasants frorn the landlords warren, of fences and field pro- duce being broken and trampled down by the .andlord's shooting parties, and of the hard work- ing cultivator himself having to witness this vaPoliatien—f or such it really is—in silent indigna- tion On venturing to put in a claim fcr compensa- tion the paltry sum of seven pounds was allowed the tenant for damage amounting to over fifty _P"nds. Another claim for damage by rabbits ~,'eaulted, says a witness, iA a "notice to quit." "'hen we are told that, in Glamorganshire, "Non- Conformists are at a great disadvantage in aPPlying for and keeping holdings." In fact it is affirmed that, in some quarters, Nonconformists need not apply, not only for a farm, but for a cottage, and tenants dare not hold a Liberal meeting. After some recent experi- ences in Cheshire, people in this part of the country will find no difficulty in giving credence to most of these allegations. In Wales as in many other parts of the country,it is quite a common thing for rents to be raised on tenants' improvernents,and,altogether,the farmers there are in many ways harassed and discour- aged. Under the circumstances, it is not surpris- ing to learn that land is going out of cultivation, and that the tenants, on their side, are crying out for legislation of 'a protective but not of a pro- tectionist character.

Miners in THE International Conference of Conferen Miners which has been held this co. week in Brussels can hardly fail to promote what is known as the "solidarity of labour." Its proceedings certainly testify that, With the exception of the dissident minority in the Il.orth of England, the miners of Europe are prac- ticallyunanimous in the demand for the eight 13.iirs day. The resolution which "affirmed the 4_l)llosipal of a legal eight hours day from bank rbank, and advised all nationalities to use every fegitimate means to adopt the same" was voted ,cir 14Y the representatives of close upon nine hun- 'red thousand miners, while those who wore op- Posed to it represented one hundred thousand work- iteri who are returned as being employed in the rrth of England. The Conference went further :lan this, and indeed it went further than it has °ll at any previous meeting. It resolved that, if the various Parliaments refuse a legal eight hours day, an international strike of miners Tilhonld be declared to obtain what was demanded. 1118 was proposed by a delegate of the ,e°teli miners, and, like the previous resolution, it 'af et With opposition chiefly fiom the delegates krein tourham and Northumberland. Mr. T. BURT Was the principal speaker against the pro- Ivt'usal which he characterised as ridiculous. There 1,1;18, however, some force in the rejoinder of Mr. jonissox, of Warwickshire, that while Mr. T:ItT opposes legal measures on the ground that aila'des Union action is sufficient in itself to secure that is required, he was now condemning TT. it-a. The resolution was, needless to say, endorsed by a large majority. tjuils, at the same time, reassuring to note that no e has been specified for the commencement of Roe O'eneral strike, and we may be sure that before ti2a6raentous a step is taken there will be ample :" for consideration and discussion.

FOUND DEAD IN THE MERSEY. On Thursday afternoon, Mr. Coroner Woreley held an inquest at the Weaver Hotel, Weston Point, on the body of a Ran unknown. On Tuesday, Captain Yates, of the steamship Preston was surveying the deep in the river, when he saw the body floating and he towed it to Wes- ton Point. It was in an advaticed state of decomposi- tion and altogether unrecognizable. There was nothing in the pockets of the clothing to lead to identification. His possessions included a pipe, a penknife, and a six- pence.—A verdict of found dead was returned. THE ALLEGED KICKING OUTRAGE AT RUNCORN. On Saturnay morning at Runcorn Police Court, before Mr. James Handley, the charge of unlawfully wounding Frank Lyden, a navvy on the Manchester Ship Canal, by kicking him and breaking his jaw, in Penketh-lane, on the 29th ult., was again preferred against James Finne- gan, lodging-house keeper, Mersey-et. Superintendent Atherton asked for a further remand for a week, as the injured man was still unable to appear. This request was granted, and the accused was admitted to bail him- self in £4O and a surety of a like amount.

On Wednesday evening, a meeting of the combined Church Councils was held in St. Paul's Parochial Room, to consider what steps should be taken in appointing a successor to the Rev. G. M. At Widnes Petty Sessions on Thursday, Robert Green, aged 12 years, of 78, Davies-street, was charged with stealing 3s. and a purse, the property of William Atli- gan, of 5, Pear-street, on Tuesday. The parties went to the baths on the date named to have a bathe. They un- dressed themselves in the same room, and prosecutor was taking the money out of his pocket to put into a safe place, when prisoner suggested that he should put it into his shoes. This was done, and soon after enter- ing the water Green left, dressed himself, and took away the purse and money, which he subsequently handed to another youth.—The magistrates ordered that he should receive six strokes with the birch rod.

THEFT OP A BICYCLE AT WIDNES,

At the Widnes Petty Sessions, on Thursday, before Messrs. J. Brock and G. Pilkington, a young man named John Connolly, 20, Ellis-street, was charged on remand with stealing a bicycle, value £lO 10s, the property of James Plumbley, Halewood, on the 20th inst. Mr. J. W. Knowles defended. The prosecutor stated that he went into the Grosvenor Hotel, Widnes-road, about half past ten o'clock on Saturday night,leaving his bicycle outside. He was in the hotel about five minutes, and when he came out his machine was not there. The defence was that it was a drunken freak on the part of the defen- dant. The Bench ordered the defendant to pay the costs amounting to 30s. JOSEPH PRITCHARD, Watchmaker and Jeweller, 51, High- at., Ruacorn.—Advt. A RUNCORN MAGISTRATE AND THE RUNCORN PEOPLE. On Tuesday, at the Runcorn Petty Sessions, the only case for hearing was one of drunkenness whilst in charge of a horse and cart, in which the defendant as John Allmam of Halton. The case having been proved, a fine of ss. and costs was imposed.—Superintendent Atherton intimated that the inhabitants of the town, as well as the numerous visitors, behaved during the holi- days in a very orderly manner, as shown by the fact that they had only one case for his Worship to adjudicate upon. Further, there had been no accidents reported to the police. Mr. Speakman, the magistrate, considered that the townspeople were to be congratu- lated upon the immunity from crime during a season when temptation abounded. He trusted the same con- dition of things would long continue. • A NAVVY'S PRESENTIMENT AT RUNCORN. Mr. Worsley, the coroner, held an inquest at Runcorn Police Court, on Wednesday, touching the death of Robert Smith, aged 35 years, a navvy who had lodged at 23, Mason-st., with a man named Wm. Smith. A fortnight ago the man became ill and consulted Dr. McDougall who warned him to shun excitement. On Friday night the deceased told his landlord that he should not live over the holidays, and on Saturday night after spending a couple of hours on the Fair ground, and drinking four bottles of stout, blood issued from his mouth and nose and he fell down. He was carried into the Wheatsheaf Inn, but he died in a few minutes. Dr. McDougall said death was due to hcemor- rhage probably from the lungs. A verdict of natural causes was returned. When in want of a CHASTE and ELF.GINT WEDDING Or STRUIDAY PRESENT go to PRITCHARD'S, 51, High-st.—Advt. AN ARMY PENSIONER'S SAD END AT WIDNES. On Thursday, at the Black Horse Inn. Farnworth, near Viidnes, Mr. Brighouse held an inquest touching the death of an army pensioner named John Prior, who resided at 31. Bold-st., Gateshead, whose body was found on Tuesday last hanging from a rafter in a barn on Mr. Samuel Hunt's farm, Liverpool-road, Cronton. The deceased went to Mr. Hunt's farm on Monday night. He said he was destitute, and asked Mr. Hunt to allow him to sleep in the barn for the night. Mr. Hunt grant- ed him permission to spend the night in the barn, and Prior was last seen alive about half-past eleven on the Monday night. On the following day when Mr. hunt went into the barn for some straw he found deceased suspended by a rope from a beam, and quite dead, The jury returned a verdict of suicide whilst temporarily insane. RUNCORN SONS OF TEMPERANCE. At the usual meeting of the above society, on Satur- day evening, under the presidency of Mr. Thomas J. Holt, Mr. Daniel Bisbrown gave an address on " Whit- suntide." He referred to the Feast of Pentecost, and the descent of the Holy Spirit. The Christian Church throughout the ages had commemorated this glorious event, and would do for all time, He said this baptism was our Saviour's dying legacy to mankind. With it the disciples had wonderful success in their preaching. The speaker proceeded to say that Whit Sunday ori- ginally was called White Sunday, for in the time of the Emperor Constantine, the children walked in procession dressed in white garments (an emblem of purity.) Hence it was now called Whit-Sunday. After the andress there was an interesting discussion, in which the following took part :—Messrs. Edward Key, Samuel M. Temper- ley, George S. Sutton and David Smith.

BITTEN DT A HORSE AT RUNCORN. WM. Taylor, of 11, Jackson's-lane, a carter employed by the Runcorn lhiard of Commissioners, met with a serious accident on Saturday morning. He was leading an entire horse to the stable yard in Mersey-st., when the animal seized his left hand and held it until John Nicholls forced his hand into tile horses mouth and took possession of its tongue. The ferocious animals teeth had made a fearful gash in the man's hand, several ! arteries being severed,and bad it not been for the prompt assistance rendered by Inspector Cooper, P.C. Ridgway , and Mr. C. Woolley (vaninan for the L. and N.-W.Co.), lin stopping the blood, he would have speedily bled to death. Taylor was taken home and attended by Dr. : McDougall, who ordered him to bed for 12 days. The ;horse was known to be a savage one, and muzzle is provided for the animal but unfortunately it was not in use on the present occasion. THE LOCAL GOVERNMENT INQUIRY AT WIDNES. At the close of the inquiry a report of which is given en an inside page, the Town Clerk addressed the Inspec- tor, on behalf of the Council, and expressed their thanks for the patience and attention with which lie Wad heard the several applications. He also stated that he felt sure that the Inspector would understand the motives of the Council in asking him to deal with the application relating to the Technical Schools and Library that day. It was not with any desire to keep back any information from the public, hut only for the sake of saving the time, not only of the public themselves but of the Local Government Board. The Town Clerk also pointed out that there was nothing in the Public Health Act, 1875, requiring the Local Government Board, to hold any inquiry in this instance, inasmuch as the moneys borrowed by the Corporation would not exceed by a large margin the assessable value of the borough for one year. WHIT TUESDAY AT RUNCORN. After the festivities of Monday, there was an exodus from Runcorn on Tuesday. The holidays extended to Wednesday morning and people took advantage of the fine weather to seek pleasures afar. The annual excur- sion of the Runcorn Liberal Club to North Wales met with its usual measure of saccess. No fewer than 800 people booked by this train. Passengers were given the privilege of breaking their journey at a variety of places St. Paul's choir, who travelled with the excursion, alighted at Bangor and spent a pleasant day at the Straits. The Primitive Methodist body arranged a pic- nic to Llangollen, and 100 took advantage of the oppor- tunity. The bookings to ether places were very heavy. There were issued 1,300 tickets for Liverpool, 700 to Frodsham, 321 to Chester, and 100 to Manchester. The railway officials dealt with the traffic in a most satisfac- tory manner. The trip in connection with the Seamen's Institute, Runcorn, was to Dunham, London Bridge, and Lymm. The party jonrneyed on two flits from Top Locks, towed by a steamer, up the Bridgewater Canal. The outing under the auspices of the Salvation Army to Moore, also proved very successful. The officers and teachers of the Weston Wesleyan Sunday School char- tered one of the Manchester Ship Canal steamers, and from Weston Point proceeded along the newly-constructed undertaking to Eastham, and thence to Liverpool, New Brighton, and other neighbouring places of interest. The excursion was extensively patronised.

THE SALE OF BOLD HALL ESTA.TH. It is stated on good, but not official, authority that the Bold Hall estate, near St. Helens, which consists of 1,500 acres, and includes 13 farms in addition to a noble but decaying mansion, had been sold for £lOO,OOO, and that the purchase was com- pleted on Wednesday. On Wednesday a correspon- dent visited Bold Hall, it being the half-yearly I rent audit, and found Mr. Arthur Wyatt, son of Mrs. Wyatt, the owner, waiting to receive the tenants. That gentleman was reluctant to enter into conver- sation, and merely intimated that the contract had not yet been signed. The tenants received 10 per cent. reduction on what was understood to be the last rent audit under Mrs. Wyatt's ownership. The purchasers are said to be Colonel Gamble, CL, Ald. J. C. Gamble, J.P., and Mr. George Gamble, of St. Helens, Mr. Foster, colliery proprietor, of Waterloo (a partner in the firm ot Bromilow, Foster, and Co., of Ashton's (,Ireen Collieries, St. Ilelens), Messrs. Fairclough, millers, Warrington, and Mr. Oulton, of Liverpool. Mrs. Wyatt has stopped all improve- ments which were being made on the estate, and in due course it is expected great mining develop- ments will take place.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 27, 1893. WARRINGTON VOLUNTEERS UNDER CANVASS. THEIR ENCAMPMENT AT CONWAY.

• the clubs too made an exemplary display, order being well maintained. In fact the condition of the town was very creditable, and the magistrates next morning had only one case with which .to deal— that of an elderly man, who had imbibed so much generous wine sr other liquor that he was unable to cake care of the horse and trap with which he had been entrusted. On Tuesday there was a great exodus from the town. Nearly four thousand people departed by train and in the afternoon the streets were quieter than on an ordinary Sunday.

It was in Whines Police Court, on Thursday, for- tunately not at the command of Her Most Gracious Majesty, and after the magistrates • had disposed of a case of cruelty to a horse, Mr. Lewin called out in his customary commanding tone, "The Corporation of Widnes." I looked round in order to see the ' Mayor in all the glory of his robes and chain, the portly aldermen and common councilmen come for- ward in obedience to the imperious call. Alas ! and alas! the collector of the rates stepped forward with all the dignity at his command,and the goodly show I had conjured up in imagination remained an airy nothing. And is this the glory and dignity which Dr. O'Keeffe so often and so eloquently des- cribed would result from incorporation ? It is the 1 same as ever. Things imperial and things local are all summed up in the words " rates and taxes."

[BY OUR OWN CORRESPONDENT.]

Dame Fate seems to have a spite against our local corps of Volunteers. Last year she caused the weather to be of such an erratic character that one day it was almost necessary for the men to be aquatic experts to avoid the dangers of heavy rain, whilst the next day they were rendered unrecog- nizable by exposure to a biistering, broiling sun. For weeks past, whilst "going to camp," has been the one theme that has occupied the minds of the Volunteers, the weather was of an ideal character for camp life. But 10l there came the disappoint- ment. With the fall of heavy rain in Warrington on Saturday there came a fall in the expectations of the Volunteers. They remembered their tunics were not waterproof, and they thought how un- pleasant it would be, as one of them dolefully Put it, "if they had to anchor their beds down" at the camp in Conway. Perhaps these depressing cir- cumstances trilled with the martial valour of the men, perhaps it did not; but at any rate at the hour for assembly at the drill hall—five o'clock— when the elements took a temporary respite from their damping operations, the men turned up in stronger force than ever, and, be it said to their credit, in as smart, and trim a manner as could be expected from any model regiment. There were 485 men in all, including officers. A little before six o'clock Colonel Cireenall, in accordance with custom, addressed to the men a few remarks of advice and encouragement. Then the order came to "fall in; quick march "; the band struck up a lively air, and at a brisk pace the battalioa set out for Bank Quay station, via Legh-st., Sankey-at., and Parker-st. All along the route was a thick line of spectators who bad turned out to witness the departure, And many of them, as is their wont, indulged in some good natured "chaff." It was a common thing to near such remarks as these, made with mock solemnity—" Leave me a 13ck ot your hair, old chap." But the men addressed treated the cynical remarks with supreme contempt, and icy stares—the latter by the way not always being visible as sometimes the helmets had a tendency to rest on the nose.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT RUNCORN.

BY AN OCCASIONAL CORRESPONDENT.

"The Queen" announces that a "marriage will take place on June first, between Philip Ernest Speakman, second son of :Philip Speakman, Eq., J. P. C.C., of the Mount, Higher Runcorn, Cheshire, and Edith Laura, only daughter of the late Alfred C. Stearns, Esq., and Mrs. Stearns,ol Radnor House, Twickenham."

The Rev. J. Pulford Williams, pastor of St. John's Presbyterian Church, Runcorn, has just passed through a very serious illness. his numerous friends will be pleased to hear that although he is very weak, his condition is improving. Congestion of the lungs was the disease from which he suffered.

The honour of being the first winner of the Gossage scholarship at Liverpool University Col- lege, belongs to Charles IL Lewis, sou of captain Lewis, of Ditton Lodge, Widnes. The scholarship which is of an annual value of £65, and is tenable for three years, is the most valuable of those ten- able at University College. Candidates must be under 17 years and be connected with Widnes. The professors of the college conducted the examina- tion on May Bth, and five following days, and there were four candidates, three from Farriworth Gram- mar school and one from a Liverpool school. The senate of the University after considering the report of the examiners and the result of the ex- amination elected Master Lewis to the scholarship. His chief competitor was Walter Nickson, who, in the last college of preceptors examination took the seventh place in honours, from a list of 10,750 candidates. Both youths are from Fern- worth Grammar School.

Nevert!seless the , streets were well crowded and when the " schools , were walking" it appeared possible to walk on heads in High-st., looking from the police station steps. THINGS HEARD AND SEEN,

AT WARRINGTON.

The Football club had a very successful day on Monday, with their old English sports and Brass Band Contest. The most popular of the events were the sack race, donkey race, and obstacle race. The donkeys quite entered into the sport, at least two did. They exhibited their asinine qualities to a degree which left nothing to be desired. In- stead of keeping last, and, therefore, winning the prize, they showed an overwhelming anxiety to get rid of all incumbrances, i.e. their riders and get at the winning post by a short cut across the field, , and were very indignant., when they were called to order. The winner was an old stager who kept at a crawling pace the whole of the journey round the course, in spite of repeated pushings, and thrash- ings, and fairly won the race. In the sack race, Tommy Lally of football fame, was one of the competitors, and fairly outran his opponents in the heat, but only came in third at the final. The obstacle race must have been a hot affair for the participants in it, and the winners fairly earned their prize by hard work. I noticed that the more prominent members of the football team distin- guished themselves by completely overhauling their opponents in the tug-of-war, Fair Turner be- ing especially prominent at the end of the rope. The brass band contest, was a new feature in War- rington, and the " Warrington United" were well cheered when they came out of the ordeal with first honours. The proceedings were a little mar- red by some of the other competitors questioning the judge (M.r. Barker's) fairness. He, however, refused to budge, and the " United" received the well-merited honour.

Whit-Monday this year passel off very quietly in this town. We were favoured with fine weather, so that the usual grumble that it is always raining when it is holiday time could not be heard. Whit Monday and August Monday are the most popular holidays of the year, as they generally come about the time when the weather is warmest, and I think if Sir John Lubbock gets us any more holidays, he would do well to have them fixed during June, or July, and perhaps one to come during that part of the sear which is so blank in holidays. I mean from August Monday to Christmas. I cannot understand the action of some of the railways either, in their way of having chew excursions. You will remem- ber that at Easter, there were trips to places some some distance froth Warrington, notably into York- shire. They left Warrington on Good Friday and extended until the following Monday or TuJsday, but should the intending excursionist be unable to ' go on Good Friday morning. he could not get on the , Saturday as there were no cheap tickets issued. Now this was a hardship to some who could not get away on a Friday. But this Whitsuntide a still ' stranger course was adopted. As everybody knows, a large number of people only have a holiday from Saturday until Tuesday morning. They are there- fore anxious to make as much of their holiday as possible. But how are some of the trips arranged r Instead of commencing on Saturday morning, as they ought, they commence on the Sunday morning and thus all Saturday is thrown away. I think the railway companies ought to exercise a little more discrimination in catering for the enjoyment of the public generally.

The price of admission into the new bath, which is one of the finest in the north of England,will be sixpence. The old sixpenny bath will become a twopenny bath,and the old twopenny bath has been fitted up as a ladies' bath. Now, I know that there are some capital lady swimmers in Warrington,why should they not go in for a Polo Club? I'm sure of this, that whoever can popularise swimming among ladies in Warrington will deserve great credit. It is an exercise both sexes should be skilled in. I suggest that some of the young lady enthu- siasts endeavour to organise a club. I should cer- tainly be glad to give publicity in this column to any further suggestions which may be made on this subject.

--- I have heard a hint that the final tie in the next international polo match might come off in the new Warring Lon Baths. The dimensions of the bath, I believe, exactly conform to the 'requirements of properly played polo, and it is also of the required dimensions for swimming matches. A well-known Manchester authority upon swimming says the new bath is the finegt in the country, with the excep- tion of the one at Leicester. All this is very gratify- ing, and justifies one in looking forward to a not far distant time when Warrington will become a centre for swimming competitions.

In this quarter's "Lifeboat Saturday "an illus- trated publication devoted to the interests of the National Lifeboat Institution, there are capital illustrations of the Mayor and Mayoress of War- rington (Alderman E. and Mrs. Greenall). Allusion is made to the very active manner in which both the Mayor and Mayoress of Warrington worked in founding the branch of the Lifeboat Institution in Warrington. It states : " Prior to last year there was no local organization in Warrington in connec- tion with the Royal Lifeboat Institution. The Mayor, Mr. Alderman Greenall, who was present at the great demonstration in Manchester on October Ist, signified his willingness to organize a Lifeboat Saturday in this town.- This he accordingly did, but we understand that the Mayoress was really the moving spirit. She organized a large commit- tee of ladies, and although Warrington was suffer ing from a serious epidemic, which prevented a demonstration being held, by the great energy displayed by the Mayoress and the ladies who sup- ported her. A very substantial sum was collected, and a branch of this institution has now been established in the town, which promises to be a very successful one for the future."

the" Lifeboat Saturday," by the way, also pubJ lished portraits of the members of the first lifeboat Council and Executive Committee, and I notice that amongst them are Mr. W. C. Jones, of Darsbury, and Mr. Charles Lister, of Lymm.

A COOL THEFT.

For cool, calm audacity commend us to the thief who was covicted on Thursday of stealing railings from an open space at Bolton. Somehow he got possession of a Corporation cart, and at once proceeded to help himself to the park palisades, a policeman and a magistrate looking on with unsuspecting eyes. Having filled the cart, he took the iron to a second-hand dealer and rea- lised the reward of his effrontery. Now he is undergo- ing two months' incarceration, and it will be well to have him carefully watched, or he will run away with the prison gates, or do some other marvellous feat.

THE ODDFELLONVS AND POLITICS.

TIIE BELFAST DEMONSTRATIONS.

The A.M.C. at Southampton yesterday morning voted £lOO to the Salisbury district, to recoup it for the sum given to distressed members through the late disastreus lire at Handley, whereby fourteen families of members of the order and four members (single) were rendered homele:s. the whole of their furniture and effects being destroyed, as well as the lodge house, the dispensation and the lodge furniture. li4errinv to a demonstration against the Home Rule l-lill, in which Belfast district took part in full regalia and with the banner of the order, the directors strongly protested against, any variation t ruin strict neutrality in Political and religious matters. A Belfast delegate con- tended that the matter was a commercial one, RS the funds of the order were at stake. Eventually the policy of the directors was endorsed.

===== 0002601_18930527_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
MERLIN.

MALINCi A START.

About half-past six, amid immense confusion, or apparent confusion, the battalion entrained, and made a start upon a somewhat tedious journey, which was enlivened by the promiscuous singing of a miscellaneous reportoire of " latest songs." When near Rhyl, how- ever, the engine of the train broke down in consequence of the water pipes becoming disconnected. This caused a lengthy delay, and as a heavy downpour of rain burst forth about this time, the feelings of the travellers were by no means of a complaisent character. It was ten o'clock when Conway was reached, and the heavy showers of the previous hour had developed into a wild storm of wind and rain. During the evening battalions , from Rochdale, Bury, and various parts of Cheshire had arrived, and the Warrington men were the last to put in an appearance. The good people of Conway had retired to rest after the sounding of the curfew bell, and the Volunteers were the only persons to be seen in the streets. The rain was falling in drenching torrents, and for a full quarter of an hour the men were hustling hither and thither in it, and then falling into line, Raving consideration for the slumbering inhabitants. the band did not play. The officers probably ordered this as a special favour to the Conway people, and they apparently wished to acknowledge the consideration, when they appeared at their bedroom windows. Gener- ally, however, they were quickly frightened under the sheets again, when the strains oh "The man that broke the bank at Monte Carlo," and other songs aisailed their unsophisticated ears.

===== 0002601_18930527_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
ARMIVAL AT TUE CAMP.

The march to theamp, upon the Marsh, was achieved in about twenty minutes, through the drenching rain, and along the puddled road in the dark. But what fears may have been entertained of the bad effect of the rain upon the tents were soon allayed for the fatigue party including 30 men and six sergeants, commanded by Captain Baynes, which had been there since Wednesday, had made everything snug and comfortable. There were many arrangements to be made, and for an hour or two the whole. camp was moo bustle; supper had to be partaken of, bedding had to be arranged, as a stronger force than was expected had arrived. Men were hurry- ing hither and thither among the lighted tents, and the hoarse cries of the officers delivering their commands all tended to create a scene, weird and confusing. But in time all noises subsided, Ind at three o'clock the camp was in quietness.

===== 0002601_18930527_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
TUE NEXT MORNING.

At five o'clock on Sunday morning,the reveille sounded gaily. The sun was shining brightly and the men leaped in one bound, as it were from slumber into their " pants," and in the twinkling of an eye the novelty of the situation was realised, whilst the memory of the previous night's hardships vanished before the brighter and cheering influences of the morn. Nature seemed refreshed after the downpour, and the air was laden with the scent of the blossoms of trees, and the oz ne from the ocean. The marsh, which is the largest cam- ping ground in ngland, is a stretch of land lying right at the foot of a line of hills, and to the south of the mouth of the Conway river, with the Menai Straits at the south. The scenery, of which a view is commanded from here is as magnificent and enchanting as any land- scape on the shores of Great Britain. Looking north from the marsh there can be seen at one glance a portion of the ancient Castle of Conway, the mouth of the Conway river, with Llandudno on its further bank, and then ex- tending from it to the west, there comes the Great Orme's Head, its high bulky body hiding the further northern coast from view. Turning and glancing south there is a large stretch of the Irish sea, then the well- known Puffin Island, and the north portion of Anglesea and the Menai Straits. l'he marsh is close upon two miles long, and is triangular shaped,and the reader may imagine the picturesque sight it presented to those who had entered camp that night, and rose to see the place altered with tents to accommodate the whole of the Lancashire and Cheshire Volunteer Infantry Brigade of 4,600 men. The vastness of the camp is said to be un- precedented. There were 460 . men from Bury, Ist V.B.L.F. ; 391 from Rochdale, 2nd V.B.L.F. ; 564 from Salford,3rd V.8.L.F.; 540 from Warrington,lst 565 from St. Helens, 2nd V.B.S.L. ; and from the follow- ing Cheshire Battalions : Knutsford, 706 ; Congleton, 600.

After breakfast th 6 whole of the brigade met at divine worship on the northern end of the marsh, the commanding officers then being Brigade General Major Thackwell, C. 8., Major General Julian Hall. Colonel Greenall and Adjutant Captain Silverthorne commanded the local battalion, and there were also the following officers present :—Captain Buckton, A.D.C. (A. Co.), Captain Appleton (C. Co.), Captain •Edleston (D. Co.), Captain Baynes (E. Co.), Captain Chorley (F. Co,), Cap- tain Widdows and Lieut. Fairclough (G. Co.), Quarter- master Captain Hutchinson, and Sergeant Captain J. Adams, Sergeant Major Sullivan, Sergeants Instructor Wooding and Barker, Quartermaster Sergeant Myeock, Orderly room clerk Whitfield, Pioneer-Sergeant Warbur- ton, Quartermaster-Sergeant Heaton, and Col.-Sergeant Daniels, lion. members ; Col.-Sergeants Jolley, Ser- geants Ward, Taylor, and T. Russell (A. Co.) ; Col.-Ser- geant Spencer, and Sergeants Ash worth, and Rooke (B. Co.) ; Col.-Sergeant Potter. and Sergeants V. Pot- ter, Dennett and Thomas (C. Co.) ; Col.-Sergeant Wood, and Sergeants Whitfield, Waiting, J. Scarisbrick, C. Russell, P. Gardener (D. Co.) ; Col.-Sergeant Stirrup,and Sergeants Wilcocks and I. Connor (E. Co.) ; Col.-Ser- geant 13ennion and Sergeants Moore,tlesketh, Bottle and Hollingworth (F. Co). (Newton) : Col.-Sergeant McCoy, and Sergeants McCoy, James Tunstall, John Tunstall, and W. Goodwin (G. Co.), Lee, Buckley and Davies. The band was led by Bugler Major Armstrong and Sergeant Bugler Gregory. The Warrington Battalion—whose camp was consider- ably removed from the remainder of the brigade— marched to ser”ice to the quick time of ‘• Let their celestial concerts." The band of the Salford Fusiliers, rendered musical accompaniment to the service under the leadership of Lieut. Archer, who is the only com- missioned officer, with the exception of Dan Godfrey, who is a military bandmaster. The spectacle of the 4,000 men, dressed and accoutred as these were was impressive in a high degree, although it cannot be said that they threw much enthusiasm into the service. The preacher' the Rev. G. E. Gill, vicar of Pendleton, preached an excellent sermon from 2nd Timothy,ii3 : "A good soldier of Jesus Christ." A collection was after- wards made, in aid of the Volunteer Forces 133ne- volent Fund.

TAICING THEIR PLEASURES.

After dinner, when the guards had been appointed, the men were allowed to leave camp and seek their pleasures where they chose. This was the case through- out the whole brigade camp, and hence Conway, Llandudno, and the surrounding neighbourhood were filled with the " Amateur soldiers," some in grey uniforms, others in red uniforms whilst some were attired in he more sombre dark cloth. The weather cleared up beautifully during the day, and there was no doubt the men extracted from their well- deserved holiday a creat amount of pleasure.

===== 0002601_18930527_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
DAILY DOINGS IN TIIECAMP.

With Monday morning the real work may be said to have commenced, for it was then that the system of guards, relief detachments, potato pealers, &c., was put into vogue. Then men lived s:x or 10 in a tent, and each day one of the number acted as orderley for the others. This for instance is the outline of the day's routinA work :—Dress at 6.0 a.m.,adjutant's parade 6.30 a.m., quarter breakfast call 7.45 a.m. breakfast 8 a.m., sick call 8.30 a.m., quarter for guard mounting 9 a.m., guard mounting 6.30 a.m., dress 9.45 a.m., staff parade 10.10 a.m., fall-in commanding officer's parade 10 30 a.m., orderly room 12.30 p.m., first dinner call 12.45 p.m., dinner 1.0 p.m., dress 2.30 p.m., fall-in adjutant's parade 3-0 p.m., first tea call 4.45 p.m., tea 5.0 p.m.,

retreat 7.30 p.m., officers' dress for mess 7.30 p.m., officers dinner 8 0 p.m., tattoo, Ist post, 9.30 p.m., last post 10.0 p.m., orderly sergeants and corporals 10.15 p.m., lights out 10.30 p.m. Before breakfast in the morning the whole of the battalion went through a number of column manoeuvres under the command of Captain A. Silverthorne. At mid-day the brigade wasl occupied in battalion drill, when a variety of movements were gone through. Our local battalion has a greater number of recruits this year than any other battalion,and considering that it was generally admitted that their drill was the smartest, the "raw recruits " should Imo longer think themselves raw. But there were other phases of camp life which are not always worthy of being officially gazetted. After the call for lights out there were some amusing scenes taking place in some of the camps. This is an instance. A belated camper came doggedly along to a certain camp knowing that there was the guardroom for his late hours. He didn't like the guardroom ; so he tried to slip by the guard quietly. This is the dialoguethat took place : " Who goes there." " ole man. Itsh me ! Tha knows —hic—me, doesn't tha ?" " Speak ! Who goes there, or I'll call the guard." " Shis—hic —up, tha great foo. Tha knows I'm—hic—nor a Rusheon—hic or out like tha." The guard lost his military equanimity and a race began in the dark. The belated individual was eventually captured and preparations were made for Putting him in the guardroom. The culprit then got upon his civil dignity and protested against being" run us " as there was no war on for they were only playing soldiers. But he had to go.

===== 0002601_18930527_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
CHANGES IN THE WEATHER.

Monday night became squally and a strong wind ac- companied by heavy showers of rain greeted the campers when they responded to the 5.30 a.m. reveille. In conse- quence of this a battalion drill had to be put off, but by breakfast time the wind had abated ; the rain ceased ; thc heavy clouds which had brooded over the western horizon, disappeared, and the sun shone forth again and went on with its task of making the tender faces of some of the corps require considerable buttermilk to keep in check the proclivity for growing which the cheeks had. After breakfast the men had a few hours' heavy work. There was a brigade drill. It was formed under the command of Major General Thackwell, and commenced by lining out in quarter columns. Then the whole of the battalions formed in a double file of three quarters of a mile long, and did the feu de joie, in what was considered a smart manner. There was afterwards a march past, and then afterwards something of a sham fight was gone through. The Warrington battalion were called upon to defend their camp against the re- mainder of the battalions who were the invading forces. Colonel Greenall issued his scouts in a line, who tock up their position for the defence of the camp near to the Bridge. The column then opened fire upon the invaders, and for some time a serious exchange of battery was carried on. It was too heavy for the defenders, and Colonel Greeaall retired his regiment to the bridge on which they took their position. It was intended that a part of the regiment should have also lined the entrance to the camp, but owing to a misunderstanding this was not done, and Warrington were is great danger of hav- ing their camps captured when, as it was getting near one o'clock the brigadier called the forces off and dis- missed them. The affair was chiefly a rehearsal of what was to take place on Thursday, and so no judges had been appointed to give a verdict—of course it was a movement which would not have been arranged had there been judges and verdict in the question. Warring- ton would have had an impossible task. During the afternoon the men went through a drill exercise, and in the evening, as the weather was gloriously fine, the " red jackets " were seen dotted about the picturesque town of Conway and the surrounding country.

===== 0002601_18930527_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
TRH QUERN'S BIRTHDAY.

The event of the week was certainly the review of the brigade and the celebration of the Queen's Birthday on I Wednesday. General Hall arrived to command at the I review. When the men turned out at ten o'clock the weather was very threatening, but about eleven o'clock the stiff wind blew the clouds over, and left a clear sky and a warm sun. The Warrington battalion turned out in the following strength ;-12 officers, 30 sergeants, 38 band and buglers, 560 rank and file ; total 840. There were absent on leave three officers, 18 rank and file ; without leave there were absent 20 rank and file. The i total enrolled strength is 15 officers, 30 sergeants,3B band and huglers,s9B rank and file; total 68f. There had arriv- ed the previous night 25 men, to take part in the review. There were at least a thousand spectators present, and among them were many Warringtonians. In addition to several brigade movements, there came the march past, a splendid sight to observe. Where the Warring- ton, Newton, and St. Helens volunteers passed, a crowd of spectators behind the General, gave voice to their appreciation of the three corps, When General Hall had been saluted by the entire line, the bands of all the bat- talions amassed themselves in the centre of the field,and the men filed up before them, and at a signal from the General, two volleys of the feuds joie were discharged. The firing began by the mountain and in a few seconds had passed down the ranks right away to the last bat- talion in the line. In the second volley the firing was, through a rifle going off by accident, opened in the mid- dle of the line. However this little defect detracted nothing from the impressiveness of the demonstration, and indeed, when in a few moments later, from a signal given by General Hall, the whole brigade delivered three lusty cheers there was an effect which must have warmed the hearts of all her Majesty's sub- jects who were present. Shortly afterwards General Hall addressed the men. This, he said, was the third occasion on which he had had the pleasure of seeing them at Conway. As the years passed his satisfaJtion with what he saw increased. 'lbis year there were present two battalions which had never been there be- fore, and the total number of men on the ground was larger than at any previous camp. It was fortunate that a day could be selected for his inspection of the brigade when they could celebrate the Queen's birthday. It gave them an opportunity of showing the country their feelings towards her Majesty. They had made a fine muster and fired a splendid feu de joie. The man- ner in which they had carried out the parade and done the firing was extremely satisfactory. It was a credit to General Thackwell, who commanded, and to those concerned in instructing them. The turnout had been very good, and he could see that great pains had been taken in all ranks to produce as smart an appearance as possible. In conclusion, he would say that as long as he was in command he would yearly inspect that brigade, certain that as every new occasion same further progress would he manifested and the strength of the battalions increased.

===== 0002601_18930527_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
TILE INSPECTION.

At the conclusion of the General's address, Colonel Williams, of the 20th and 40th South Lancashire Regi- ment, who had witnessed the march past, took up the inspection of the Warrington corps. Captain Baynes and Captain Edlesten were called upon to put the men through several evolutions. which was done in a manner highly satisfactory to Colonel Williams. The St. Helens battalion was inspected at the same time, the inspection being the lightest for years. The men, on being dis- missed were allowed an afternoon's holiday in celebra- tion of the event of the thy. ANOTHER nid EVENT.

STRAY NOTES.

The brigade got a capital Postmaster General in the person of Sergeant Russell, of the Warrington brigades and also of the Warrington G.P.O. The brigade Provo was Sergeant Taylor. Captain Hutchinson superintended the preparation, for camp. During the week Quartermaster Mycoek wag in charge of the whole of the storage and was assisted by Orderly Marsden and Sergeant Hollingsworth. The sergeants' mess of the Warrington Battalion wail generally voted "the jolliest one "in the camp ; and it was true. Colon r-Sergean t Ward was the president,Colour. Sergeant Bennion was the secretary, and Colour-Sergt. Potter the treasurer. P's D. Watkinson and W. Judson. of G Company, were orderlies. Sofgeant Whitfield had a busy week's occupation as regimental clerk. General Thackwell was struck by the youthful ap- pearance of the Warrington litattalion,and the smart way in which they went through their manoeuvres ; so he asked for the total number of privates in the corps, who were under 20 years of age. It turned out there were 296 of them. The total strength of the corps is 682,and there have been 250 recruits this year. On Monday evening the Mayor and Mayoress (Alder- man and Mrs. Greenall) drove down to the camp. They witnessed the brigade drill on Tuesday morning, and afterwards inspected the Wa,ringtoa camps. They afterwards lunched with Colonel Greenall,Capt.-Adjutant Sfiverthorne, and other officers. . .

In the brigade there were three cycling corps, sae attached to the 3rd Cheshire Battalion and another to the 4th Cheshire. St. Helens also had one, and next year it is believed that Warrington will turn out a good cycling detachment. During the past yearan attempt was made to organize one, but it was not very success- ful, there being only four or five members. An amusing episode occurred on Monday. A certain senior non-commissioned officer had an undress tunic— one of the old serge green collar ones, which went out of vogue a considerable time ago. This one was the sole sur- viver, and as it was about 13 years old the other non- commissioned officer warned their messmate not to wear the tunic,but he did so. In consequence of this they deter- mined to act stringently, and they captured the article and adjourned; a hole was made, a mock funeral service was held, and the tunic was duly consigned to the grave. But in the night an enthusiast of cremation resurrected the article and was about to cremate it in an oven when the owner rescued it. He appeared at the next mess with it on.

Sergeant T. M. Moores acted as superintendent of the temporary hospital, under Dr. Adams. There were eight patients in by Tuesday,suffering from sunburn and sore throats. Sergeant Buckby on Wednesday took up duty at the hospital. The catering for the Battalion was done by M. B. Hewitt of the Grill, Chester, and as everything was always ready for the men when they were ready for it, and as everything was also good, it was considered that the catering was exceedingly satisfactory. The officers of the Warrington battalion were hon- oured on Wednesday night, by the fact that General Thackwell and his staff dined with them. Among the people from Warrington who were at camp on the Queen's birthday, were the Chaplain, Rev. F. W. Willis (Rector of Warrington), the Rev. T. Cooke, Rev. A. Buckton, and Captain Withington of Culcheth Hall; and Colonel Bolton was else present 'for a short time. After the feu de joie was Bred on Wednesday, about 30 Welsh scrap collectors rushed on to the ground,and almost before their presence was noticed they had followed up the line and collected the whole of the discharged cart- ridges which were lying about. They would sell the copper heads. The Fairy Glen, one of the most beautiful spots ia Britain, was a favourite resort of the men. The entrance fee was threepence. This is the property of Mr. W. Smith, corn merchant of Chester. Last year £2OO was realized in the camp week from visitors. There is a small village at the foot of the Glen, and there is only one Conservative amongst the whole of the inhabitants. So the oldest inhabitant himself said.

THROWING STONES AT TRAINS.

MR. GLADSTONE'S NARROW ESCAPE.

George Munday, fourteen, and Herbert Michael, eleven were charged at Harlesden on Thursday with wantonly throwing stones at passing trains. Mr. Lambert stated on behalf of the London and North-Western Company, that the boys were detected throwing stones at trains on Bank Holiday. The practice had become common, and the breaking of a window some time since occasioned a rumour that the window of a compartment in which Mr. Gladstone was travelling had been struck, but the stone really struck the next carriage. Munday said in reply to the charge that they were throwing stones at the engine.—Mr. Bird, the magistrate, said it was a very dangerous practice. They had both been in prison four days, and Munday would be fined 20s. and costs, or go to prison for fourteen days further, and Michael would have six strokes of the birch rod.

===== 0002601_18930527_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
ONE OF OUR RICHEST MEN.

One of the richest men in the kingdom is Lord Want- age, the Victoria Cross hero, and a most successful volunteer officer. A Lindsay of the Crawford stock, Lord Wantage acquired his millions through wedding the Hon. Harriet Sarah Lyod, only child of the late Lord Overstone, sometime M.P. for flythe, and head of the now extinct banking firm, Jones, Lvod, and Co., of Manchester and London. When Miss Sarah Jones, daughter of a Manchester banker, worshipped last cen- tury in the chapel of a Welsh Dissenting minister named Lewis Loyd, she doubtless didn't intend to fall in love with her pastor. But she did, and married him, her father subsequently taking the young man into partner- ship. Her son, Samuel Jones Loyd, was Lord Overstona The Loyd shekels thus date back to the pre-Factory Act days, when " hundreds per cent. mscle the fortunes of Lancashire."

===== 0002601_18930527_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
A LOVE TRAGEDY.

In the community of Parabita, in Southern Italy, a shocking crime was perpetrated the other day. A cer- tain Angela Pespico, a married woman, fell in love with a man named Caggiula, who was betrothed to a highly respectable young woman. Pespico vowed the marriage should never be solemnised. On a recent feast day, Cagg,iula, his betrotited, and a relative of Pespico, with his child, spent the day in the best of spirits, and in the evening, made for the dwelling-hoiAe of Angela Pespico. After the usual greetings Angela produced a flask of wine and all ate and drank save Angela, who only sipped. A short time afterwards the visitors were seized with terrible pains, and Angela herself was ill, though her attacks were of a lighter kind. In the morning of the next day the neighbours found four persons on the floor, of whom three—Caggiula, his betrothed, and the child —were dead, while Fa nizzi, Pespico's relative, still breathed. Angela and her husband are in the hands of the police.

PENKETH.

F/RX AT SANKEY STATION.—On Wednesday night two wagmns of hay that were standing on a siding at Sankey station caught fire. The hay was almost all burnt, very little being saved. It is supposed that a spark from a passing engine caused the fire. No other damage was done. •

BANK HOLIDAY.—The works at Penketh were all closed on Monday and a general holiday was enjoyed by the workmen. Several parties left the village to visit other parts. The weather was very fine and a pleasant day was spent by the various travellers. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

ASSAULTING A WHOLE FAMILY.

Charlotte Cee-;;;Ch-arged at the Warrington Police Court on Monday, with being drunk and disorderly in Parker-street on Monday night, and also with assaulting Phcebe Dutton, Elizabeth Dutton, and John Dutton. P.C. Turner, said that from information received, he went with P.C. Meacocleto 30, Parker-street, where the Duttons live. He found prisoner in the kitchen, kicking very violently. John Dutton was trying to prevent her frcm assaulting his mother and sister. They got her outside and tried to persuade her to go home quietly, but she refused, and the officers were obliged to lock her dp. Phcebe Dutton said that prisoner knocked at the door on Monday afternoon, and her daughter Elizabeth opened the door. Prisoner throw the door wide open and came inside. She knocked the little girl on to the stairs, and came into the kitchen, and gave witness blows on the eye arid mouth. Elizabeth screamed for her brother John who was upstairs and he came down- stairs and pulled prisoner away, and kept her on the ground, until the officers arrived. Elizabeth and John Dutton corroborated the latter adding that prisoner bit him on the arm when he took hold of her. Prisoner stated that the bother was occasioned through her son going to Dutton's house, to which she objected, and wished him to keep away. The Chief Constable said that prisoner had been a very good character previous to new, and taking this into consideration, prisoner was bound over in two sureties of £5 to keep the peace for six months, and ordered to pay the costs, or in default seven days' imprisonment. THEFT FROM A MOTHER.

At the Warrington Police Court on Tuesday, George Harrop was charged with stealing from 3, Egerton- street, on Monday, Ll 15s. in cash, the property of Ellen Dutton. The CIAO Constable said that complainant was mother of defendant who lodged with her. On Monday be was in the house, not very sober, and she went out, leaving a purse containing Ll 153. locked up in a chest of drawers. When she came back, her son had gone out and the chest of drawers had been forced open, and the money was gone. She afterwards met him in Butter- market-street and asked him for the money, but he denied having it. He, however, went to the police station with her, and he was there searched and El 63. was found upon him. He then admitted stealing the money. Prisener was sentenced to two months' hard labour,

_ A SIISPICIOI79 SLEEPING PLACE. At the Borough Court on Monday, Richard Lowe, William Taylor, and T. Warburton, were charged with being on the enclosed premises of Messrs. Evans', Foun- dry-street, with intent to commit a felony. Inspector Cox said that at 11 o'clock on Sunday night he received information of three men seen on the top of the roof of the Three Pigeons public-house. On going there he ex- amined the back-room window' and found that the latch had been tampered with. He sent a constable on the roof but the men had left, and dropped through an old skylight of a stable which adjoins the public house, and is used by Messrs. Evans for storing coal. They were all three found lying down there covered with some goal sacks. The prisoners pleaded not guilty to the charge, but guilty to sleeping out. Inspector Cox said he found 7d. in the pockets of Lowe, which would have paid for his lodgings. Mr. Monks remarked that it was not a very suitable place to sleep on cobs of coal. Prisoners who bore bad characters were sent to gaol for one month.

ALLEGED SETTING FIRE TO A WIFE

AT FRODSHAM.

On Wednesday morning, at Frodsham Police Court, Messrs. J. Handley and T. Rigby were en- gaged hearing a charge against George Jackson, 28, of Volunteer-st.,Newtown,of throwing a lighted paraffin lamp at his wife,with intent to do her gre- vious bodily harm, on Saturday night. Superinten- dent Meredith prosecuted, and Mr. A. Linaker ap- peared for the accused.—Emily Darlington, wife of Henry Darlington, Newtown, Frodsham, said the prisoner and his wife lodged with her. Ile caine home on Saturday night, between ten and eleven o'clock, and got hold of one of the chairs, and threatened to break everything in the house be- longing to him. His wife came in about half-an- hour later. A quarrel ensued between them, he cased her, and she rushed into the kitchen, he fol- lowing her. Prisoner then returned to his own apartments. His wife ran to the front door scream- ing "Murder." La.er he seized a loaf and threw it at his wife, or daughter, and the former then went to the front door again. He pic;:ed up a lighted paraffin lamp from the mantel sbei:, followed her, and threw it at her. Her clothing was immediately in flames. The wall paper and skirting boards of the house were also ignited. Witness's husband went to her assistance, and tore off a portion of her clothing, Her other garment e were etill on fire,but some neighbours by means of a macintosh extin- guished the flames. Prisoner did not assist to put the fire out. He was very quarrelsome. P.C. Price was soon upon the scene, and afterwards he and P.C. Doyle took the man into custody. She heard the sergeant ask prisoner's daughter whether her father threw tbe lamp at her mother, and the little girl replied "Yes."—By Mr. Linaker: Both husband and wife were under the influence of drink, but they were not so drunk as not to know what they were doing. She could not say who commenced the quarrel. fhey often quarrelled. Other evidence having been given, , Mr. Linaker urged the Bench to dismiss the serious charge brought against his client. Whilst admitting that if he threw the lamp at his wife he deserved severe punishment, he pointed out that there was nothing to justify them in convicting or committing him for trial. There were cross•statements on all sides, and they ought to exercise a wide and wise discretion.—The Bench, after a few minutes' consultation, said they had decided to commit the prisoner to take his trial at the next Chester Assizes.—Mr. Linaker asked that his client might be admitted to bail, and Superintendent Meredith not objecting if it were substantial, the request was granted, Jackson in his own recoguizances of £4O and two sureties of £2O each.

THE ALLEGED THEFT OF TIMBER

AT WIDNES.

At Widnes Petty Sessions, on Thursday, before Messrs. J. Brock and George Pilkington, Henry Dagnall, Bryan-street. was charged on remand with stealing various pieces of wood work, valued at 10s., the property of Mr. T. Sadler, builder and con- tractor, Widnes. He was also charged with stealing lag several tools, the property of William Williams, foreman joiner for Mr. Sadler. The name of James Edwardson, Warrington-road, appeared on the charge sheet for stealing a quantity of timber, and also receiv- ing timber knowing it to be stolen. Mr. T. Swift (St. Helens) prosecuted, and Mr. Knowles defended.—At the outset Mr. Swift said that with regard to the charge against Edwardson, he proposed to offer no evidence. It was very doubtful whether they could obtain a con- viction for larceny, and he therefore withdrew the case and any imputations. He went on to say that Dagnall for a number of years had been employed loy Mr. Sadler as a labourer. Articles had been missed during the past twelve months, and from what came to Mr. Sadler's knowledge, on the nth inst. he gave information to the police, and the premises of Dagnall were searched, with the result that the articles produced, which belonged to Mr. Sadler, were found. At the same time, the tools belonging to the workman Williams were also come across.—The prisoner was senteneed to a month's imprisonment.
