ANOTHER USE USE FOR MONEY.

Many are the uses of money ; but Mr. Joseph Newton late of the Royal Mint, points out there is at least one use which is not generally known. This is the availabil- ity of coins for letter weighing when the ordinary. pos- tal weights do not happen to be at hand. Commencing. with the sovereign, this weighs a very safe quarter- mince for foreign postage, two equalling of course a half- ounce, and four one ounce. The next denomination, the crown piece, is just on the right side of one ounce in weight, and the half-crown of half-an-ounce. Five separate shillings, or two shillings and sixpence, for the ounce, and half-ounce, will do as well. Descending from gold and silver to hard-working bronze, it is found that either three penny pieces, or five halfpence, or ten farthings, will respectively make up one ounce. Another peculiarity in regard to the halfpenny is noted by Mr. Newton. That is its diameter, which is pre- cisely 'one inch, so that by using it as a measure it will be seen that twelve halfpence laid flat, and in touch, are equal to one foot, and thirty-six to one yard.

Professor Virchow has assured the Berlin Medical Society that a cholera epidemic is not likely to break out anywhere in Germany this year. The Hamburg police threaten proceedings against local papers which asserted that some recent cases of illness were cases of Asiatic cholera.

Potato blight has already broken out at Skibbereen. county Cork. Two men were killed on Thursday morning by an explosion at a colliery at Dunston, near Newcastle. The cause of the explosion is not yet known.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 27, 1893.

WARRINGTON V. WERNETIL—This match was played at Arpley on Saturday, in unsettled weather. The sharp showers of the forenoon, followed by sunshine, rendered the wicket in favour of the trundlers. R. Smith won the toss, and Messrs. Crosfield opened the batting to the deliveries of Hall ar.d Hodgkinson, both medium pace bowlers. Hall was soon busy, and after three singles I had been chronicled the Werneth professional got S. M. Crosfield caught at third man. The score had been exactly doubled when J. J. Clarke's stay was likewise brief, and five good wickets were down for 12 runs. F. Sugg (who acted in lieu of his brother, who was assist- ing Derbyshirel joined Smith, and the former doing the bulk of the scoring, the first boundary hit was received with cheers after 35 minetes play. Hall reached Sugg's wicket with a good ball at 23, and although Podmore stayed whilst 46 runs were added his contribution was only a couple. Meanwhile Smith hit steadily, and with Clark in brought Bedford on for Hodgkinson. Smith treated the new bowler with contempt, and boundary hits now became frequent. Clark too, was in a hitting mood, and profiting by a let off at the hands of Hall, the total was taken to 65 when Hall had his revenge by clean bowling the professional. Bell came, Smith still doing the bulk of the scoring. At 89 the players were I compelled to retire, but on resuming Bell was bowled first ball, and when the total had reached 114 Smith ' failed to hit One frem Hall. His brilliant innings includ- ed nine fours. The venture closed for 114. On Werneth going in to bat, Eleyworth was beaten in J. J. Crosfield's first over, whilst at twelve Bedford fell a victim to a good catch by S. M. Crosfield. Hall and Haggis put a different complexion on the game, the total being taken to 25 before Clark sent flaggis to the pavalion. WARRINGTON 2ND V WERNETH 2No.—This match was played at Werneth on Saturday, the wicket being in a very sodden condition owing to the heavy rain which fell during the week. Warrington, winning the toss, opened with Jolley and Bibby, to the trundling of Griffiths and Wrigley. In the first over Jolley was given out, and his place filled by Williamson, who had also to retire in three or four minutes. Jeans made a fair start, but the separation came from the other end, a splendid delivery from Griffiths disposing of Bibby, and I the third wicket fell for nine. Disaster did not stay here, for Jeans and Isherwood were soon afterwards dismissed, the fifth wicket falling for 16. When Brown and Dunn got together a better aspect was put on the game, and the score soon reached 43, the former hitting the ball all over the field. At this total he fell to catch by Mellor. A change of bowling was now tried, with the result that Prestwich took three wickets in succes- sion, doing the "hat trick," and the innings closed for 56 runs. Werneth commenced with Turner and Seaton, and disaster attended the 'Werneth start, for three wickets were down when eleven runs had been regis- tered. Isherwood now commenced bowling, and caught and bowled Wrigley with the first ball, also taking another wicket in the same over. Bowers and Prestwich hit well, and the game was rather exciting now, as the seventh wicket fell at 47, while the Warrington total stood at 45 for the same number of wickets, but Sugden and Bowers managed to pass the visitors' total, the home team winning by six runs. RUNCORN 'V. Warros.—These locals met at Runcorn on Saturday. ,The match aroused considerable interest, with the result that the two clubs shared a very good gate. The play was well worth witnessing. Wright and Burton went to the wickets for Runcorn, but the latter had his wicket taken by a full pitched one from Tudor, jun., before he had got settled Woods partnered Wright, and the two treated the spectators to some capital cricket, both compiling their respective scores in good style. Crompton hit freely for his eighteen. It appeared that the side would be out for considerably under seventy, but C. E. Linaker last man in did useful service by adding a well played 3:4-before be succumbed to Bleakley, and J. M. Frith carried his bat out for 5. The bowling of Tudor, jun.., was very effective: In consequence of a heavy shower, there was some delay i before the players again got n the field. Weston com- menced.their innings in substantial fashion. On the second ball from Woods, Bleakley skied out to Gatley, and then Tudor, jun., gave a difficult chance to Wright who brought off a snlendid catch. Two reverends were now together, one hiding his name under the nem de plume of Weston, and runs came rapidly. It seemed as though Runcorn were to suffer defeat at their hands alone, when a chaoge was made in the bowling, Burton and Crompton taking the place of Woods and Gatley. The former captured four wickets in four consecutive balls, a feat which elicited loud cheers. His analysis read four wickets, eight runs. When stumps were drawn at 6-30 the game remained drawn :

ST. HELENS.

THE PROPOSED MEMORIAL TO THE MAYOR.

About £2BO have already been subscribed t.c-i—the pro- posed memorial to the Mayor of St. Helens (Mr. A. Sin- clair) in recognition of his fifty years services to th borough. Several of the most important subscription have yet to be received. ST. HELENS AND DISTRICT GOLF CLT/B. _ . .

- - - The final in the handicap bra set of golf clubs given by the president, Mr. Henry Seton-Earr, M.P., was played MT on Grange Park Links, St.Helens, on Wednes- day night. There have been twenty-seven competitions, and play has occupied portions of six days. Dr. Andrew Gray and Mr. Harry Gamble were the scratch men. In the final Dr. Gray beat Dr. John Cotton and secured the prize. ANOTHER CASE OF SMALLPDX.

At the meeting of the St. Helens Health Committee on Wednesday afternoon, the medical officers reported that there had been 55 deaths during the past fortnight, giv- ing a rate of 19 per thousand. There had been 18 noti- fications of sickness from infectious disease, including one of smallpox from Gerard's Bridge, which there was no doubt had been brought into St. Helens. From the number of towns in the neighbourhood, it was only to be expected that occasional cases would be imported. Tax INFECTIOTIS HOSPITAL.

The Local Government Board has sanctioned the borrowing of £4,000 by the St. Helens Corporation for the purposee of infectious hospital extension at Peasley Cross and at Blackbrook. The estimate of the expendi- ture of the Health Committee of St. Helens for the year ending March, 1894, approved on Wednesday, is £11,700, against an actual expenditure last year of £ll,OOO. The increase is in prospect of an epidemic of cholera or other disease during the summer.

GRANGE PARK.

At the fortnightly meeting of the Health Committee of the St. Helens Corporation, on Wednesday, a plsn for the formation of streets on Grange Park, St. Helens, deposited by Mr. William Goldsworth, estate agent and surveyor, G.Prescot, was submitted by the borough surveyor (Mr. J. C. Broom.) The estate in question is situated in close proximity to the new park presented to the town by Mr. S. Taylor, J.P. The committee approved ot the plan. RESCUR FRO3I DROWNING.

On Monday afternoon, Henry Davies, aged eight years, of School-street, Peasley Cross, St. Helen, had a narrow escape from being drowned. He w hi a field near the Mill Dam, Sutton, with other children' ,rOM the Peasley Cross Congregational Schools, searching for frogs on the edge of the water, when he fell in. • A lad named Swain, of Peasley Cross, threw off his coat and jumped in and brought out the lad, who had gone down twice. The Rev. W. R. Parry and others at once suc- cessfully attended to the little fellow. .

ALLEGED WIFE ASSAULT.

John Thomas Archer, glass maker, Doulton-street, St. Helens' was charged on Monday, with wounding his wife. The parties have been married two and a half years, and have lived very unhappily. On Thursday morning last, a dispute arose, and the prisoner is alleged to lave kicked his wife savagely. She lost a consider- able quantity of blood, and Dr. Gray was called. Mrs. Archer was bedfast.—The prisoner was remanded, bail being allowed: - - -

ALLEGED FARM ROBBERIES.

_ On Tuesday, three men were brought up on remand at the Prescot Police Court, before Alderman 11. R. D'A. Willis. The prisoners were William Atherton, John Highcock, and John Lyon, and they were charged with stealing a number of articles from the farm of Thomas Ashton, Kiln-lane, Eccleston' between the sth and 6th inst., value £1 75. 6d. ; and further charged with steal- ing a hen, value 39., about the same time, from William Fishwick, farmer, Eccleston. They were committed for trial at the quarter sessions.

RgNT AUDIT.

The half-yearly rent audit of Mr.F. Stapleton-Brether- ton's Rainhill estate was held at the Ship Inn, Rainhill. On Tuesday, when Mr. Stapleton-Bretherton returned 15 per cent, of the half-year's rent to the whole of the farm tenants, who expressed themselves highly pleased with the consideration shown towards them by their landlord a similar return having been made at the last rent day. After the audit, which was conducted by Mr. Coiling. wood, the agent. assisted byMr. Lowe, of Warrington, Mr. Stapleton-Bretherton presided at the usual dinner, and discussed various topics on agriculture with his tenants, a very pleasant and social evening being spent.

POLICE COURT V. COUNTY COUIZT.

At St. Helens Police Court on Friday; -tile. adjourned case in which Mr. John Kitchen, estate agent, applied for an ejectment warrant against Owen M'Satron, of 26, Parr-street, came on for hearing. Mr. F. J. Bennett said that on the 7th April, 1892. he gave defendant notice, which expired on the Ist May last. The rent was £ls—£7 10s. each half year. In answer to Mr. Riley, he said he had no knowledge of when defendant entered the house. The property formerly belonged to Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co., but Mr. Michael Duffy was now the owner. Some years back the house had a license, but this was taken away when the license was granted to the Lingholme Hotel.—Mr. Riley sub- mitted the magistrates should dismiss the case on the ground of procedure.—The magistrates considered it a case for the County Court, and dismissed it accord- ingly. BUTCHERS AND THEIR SCALES. . _ . _ _

At St. Miens, on Friday, before the Mayor and other magistrates, Richard Hatton and Walter Marsh, butchers in the St. Helens Market, were summoned for having in use for trade a pair of scales which weto incorrect and unstamped.—Chief Constable Wood stated that on Satur- day last he visited the market, and at Hatton's shop he found a pair ef scales which when tested at 141 b, were 60z. against the purchaser and at 111, were ioz.'„against the purchaser. Hatton said they were Marsh's scales. They were worthless, and at the stamp cfEce they re- fused to stamp them.—Mr. Thomas, who defended, said he understood this pattern of seats had been condemned in Liverpool.—Chief Constable Wood said he had refused to stamp a number of them in St. Helens.—Marsh said he had seen several pairs of the same class of scales in the market which Mr. Wood had passed.—The Mayor. said that had nothing to do with the case before the court.—Mr. Thomas said they denied entirely that the scales were rejected. They had been broken and re- paired, and the stamp might be found under the new plate which had been put on.—The magistrates dis- missed the summons against Hatton, but fined Marsh 20s. and costs for not having the scales stamped.—The -other information against Marsh was dismissed.

A WOMAN BURNED TO DEATH,*

A married woman named M'Gahey was burned to death in Belfast, on Wednesday night. Smoke was seen issuing from the house in which she lived in Spruce-st:, and on some neighbours going inside they found the unfortunate woman lying over the fire, her head and shoulders being reduced to a cinder.

THE QUEEN AND MR. GLADSTONE.

The " Daily Chronicle" announces that it is able to state that there is no truth whatever in the report which has been circulated to the effect that Her Majesty has refused to assent to Lord Spencer being made a mar- quis, and that Mr. Gladstone has thereupon withdrawn his honours list in tote and that there would be no " birthday honours."

PARACHUTE ACCIDENT.

A serious accident occurred at the City and Suburban Park, Dublin, on Thursday. Maud Brooks, of Liverpool, made a balloon ascent, sitting on a trapeze beneath the car. When she had reached the height of a hundred feet she leapt from the trapeze, intending to descend with the support of a parachute; but the parachute failed to expand. She fell to the ground with great force, and si,as severely injured. SERIOUS CHA ROE AGAINST A JOURNALIST.

Augustus M. Moore, journalist, was committed for trial without bail on Thursday, charged with criminally assaulting Kate May, servant t 3 a lady with whom Mr. Moore was acquainted, and who lives in Bentinck- terrace, Regent's Park. This lady said she had been on the stage, and gave the name of Gardiner, but stated in cress-examination that this was not her real name, which was eventually handed to the magistrates on a piece of paper.

AMERICAN IRISHMEN AND IRE HOME RULE BILL

NEW YORIE,THIIRSDAL—The Irish National League has issued a circular in which it congratulates the friends of Ireland in America on the advanced position of the Home Rule programme, and says : Our confidence in the Liberal party has been signally vindicated by the course of events. We are looking to Home Rule, and we will take it from either Liberal or Tory, inasmuch asi our political action is governed solely by the +Wes of Ireland. While thus maintaining our entire political Independence of English parties, we recognise but only as having pledged itself to redress Ireland's wrongs. Until we have reason for change we shall continue our confluence in the.party led by Mr. Gladstone, and in Home Rule. The circular concludes with an appeal for additional funds in aid of the Home Rule cause.

SINGULAR. DEATH AT ST. HELENS.

1 Sergeant Jackson, of the St. Helens Police Force, on Monday,reported to the county coroner the death, which took place under singular circumstances, of Peter Mad- dock, a collier, aged fifty-six years, of 27, Peasley Cross-lane. ' Deceased was employed at the Whitecross Colliery, and oirWednesday afternoon was, with others, at the pit eye, waiting to ascend. He was carrying a tin tea can under his left arm, which appears to have been crushed against his side. A slight bruise only was visible,. and the injury was poulticed. It gradually grew worse and, notwithstanding the attendance yester- day of Drs. Reed, Masson, and Wallace, he died shortly before 12 o'clock.—Mr. Brighouse, county coroner, held an inquest at the Griffin Inn, Peasley , Cross, St. Helens, on Wednesday, on the body of Maddock. CM.Wednes- day, the 17th inst., deceased returned home from the White Cross Colliery,and told his wife that when he was coming from work there was a rush to get inso the cage at the pit eye, and his tin bottle, which he had under his arm, was crushed against his side. •Mrs. Maddock said the side went black, and she poulticed it. He would not let her send word to the colliery that he had been Injured. When she asked him he said, "Never mind, it may get better." On Monday morning she sent for medical assistance, and batween 1.30 a.m. and mid-day, when he died, Drs. Wallace, Masson, and Reid all saw him.—Dr. Masson, who had made a post-mortem exami- nation, said that under the left armpit there was a swel- ling, a small abscess having formed. This had set up acute blood-poisoning, from which be died. The man was in a moribund condition, being both unconscious and delirous,when the doctors were first called .—George Follen, a collier, said the crushing in the colliery com- menced eight or tea yards from the shaft.—By Mr. Hall, Government Inspector of Mines : There was no rail to prevent the crushing. Wm. Brown, another collier,said deceased complained to him that " they had pushed the bottle into his side." A verdict of " Accidental death " was returned.

===== 0002601_18930527_art0159.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMAN EXPORT ACTIVITY.

The results of the operations of the company which was formed in Berlin in September 1891, for the purpose of developing the export trade of Germany in metals and machinery, especially with Eastern Europe and Asiatic Turkey, do not appear to have, so far, been of a very encouraging nature, as the report of 1892 points to a rather heavy loss. The members of the Export-Vetband deutscher Maschinenfabriken und Hiittenwerke are not, however, disheartened by this adverse balance, which they say could only be expected in consequence of the comparatively large sums which have been expended in furthering their cause. For example, a permanent exhibition in Athens was inaugurated last year, a similar institution was erected in Constantinople, and a warehouse was opened at Pirceus. Besides, agencies have been established in various trading centres in Syria and Palestine, to wit, Aleppo, Beyrut, Damascus, Haifa, and Jaffa. British exporters might do worse than keep an eye on the doings of the German body. Iron.

===== 0002601_18930527_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN

BY AN OCCASIONAL CORRESPONDENT. •

The procession of Sunday Schools on Whit Mon- day was of a very interesting and pleasing charac- ter. The elements were all in favour of a success- ful gathering, and the children were fortunate in having it fine and dry for their romping in the fields. The only drawback and one that was freely com- mented upon by the spectators, was the absence from the procession ot the Church of England scholars. It is a pity those in authority, cannot for once in a way, put aside any feeling they may have as to religious equality, and throw in their lot with their dissenting brethren. can quite under- stand the absence of the Congregational Sunday school, the distance the. children would have to traverse being quite sufficient to account for this. But I really cannot understand 'why the church scholars refuse to join.

We are having quite an epidemic of measles among the children in Earlestown, and no steps seem to be taken toward 2 closing the school. There cannot be any question as to the origination of the disease, formn every case the sufferers attend one or other of the schools in town. The mischief arises from the fact that parents will persist in sending children to school, when they already have a child afflicted with measles confined to the house. The commissioners should take severe measures with any case of this Lind, which .comes under their notice, as a warnigg to others who may offend in like manner. There are now a large number of cases, and it would be well if the School Board officer made strict enquiry into every case of ab- sence from school through sickness. In this way he might possibly be able to check the evil to which I have referred Above.

We cannot expect to be free from disease until the Commissioners take steps to remove the offen- sive smells which arise from two or three brooks or ditches in the district. I have frequently drawn attention ton es and Jam given to understand that the Medical-Officer of Health has directed attention to ot4rs repeatedly. But up to the present noth- ing hiii been done to improve the existing state of things. I suppose sufficient disease is not ripe in the district to set them wondering how is has arisen. This is a splendid opportunity for Mr. Col- lingwood, of whom we hear practitally nothing to distinguish himself.

The case of smallpox which has been dealt with this week in Earlestown, again brings to the front the question of an infectious diseases hospital. The 'case broke out in an hotel, but nothing was done to isolate it, or remove the patient until Monday, a day or two after it was discovered that the patient was really suffering from smallpox. A special meet- ing of the Commissoners had to be called, and it was decided to remove the woman at once te Astley I Hall. This was done the same night, and the woman had to endure along and painful drive to an infectious diseases hospital. I think the ques- tion of having some means of isolation a little nearer home might very well be considered, as it is impossible to say what mischief has been done through the delay which occurred in connection with this case. It is to be hoped, however, that no other case will arise ; still it is well to be prepared for any emergency.

WHIT-MONDAY AT ST. HELENS.

The weather at St. Helens Wat 3 all that could be desired by holiday-seekers on Monday. The early excursion traffil, however, was light compared with last year. Trips had been arranged by, the Liberal Association, to Blackpool and SouthpOrt ; Sutton Conservative Club, to Blackpool; St. Thomas's Sun- day School, to, Blackpool; and the Primitive Metho- dists to Southport. The St. Helens Cricket Club left the town early for Preston, where a match had been arranged with the club of that name; and on Tuesday the club visited Blackpool. The chief attraction in the town was the annual fcstival at Littler's Field, Den- ton's Green, of the Parish Church, York-st. Mission, and Windle Mission Sunday Schools. A monster procession was formed at the Parish Church, and proceeded to the field by way of Church-st., Baldwin-St., and Duke•st. It was headed by the St. John's (Ravenhead) Brass Band and the banner of the Men's Bible Class, and was com- posed of the Men's Bible Class (nearly 400), the Women's Bible Class (200), and the scholars, who numbered about 2,000. The Parr Brass Band accompanied the York-st. scholars, who brought up the rear. Banners and flags added to the general effect of the procession, which was witnessed by large crowds of people. Half an hour after arrival the athletic sports were commenced, and these continued until nearly eight o'clock. There were forty- two events and 402 entries. The Gerard's Bridge Con- gregational Mission, in charge of the Rev. G. White, and the Nutgrove Weslryans held their annual field-day at Carr Mill and Elton Head respectively. At the railway station the bookings for Saturday and Monday showed an increase of about 400 on the corresponding period last year. The returns for Monday were :—Guaranteed excursions: Liberal Association, Southport 700; Black- pool 300; Primitive Methodists, Southport, 500; and St. Thomas's, Blackpool, 300. Company's excursions: Llandudno, Conway, Bettws-y-Coed, and Rhyl 100; Windermere and Morecambe, 50; Liverpool, 1,000; Chester, 100; Blackpool, 250; Southport, 450; Man- chester, 120; Belle Vue, 200; Salvation Army, to Run- corn, 100; United Methodists, to Frodsham, 50; ordinary passengers, about 500. On Saturday 550 Volunteers booked to Conway. The number of passengers was greatly in excess of last year, and although the fares were rather smaller, there was a slight increase en last year in the takings.

===== 0002601_18930527_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SHIP CANAL.

According to a recent statement by Sir John Harwood who may be supposed to have the best possible informa- Ulan, the Manchester Ship Canal will be completed in January next. One of the older directors of the com- pany thinks this anticipation may be realised, but his own opinion is rather that it will be March before the canal is opened. Nobody connected with the canal now puts the date later than March or April of next year. The disappointing postponements from year to year have been traceable far less to engineering difficulties than to those connected with money and with law. These have taken turn and turn about from the beginning. Now that the money seems to be secured, thanks to the resolve of the Manchester Corporation to find it, and an outlay of £15,000,000 or upwards is fairly faced, law is again the ruling factor. It is stated that until the Lon- don and North Western and Great Western companies come to terms under arbitration with the Ship Canal Company, the latter•will be prevented from dealing with three of the five,railway lines that the canal must inter- sect. The arbitration proceedings have yet to begin,' and their issue is said to be vital as regards the comple- tion of the canal in any calculable time. Monetary causes of delay being overcome. There is no doubt that the works have made satisfactory progress during the past twelvemonth. Even the railway deviations were com- pleted early this year, and handed over to their respec- tive companies. But whereas the two Cheshire Lines deviations have been, in the one case, brought into use, and in the other put in train for use with- out much further delay, the other three have been so far used for goods traffic alone, and it is only when when they are used for passengers under terms pending arbitration that the old lines can be cut and the Canal works at those points proceed. A length of about eleven miles between Eastham and the river Weaver has been open since September, 1891. It was necessary to push on the work in that section, be- cause under the Company's Act of Parliament ,the im- portant works in the neighbourhood of Runcorn were not to be commenced until there was a clear course for the pre-existing traffic between the river Weaver and Liverpool. This has been one of the causes of the delay of the works as a wholer Th'ePpening of this seaward section has been rewarded with success sufficient to confirm the Canal directors in their anticipations of very striking traffic developments along its banks. The new port, Saltport, at the Weaver mouth, has become a growing centre of shipping traffic with distant parts of the world. The very difficult sections of works to the eastward, round Weston Point in the Mersey estuary, has made a great ad- vance since last Whitsuntide. The section includes a length of close on 3.000 feet of river wall, 16 feet wide at the top, risine from a base of 22 feet. The Weston Mersey Lock (600 feet by 45 feet) gives access to the estuary opposite Weston Point, the Bridgewater Lock (400 feet by 45 feet) forms the river entrance to the Run- corn docks, which consist of 150 acres of docks and buildings, including a seaboard of 900,000 square yards. These works are virtually completed. Water was ad- mitted on May 2, and very shortly the Runcorn docks will resume their former appearance. The Runcorn Old Quay Lock is for the accommodation of the Whines trade. This also is finished. The swing aqueduct of the Bridgewater Canal at Barton, near Manchester, where the Ship Canal passes underneath, is so nearly finished that the pumping of water into it has begun. At the Manchester end of the waterway five or six miles are practically completed, and water has been admitted into the Salford docks for several weeks past. There is still some dredging to be done in the neighbourhood of Salford, but ships could very speedily come up to Man- chester if there were no other obstacle in the way. The Manchester docks are not in so forward a state as those at Salford, but there is no doubt that they will be ready when required. A start has been made with the erection of sheds in the docks, and it is expected that before long abattoirs will be ready for the reception of foreign cattle, which it is intended shall be brought up to Manchester to be slaughtered as soon as the Canal is completed. This is one of the many ways in which it is expected that Manchester will benefit by the Canal. Butchers'-meats may or may not be cheaper to the con- sumer, but it will clearly be brought to the city at less expense than now. THE "BENT BOW."

The "Daily Chronicle" says :—Mr.Gladstene's friends and opponents can join forces in convicting him of a fine niece of inconsistency. In his admirable and strikingly democratic speech on Monday he began by declaring that "The Almighty did not intend the bow always to be bent." We defy the Prime Minister to point to any known public career which has consisted so completely of the " bent bow" as his own. He would, of course, answer us that recreation has played a sufficient part in his life, but we should retort that he has bent the bow of his own health and strength almost as fully in his recreation as in his work. From the time he went up to Oxford to the present moment he has pursued his inte- rests with a perfectly relentless energy. To mcst of us " recreation" includes a proportion of mere idling. It has been well said that a man who is unable to lie on his back in a field and be happy in gazing for an hour into the mild and unresponsive eyes of a cow has lost one of the most important of human capacities. Yet it is im- possible to imagine Mr. Gladstone idling—and certainly his recent exp.rience would hardly induce him to be- gin the particular process of lotus-eating to which we have alluded. No, he has taken life very seriously in- deed. Every moment of his life has been filled with some definite and high purpose, and he has pursued this purpose with all his might, and if there has ever been a more marvellous example of the "bent bow" than the spectacle he now affords in his ninth decade, carrying on his shoulders almost the entire weight of. a contro- versy against odds so great that all the world is gazing with fascination into the lists, we have never heard of it.

INTELLIGENCE OF BUIL•DOUS.

• With regard to the fidelity of the bull-dog, that quality is almost proverbial. Their intelligence has, however, been often questioned, and without doubt many are slow at picking up new ideas ; but the majority of bull-do.; pups of to-day, if carefully and kindly brought up, will develop intelligence at least as great as that of most dogs. The writer's experience has been that a properly trained bull-dog is only equalled in cunning by a fox or a monkey. They have not yet been given a fair chance in this respect, and great improvement will no doubt be shown in a few years. " His Grace the Duke of Guise- bury" (when the property of Mr. Wilton J. Rix) was left for some time at a strange house by his master, who slept there a few nights and then went away, much to the consternation of" Guisebury," who was apparently unable to believe that his master could be safe without him at his side. However, in a day or two, the dog Seemed to console himself with the idea that, after all, business was business, and must be attended to,and that there could be no good in worrying about what was done, and accordingly settled down and became his old self. In about a fortnight, " Guisebury " happened to be wanted, but was nowhere to he found, although a characteristic snorting and whining could be heard when he was called by name. After some time this was traced upstairs to the room where " Guy's " ewner had slept, and there ms lord was found, seated on the bed in great triumph, and saying as plainly as dogs can speak, "This is where my governor sleeps, and I am going to stop here till he comes back. I'm sorry I could not come when you called me, but you see I had other engagements." The doorof the room was open, so that he might have come when called, which, as his whining showed, he evidently rather wished to do, if he bad not stuck to what he thought his duty. This is by no means en exaggerated instance of the intelligence of these dogs, and this particular animal has not been brought up in a manner calculated to develop sagacity, but, on the contrary, had been disgracefully used.

BOATING ON THE MERSEY.

Sir,—l have often wondered why there are no boating clubs in Warrington like those of Chester and Preston. There is a splendid stretch of river between Howley Dye House, and Dobbins Lock (2 miles), the current is slow and every day the water is getting clearer, thanks to the Conservancy Committee of the County Council. There is a field between Howley Dye House and Howley Wharf which can be let off for boating sheds. lam glad te see an enterprising boatman has already started, and hires boats out at is. per hour, and I under- stand he is doing well. I also understand the owner of the land is willing to let it off for boating sheds. Boating is not only exceedingly healthy but is one of the accomplishments everyone can take part in, for if be is weak or in bad health he can steer. Rowing is most healthy, and exercises every part of the body. I enclose my card and shall be glad to join any club or subscribe for a prize for a boat race.—Yours respect- fully,

AN OLD WARRINGTONIAN.

MAN'S IMMORTALITY.

Sir,—Sometime ago—l believe it was at the last sitting of the Church Congress—the question of the Press and Religion was one of the subjects that came up for discus- sion and it was agreed that the cause of Christianity was though; likely to be furthered if the daily and weekly Press of the country would allow the free ventila- tion of rellgious matters in their columns, and to a cer- tain extent, and within reasonable bounds, I quite agree with this opinion. But when the doctrine of man's future existence is not only questioned, but blankly denied, as your correspondent" Snodgrass" and others have done, without as yet, anyone daring to repute their views, I for one shall soon be inclined to swivel round and tate directly the opposite opinion on the mat- ter. Instead of furthering the cause of Christianity such discussions will have, on many minds, directly the opposite effect. Though myself believing strongly in man's future state, yet I admit that some of your corres- pondents have dealt with their views of the subject in a creditable and clever manner. But lam sure there are some of your readers of the opposite school of thought that are equal to the task of refuting and demolishing your correspondents' false line of arguments. Do they think that by simply ignoring such assertisns as " Snod- grass" and" Clifford Dix " makes, that they best serve the cause of Christianity ? If so, then I think the champions of the great Christian, or more properly speAking„ universal doctrine of man's immortality, are labouring under a wrong idea, and one that has about as much vitality in it as that of the ostrich when it hides its head in the sand, thinking thereby to escape from its hunters. Where is Mr. Dignum in this contro- versy? Surely he is equal to the task of taking the ether side. That gentleman, in my opinion, certainly seemed to have rather scored in the recent controversy in your columns on "Buddhism versus Christianity."—l am, sir, yours, kc.

" PSYCHIC."

THE LATE FIRE AT THE FEVER HOSPITAL. Sir,—Will you kindly allow me through the medium of your paper to express my surprise that so little seems to have been done in this town in the way of precaution- ary measures against fires. This is all the more surpris- ing when it is considered that, with two exceptions, Warrington is the only town in England where the sub- stance forming the basis of all fire-proof material is manufactured. Messrs. Crosfield and Sons send their silicate of soda (or glass dissolved in water) to all parts of the globe, and a large part of this is used for render- ing fire-proof, wood and other combustible material. There seems no prophet without '• honour save in his own country," and so perhaps may be explained this remarkable fact, that in the centre of the Silicate industry so little use is made of this important material.

There can be no doubt that a solution of silicate of soda mixed with some mineral colouring matter, forms an excellent material for converting wood, &c., into non-combustible material. As far as my own experi- ence goes the only conditions are : The wood which is to be impregnated, or painted, with this material must be thoroughly dry. 2nd : The mixture must be laid on in very thin coats ; three or four layers are advisable. I know a number of firms who, by impregnating all the wood structure in their factories, e.,pecially that near boilers and furnaces, have every reason to be well satisfied with the result of their care. Such wood-work besides being fire-proof will not warp or be affected by the influence of atmospheric moisture, or steam, and will therefore not show signs of decay for a much longer period than would be the case in the ordinary course of things. The increased protection against, or power to withstand,the destructive influence of weather, which is possessed by wood properly treated with silicate of soda, is alone sufficient to recommend its widespread use. And in the case of edifices where by a fire such certain risks are incurred to human life as in the case of hospi- tals, its use as a precaution against fire is surely more justified ; indeed, seems to me indispensable.—Yours Ecc.,

K. E. MARKEL, Ph. D. Lodge-lane, Warrington, May 25th, 1893. GAMBLING ON ADULT AND CHILDREN'S

LIVES.

Sir,—ln reference to" legal proposal fcrms" as men- tioned in the latter part of my last latter, I desire to add this assertion : that as regards legality of naillioss of policies, now in force, they do not conform with a special Act of Parliament, passed for the express purpose of putting a stop to the gambling in lives.. lam extremely anxious to know why this important provision is almost a dead letter. These policies are either being paid upon by near and distant relatives, in the hope that the next of kio will either sign all the papers at death of the assured—for love or a bribe—or the agent is compelled to help them through in order to make it very easy to pay the money, by bringing forth a nominator, an assignment or a bill form, to patch up such spurious documents.

Will any of your kind readers furnish me with reasons why" our life policies" are so much preferred by ofEces, instead of using forms and policies which bear on their faces the true rclationship and the assurable interest clearly defined, the age at entry, how many offices, and the full amount assured, with policy numbers, and with signatures of the assenting and contracting parties on the form ?

Will anyone describe, how any agent, or "super," or effice, would succeed in passing and completing such forms, when entering four parents, four grandparencs, ten to fifteen uncles and aunts, ten to twenty brothers and sisters, and the thirty-two cousins which is about an average family connection for a man and his wife.

I shall certainly continue to press this matter very seri- ously upon those who countenance the method of using forms which were never intended Exr use when entering another person's life, but only using one form for every adult, no matter who pays the ;premium. I have chal- lenged those important journals the "Chronicle "and " Agents Journal," by inserting very strong articles upon the " me and abuse of Life Assurance," and " Fictitious Polices " viz., in January 1892, and Febru- ary and June 1892, and also in your columns in October 1891 and January 1892, with the result that, although I offered two guineas reward to any person that would refute my argument, set forth in the columns of the " Agents Journal," no one has the courage to defend this method of doing business. There are other gross inconsistences of a glaring character practised by in- dustrial offices that would be better ventilated in the interests of agents and members alike, for allaw me to raMark that a great many of the agents and members are more sinned against than sinning. I shall have occasion to comment on the very import- ant but elastic question of "assurable interest "; also upon payment of death claims, why deductions are made and " full payments ref used,if proof of agea at entry are given; the method of paying " grants, and upon children who die under three months and who are out of benefit. The" sweating of agents "is a standing disgrace in any trade, but when applied to the driving of bands of men, by all kinds of devices, to force men, women and child- ren into contracts. I believe it is repulsive to reflect upon, and ought to receive some attention. Nomina- tions, assignments, and will forms, coupled with deceit and falsehood, work endless mischief, in the cooking of death claims, and I shall have occasion to show your readers how thee are manipulated by persons interested. This method applies to every corner in village, town, and city.—Yours faithfully, _

JOHN MOORFIELD. 44, Parker-at., Warrington, May 25th, 1893.

MINING ROYALTIES. - SOME REMARKABLE FIGURES.

Sir,—l cannot help being highly amused at your con- temporary, the "Observer," in its notes continuously reiterating the phrases " untrue and nonsense," re the facts used by Mr. A. H. Crosfield in his address, and by myself, in the correspondence to your paper, on the sub- ject of mining rents and royalties, but I still maintain that the figures used were, and are,substantially correct. Let me say, too, that most of the information I have used has not been got from the Manchester paper costing a penny, but from the debates in the House of Commons on the question of mining rents and royalties. Your contemporary has questioned, nay, denied, the truth of my assertion that the Duke of Hamilton ex- torts £114,000 per annum from the mining industry and the facts I used about the mining industry in Lanarkshire. Mr. S. Mason, the then member for Mid- Lanarkshire, in bringing the subject of mining royalties forward in the House of Commons on the 14th of May, 1886, said the principle has been recognised by Parliament that the Government is fairly entitled to interfere where the public welfare is involved, and surely, Sir, the monopoly enjoyed by land- lords in regard to minerals, interferes with the welfare of the nation at large. Mr. Mason representing Lanarkshire, which is probably the most important mining constituency in Scotland. said the rents being a fixed sum, have to be paid whether the mines are worked or not. The coalmasters, therefore, have to do something to enable them to pay the rent and dues exacted, and wages not being a fixed sum they took advantage of the labour market and reduced the wages of their men to such a point that the miners were scarcely able to keep body and soul together. Where then is the truth of the assertion that royalties do not affect labour? He (Mr, Mason) further said that some royalties were as much as Is. 4d. per ton. While tho sleeping landlord is receiving this amount, the miner only gets 101. and in some instances as low as 7d. per ton for hewing the coal. Mr. Mason further stated the fact of the Duke of Hamil- ton receiving £114,000 a year in royalties from his pro- perty in Lanarkshire, and yet neither lie nor his fore- fathers contributed one iota towards the creation of this enormous income. The original method of entering into leases for the working of minerals in Scotland w.s not as it is now, by paying fixed rents and royalties, it was a much more simple system. The recognised owner of the minerals received a given quantity of the output of the mine,or a percentage upon it. The larger the quan- tity the greater the return to the landowner, who shared with the mineowner in the prosperity of the mine, but, if the mine was not worked there was nothing to pay, which was a more satisfactory arrangement than the hard and fast system upon which the mines are worked now. _ _

Mr. Howell, the member for Bethnal Green at the time, said that capital is too largely accumulated in too few hands. Mr. C. Fenwick, one of the members for Northumberland said that a friend of his, largely inte- rested in the coal trade in the north of England, for a number of years, had received no return on the capital invested, and was paying the lord of the manor a sum of £20,000 per annum in the shape of royalty rents, while the wages of the miner had been reduced to the lowest possible point,and indeed but for the large sum distributed among the mining population in the north of England through their trade organisations,many able bodied men of independent spirit would have been compelled, through sheer force of circumstances, to have sought relief from the Guardians. In Northumberland alone over £7,000 had been so distributed, while in Durham a much larger amount. During the same period colliery proprietors had been receiving no return on their capi- tal. On the other hand, royalty owners had been receiv- ing large sums of money, part of which, in all fairness, ought to have been devoted as interest on capital,and to the wages of the miners. The figures quoted as Sir Isaac Lowthian Bell's may be seen in the Blue Book containing the evidence of that gentleman before the Commission, and wherein he stated that the royalties on pig iron varied from 3s. to fis., this going into the pockets of private individuals while in Germany they wore 81,in France Bd. ,and in the United States the amount varies very considerably, but the royalties in those countries, in the main, are in the hands of the State.

Mr. Conybeare, quoting a case, at the gime time, in Cornwall where China clay is the principal industry, said that the system was mast tyrannical. The custom is for 21 years leases, and it is not merely digging for clay, for the owner of the sett must lay out from £B,OOO to £lO,OOO in kilns, tanks, machinery. The rent is usually fixed at several hundreds yearly, which is arranged shall merge into dues when any clay is being returned which shall create a profit. Here is the injustice of the system. Should the tenant not find clay enough to pay the rent, the dues have still to be paid, the dues or royalties averaging from is. 6d. to 4s. per ton. In one case two lords own one the water, the other the clay, the lessees having to pay 2s. for the one and 2s. 6d. for the other, thus making the royalties 49. 6d. per ton. Here is a quotation from one of the leases. At the end of 21 years, or at the determination of the lease, the tenant must leave" all and every suet. work, or works, with buildings, sheds, engine houses, levels, shafts, water courses Am., including tanks, settling pits, and drying sheds, in good and efficient repair," for the land- lord to take possession without compensation being Mr. W. Abrahams, M.P., said that within one and a half miles of the Rhondda Valley,there are 10 collieries producing daily 8,000 tons of coal, the royalties being 9d. per ton. These colleries belong to three landlords, who have been receiving £3OO per day or close upon £32,000 per year each. The South Wales coalfield has been producing annually for the last five years at the rate of 16,000,000 tons, which means that £600,000 has been divided yearly amongst a few landlords, or in ten years, we find that these few individuals have had as their share, no less than £6,000,000, and it might be reasonably asked, for what ? What have the landlords done ? Since the coal has been put there by an all-wise providence for the benefit of man-kind, one would like to know what right they have to these enormous bene- fits. While the employers may go to the dogs, work- men may starve, the landlords must have their pound of flesh, and extra weight at that. .1 hen, sir, having read the report of the Commission recently issued, 1 cannot accept the same as being full and complete, especially in the face of a communication I have received from a member of that Commission wherein he states the Commission found it difficult to get full and accurate figures as to Royalties paid. It is said that trade has increased, but it does not follow that there might not have been a much greater increase if this embargo had not been upon the industry. I have no doubt we are placed at a great disadvantage by this exorbitant incubus, and soon the question of nation- alization of both minerals and the land will have to be considered seriously by our legislature.—Yours. Warrington, May 19th, 1893.

I enclose copy of a letter I have received from Mr. A. H. Crosfield re the comments in the " Observer " upon his address, and the controversy that has bees the out- come of the address :

May 11th, 1893.—Dear Sir,—l am obliged for the copies of the' Observer" you sent me. lam unfor- tunately rarely able to study this journal, so that I had missed the notes to which you drew my attention. Other engagements do not admit of my entering into any controversy even though this may not exactly fall in with editorial purposes at the "Observer " office. But as you are helping to thrash out the question of these mining royalties, and have been good enough otherwise to support my paper for the Debating Society, let me draw your attention to the following points :—ln the first place the report by the Commission on Royalties is more favourable than some of us feared it might have been. But on the strength of this, yoer critic makes a remarkable assertion,that mining royalties have nothing whatever to do with trade. That is putting the ease in a nutshell. Of course he is entitled to his opinions, but you might be excused if you felt inclined to reply in one syllable " fudge." When business is cut fine and gets down to what the Yankees call " bed rock it doesn't take much to turn the scale agoinst it. That there has been an enormous increase in the mining industry is no proof that that inclease might not have been still greater._

Soap Trade : The reference to the soap trade is not a little entertaining. Fabulous fortunes appear to be the order of the day, but employers are it seems having much better times of it than the men, compared with the old days. Well now I happen to have been secretary for the northern soap-makers for some years past, and have had a good many opportunities of learning at first hand the actual state of trade. And lam divulging no trade secret in saying that in London, Bristol, Yorkshire, and also in minor English centres for the soap trade, the competition of late years has been keener than it ever was before. Wages on the other hand have risen con- siderably above what they were formerly.* The state- ments I refer to are, therefore, about as inaccurate and misleading as they well could be. If he refers in them to the occasional success of highly speculative enterprise then, intentionally or otherwise, he confounds a branch of trade which stimulates industry with the excessive 1 outlay or charge that tends to check it ; and that which tends to improve the labour market with what tends to damage it. This may be instructive and a warning against the obfuscation Toryism in some cases is liable to produce, but it serves no other useful purpose, and certainly does not form the basis for any serious discus- sion of the practical issue in question. By way of putting a finishing touch to his criticism, this brilliant writer, after thus dragging in the soap trade, refers in the same breath to mining royalties as a red herring drawn by manufacturers ! Whether for unconscious innocence or mere effrontery this fairly takes the cake which the writer should be left to enjoy. Graduated taxation : If the Soap trade was introduced as an excuse for changing the subject, the choice was not to put too fine a point on it, unfortunate. Soap is a slippery thing to handle. The very paper which the writer fondly imagines he is pulling to pieces afforded him plenty of choice. It was not entirely devoted to mining royalties though his readers might think so. Take fer instance the question of revised taxation. What an opportunity for dilating on the impracticability of graduation or differentiation, and having proved this to his satisfaction, of denouncing the principle as t applied to death duties—if indeed he rejects this princi- I pie, whieh (not entirely new) is at present somewhat

The long-expected opening of the new baths took place on Wednesday afternoon, before a very dis- tinguished company. Before the opening ceremony was performed, the Mayor invited the town coun- cillors and other officials and friends to the Mayor's Parlour of the Town Hall, when a good supply of refreshments was partaken of. The company then formed in procession at the front of the Hall, and headed by the Chief Constable and eight members of the police force, marched to the new baths. At three o'clock, almost to the minute, the Mayor opened the door and entered into the building. The procession then marched up to a platform i at the three-feet end of the baths,where a splendid view of the baths could be obtained. The public were after- wards admitted, and a good number availed them- selves of the opportunity of seeing the performances of Professor Lomax, who undertook to do the ini- tial swimming operations. Among those present were the Mayor and Mayoress (Alderman and Mrs. Edward Greenell), Alder.nan Holmes,Alderman and , Mrs. Harrison, Alderman and Mrs. Francomb, Alder- man Chandley, Alderman Wright,Councillor Plump- ton, Councillor and Mrs. Smethurst, Councillor and ' Mrs. Shaw, Mr. Stanley Wilson, Councillor Wilkin- son, Mrs. Shaw-Green, Mr. Longdin ('orough stir- veyor),Councillor R. T. Fairclough, Councillor Hen- ton, Mr. Holt, Councillor Browne,Councillor Garner, Mr. and Mrs. Madeley, Councillor and Mrs. James Fairclough, Dr. and Mrs. Genial), Councillor and Mrs. Bolton, Mr. Haddock (gas manager), Mr. and Mrs. Woodcock, Councillor and Mrs. Ilepherd,Coun- cillor Muir,Councillor and the Misses Bonnell,Coun- cillor and Mrs. Hill, Councillor H. Roberts, Mr. G. F. F. White, Councillor Dr. Cannel], Councillor and Mrs. Dixon' Dr. Peacocke, Superintendent Turner, Dr. and the Misses White, Revs. E.C. E. Carleton, T. Ross, and Horden, Mr. W. Dtggle, Mr. and Mrs. R.H. Pearson, Mr. Henry Greenall, Father Craven,Father Turner, and Father Cody. Mr. Birtles, before the proceedings commenced took a photograph of the occupants of the platform. Alderman Chandley occupied the chair during the opering proceedings, and in introducing the Mayor,said that he thought that most of those present would re- member that the baths in Warrington had had a mere chequered existence than any of the other institutions in the hands of the Corporation. They would no doubt, remember that the baths were originally in Mersey-fit. They were tolerably capacious, but were afterwards found to be inconvenient, and they were removed to Church-st. The Church-st. baths were for various reasons not well supported, and they were removed to Legh-st. on the present site. They were found after- wards to be Unsuccessful financially, and they wese pur- chased by the Corporation. Some time afterwards, the public began to complain that they were inadequate for their purpose, and wished for further enlargements. A scheme was accordingly placed before the Town Hall, Park, and Baths Committee, and a very handsome sum was placed at their disposal by the Town Council to- wards the erection of the new baths, and for the exten- sion of the old one. At the time very much discussion went on as to whether it was judicious or wise to spend so much money on an institution that had been so in- differently attended by the mass of the public. It was then urged that when the new baths were built they would be more attractive than the former filthy and disagreeable one. The committee accordingly adopted the recommendation of the Council, and they placed before the public ot Warrington the bath they now saw. (Applause.) He thought that the public would most certainly say that the Corporation had done what was desirable to be done. They had placed the bath within the reach of all classes of men. A Turkish bath was found to be fraught with too much expense, and it was consideied undesirable to go to the outlay of erecting one, as in many other towns larger than War- rington, Turkish baths were worked at a serious loss. It now depended upon the people of Warrington whether, the baths were a success or not. The Council invited the public to make all possible use of them. They could do no more. It was thought at the time of the laying of the foundation stone, a proper thing to invite the then Mayor to lay it, and it was now desirable that the present Mayor should perform the opening ceremony. (Applause.) With regard to the Mayor, he had taken part in the promotion and general welfare of the Cor- poration, and he (the speaker) could not leave out the name of the Mayoress. (Applause.) She and the Mayor had taken part in many charitable works for the welfare of Warrington. It was with great pleasure that he offered the Mayor on the present occasicn of opening the baths, a salver, as a souvenir of the occasion, in the name of the Corporation. He hoped the Mayor would be enabled in years to come to look back on the present occasion, as one at least in which he stood proud to occupy the position as Mayor of Warrington, and with a certainty of having so many good, loyal and faithful townsmen, whose esteem he had so successfully earned. (Applause.) Alderman Chandley then called upon the Mayor to declare the baths open. The MATOR, who was received with applause, said that he had been requested by the chairman, Alderman Chandley, on behalf of the Town Hall, Park and Baths Committee, to open the new plunge bath, which he believed to be equal to any in the county of Lancashire. The cause of the extension had been owing to the Cor- poration and the public of Warrington feeling most strongly that the accommodation provided by the old baths had become totally inadequate for the town with its great increase of population since they were built on May Ist, 1864. He thought that they had great cause to congratulate themselves that the bath which had been erected was a great improvement. Feeling strongly as he did, the necessity of good public baths, which "night be classed as a great consideration from a sanitary point of view, in a town like Warrington, which had no river worthy of the name, owing to pollution above the weir ever which they had no control, he sincerely hoped that the baths would receive the support they deserved, so that they might be self-supporting, and a comfort to the community at large. He could not allow the occas- ion to pass without thanking in the name of the town, those by whose assistance the baths were as they saw them that day. First and foremost was the Chairman —(applause)—and the members of the Town Hall, Park and Baths Committee which had given so much atten- tion to the work. They thanked them most heartily for the trouble they had taken, not only with the baths but also in the improvements of the park and public gardens. There were also Mr. Longdin, the architect.and Messrs. Gibson, the builders and contractors; and Messrs. Tho- mson and Taylor, and many others who had so ably assisted in the erection of the baths. The Mayor then declared the baths open.

- The salver which was presented to the Mayor by the Chairman and members of the Baths Committee was supplied by Messrs. William Woods and Son, silver- smiths, Sankey-street, and was of solid silver, the design being ot Louis Quatorze period. . .

Inimediately alter the Mayor sat down, an amusing incident occurred which was no doubt pre-arranged. Fair Turner the well-known footballer, and Dick Scans- brick the humourist, were together on the side of the baths. [hey pretended to have a quarrel, and Turner taking hold of Scarishrick threw him into the water, he being at the time fully dressed. Turner then jumped in after him, and the two caused roars of laughter by their antics.

The programme of the afternoon was then begun, Alderman Francomb taking the chair. The first part consisted of swimming feats by Professor Lomax who went through the following feats :—" Swimming like a porpoise," " Sculling on chest, feet first," "Monkey up a stick," " Swmming on the back, with one leg out of water," "Swimming like a seal," " Peeling an apple under water," " Eating under water," "Swimming like a crab," "Drinking under water," "Swimming on the back with both legs out of water," "Swimming with hands and feet tied," "Showing how to rescue a drown- ing person," "Showing how to break away from a drowning person when clutched by the wrist, both wrists, waist, or the head," "Spinning like a top," "Picking up a coin with the mouth," "Creeping at the bottom of the bath." The professor went through these feats in a first class manner, and elicited frequent ap- plause. The second part of the programme consisted of swimming feats by Masters W. Slater, aged 10 years; R. Lomax, aged 10, W. Lomax, aged eight, and J. Slater, aged six. These four did some clever ornamen- tal swimming, as also did Miss Ethel M. Slater, aged 11, and Miss Gertrude Slater, aged eight. The afternoon's entertainment was closed by a water polo match between Barlow's team and Turner's team which resulted in the victory of Barlow's team by two goals to one. In the evening Councillor Shaw took the chair. Pro. Lomax and Masters W. Slater, R. Lomax, W. Lomax, and J. Slater again gave an exhibition of their clever- seas in the water, Professor Lomax, in addition to the afternoons programme, smoking under the water, and imitating a screw steamer. A water polo match was then held between St. Helens and Warrington,which re- sulted in a decisive victory for St. Helens by six goals to two. The Warrington team were completely out- played when they defended the seven feet end, although they kept on equal terms with their opponents when they were at the three feet end.

_ The new bath is without doubt a splendid bath. The entrance is by a long lobby, from ;the ceiling of which a number of ferns and other flowers hung which gave the place a pretty appearance. In the bath itself, there are platforms on each side,from which spectators can watch the proceedings without any danger of being drenched by the splash of the divers. Here again flower baskats hang from the ceiling, and atthe three feet end is 'a tube from which a shower bath can be obtained. The line of blue glazed bricks give a tine effect to the baths. There was a large number of persons present at the open- ing, and a still larger number at the night performance.

A Sheriff's jury sitting at Ywrk on Wednesday, awarded £5O damages to Louisa Bardman Robinson, domestic servant, in an action for breach of promise brought against Isaac William Wise, cab-driver, of Beverley. The defendant disappeared on the morning fixed for his wedding, and kept out of the way for a considerable time.

About three years ago the increasing demandimade upon the accommoaation for patients at the War- rington Infirmary, induced the Committee to pro- mote a scheme for enlarging the building. Accord- ingly plans were drawn up and public subscriptions invited and received. The first addition was the new dispensary which overlooks a portion of the park and which has been in use fifteen months ago Next, three additional wards were erected on the first floor, and three others were erected on the top story. Every accommodation has been placed in the rooms which have been built according to the latest avid most approved styles. The fire places in them are situated in the centre of the room, and the fire places in the old wards have been altered to the same position. Altogether these six wards will accommodate an additional 20 inmates, there having hitherto only been accom- modation for 23. Several alterations have been made to the operating room including the laying of tiles on the floor, which is an acceptable improvement to those who have to use the room. A new waiting-room has also been I constructed on the ground floor and an additional room for treating " accidents." The new dispensary will be opened on Thursday next, at three o'clock by Major Pickmere. The Beamont ward will be named by Lady Greenall,the Greenall ward by Mrs. Pickmere, the Pickmere ward by the Mayoress, the Winmarleigh ward by Mrs. Beamont, the Davies ward by Mrs. Litton, and the Litton ward by Mrs. Armitage. We are requested to stste that gifts of plants and cut flowers will be thankfully received by the house surgeons.

NOTES ON GAMES AND

fIILETICS.

CRICKET.

Ourfirst eleven were entitled, on Saturday, to wear collectively the wreath of victory. They won the game against Werneth by 114 to 65. But perhaps it is worth mentioning that Smith ran 69 out of the 114 for Warring- ton, indeed it may be suggested that had he not scored, that Warrington would not have been entitled to the above mentioned wreath. And then again it should be mentioned that Warrington played eleven wickets to their opponents eight, and so some people would think it wasn't a streight win ; which it wasn't. Not another placer in the match than Simth scored more than 14-, except Clark who came off with 17. S. M. Crosfield and J. J. Crosfield took over the responsibilities of bowling, and also took over eight wickets.

Werneth second eleven met the Warrington second eleven at Werneth and beat them by 62 runs to 56. There was very little difference between the play of each team, and the victory certainly could not be called a decisive one. Running matches interspersed the game here and there when the players had to run to the pavilion to evade the drenching tears of " old Mau Pluvius."

Those doughty champions of the West End, Sankey Wesleyans and Sankey Wire Mills, met at Mill-lane, on Saturday, in deadly combat, determined to do or die or something after that fashion. By the way, it is rather peculiar that a large part of the Sankey Wire Mills team do not work at the Sankey Wire Mills at all, but let us get to the game. The Wesleyans were decidedly the favourities for the day, as they had by far the better record. The Wire mills went in first, and so well were they looked after that they were all out for 27, Cook being the highest with only six. When the Wesleyans went in, it seemed to be all over bar shouting, when they scored 21 for the loss of one wicket. Unfortunately for the Wesleyans a severe storm came on, which lasted for a long time, and when Jupiter Pluvius thought fit to turn off the tap, the wicket was completely soaked, and spoiled. The batsmen could scarcely stand, and the Weslevans only scored 33 altogether, which was very poor considering their good start. CoA as wicket keeper for the Wire Mills proved to ben regular " stunner," and the way he watched his wicket is well shown as Mr. Extras scored nothing. Clare on the opposite side was also ml good form. Watkins was scarcely so successful as liana in his bowling, but he received a loud cheer when in his innings by a single, he placed the match beyond all danger. Jefferson for the Wesleyans, made his presence known by a score of 17. The Wesleyans will soon get used to the " hat " trick, as Simpson did it again on Saturday. The result on Saturday was very gratifying to the Wesleyans, as they had not yet tasted defeat this season ; on the other hand the Wire Mills had only tasted the sweets of victory once.

The juniors of Sankey Bridges were again on the war- path on Saturday, and like their seniors have yet to be defeated. Their latest victims were Buckley-st. juniors, whom they whalloped by 13. Well done Junicrs ! keep at it. and you will make your name a terror to all other juniors in and around Warrington.

The Sankey Wesleyans 2nd cut a very poor figure against the Wire Mills 2nd. W. Woodcock for the latter scored 29, the innings realising 72. The Weslevans on going in,only made 8 for six wickets; and time saved them from a disastrous defeat. A feature of this match was the number who were clean bowled, for out of the 16 wickets taken, no fewer than 14 were clean bowled. This is surely a record in this line.

CYCLING.

The temperance section of the employees of the Tanners-lane, Tanning Co., Limited, are arranging a cycle club for this season. Mr. E. M. Crosfield. the managing director, who has set a good example to other persons in similar position 3, by enabling his men to get their machines at first cost, has kindly consented to be president. Mr. S. M. Crosfield, the sub-captain of the Lancashire County Cricket team, is a vice president, as also are Messrs. Percy Mortimer, E. A. Atkin, and John Roberts. A committee of management bas been formed and the club has already bad two runs. On Monday, they proceeded as far as Knutsford, and to-day weather, etc., permitting, they will have a run to itainhill. It is intended to have a trip each Saturday, and as the club are very desirous of raising a fund to meet emergencies, donations from friends disposed to assist them, will be thankfully received by thesecretary.

The Warrington Cycling Club had a "good time " at Mickle Trafford, on Saturday, and on Monday about 20 of the members went via Holmes Chapel and Middle- wich to Dalamere Forest, where they spent an enjoyable afternoon. On their return jotirney they came through Frodsham, where they made a short halt. The club made good use of their time during the Whitsuntide holidays as on Tuesday 18 members rode to Bucklow Hill. The weather and roads were in good order and the runs were much enjoyed.

Whit-Monday was the occasion of the Anfield C.C. 100 miles invitation handicap. The starting point was hard by Whizchurch, and the run •was through Shawbery, Hodnet, Wem, and back ;xi Whitchurch. "Crack " cyclists from all parts .f the country are brought toge- ther in this race, which has boea taken part in by such knights of the wheel as Holbein, Shorland, Edge, and Bidlake. The ccmpany this year was again quite first- class.

It is highly creditable to Mr. J. Waddington, of the Warrington Cycling Club,that he should come in second in the race, being only two minutes behind the winning rider. Waddington's performance was a splendid one, when it is considered that he is practically a Rovice at racing, and as a working-man has only limited opportu- nities for training. His machine was a last year's road- ster, weighing 331bø., most of the other competitors be- ing mounted upon "thoroughbred racers," varying from 201bS. to 301tH.In weight. That the pace in the race was "killing," may be gathered from the fact that only five men out of the 24 starters completed the journey. Wad- dington, and Conway, of the Anfield Club, who was the winner, were both scratch men. Mr. Waddington, it may be mentioned, was paced in a generous, sportsman- like manner,by a number of Manchester cyclists,and also by two members of his own club, T. Pierpoint and F. Silvertou, the latter coming in to the winning post with him.

By the way, I should like to know what are the War- rington cyclists about. Here we have a Volunteer Corps requir!ng a detachment of cyclists, and only Mr. P. Fair could be procured to go to the camp at Conway during the week. lam sorry to hear this, because we have such a fine lot of yousg fellows in he Warrington Cycling Clubs, and some of them ought to go and join the suggested detachment, even if only for their own .)enefit.

SWIMMING.

lam glad to see that Mr. A. Barlow has been ap- pointed bath manager to the new baths, which were opened on Wednesday afternoon. Two Polo matches were played,one between Mr. Barlow's team and " Fair " Turner's team, which was won by the former, and one between Warrington team and the St. Helens team, won by St. Helens.

It was a good idea to introduce the Polo, and what I sinscerly hope is that it will be kept up, that it will be- come one of the attractions of the baths, and that it will be played by a team who will go in for the thing heart and soul. But lexpect Barlow will not lot grass crow under his feet, for with his extensive knowledge of aquatic matters he should be able to stir things up con- siderably.

FOOTBALL.

Mr. J. E. Warren, hon. secretary of the Warrington F.C., has received the following communication from the Mossley secretary, which will interest local football enthusiasts : "At a committee meeting of the Mossley F.C., a resolution was unanimously adopted thanking your club for the generous gift of £7 10A., in lieu of a match at Mossley, in April last. I desire personally to also thank you for the courteous and generous manner in which you have met us, and trust that the good feel- ing which exists between our two clubs may long con- tinue and that next season may prove a good season for our respective clubs."

The annual congress .f Co-operative Societies of Great Britain and Ireland opened on Saturday at Bristol, when upwards of 700 delegates from all parts of the kingdom were present. Mr. Tom Mann was the chief speaker at a public meeting held on Saturday evening. Daniel Davies, secretary to the Unity Lodge of Odd- fellows, Swansea, was on Thursday sentenced to six weeks' imprisonment for embezzling a small sum of money.

F RODS HAM.

TEM FRIENDLY SOCIETY PROCESSIONS. —The village of Frodsham was, as usual, crowded with visitors from the neighbouring towns on Monday, where they had pro- ceeded with the object of getting a breath of fresh air and a day's general "outing." The customary hobby horses, swings, aunt sallies, &c., were en evidence in the village, whilst dancing was carried on with zest on Overton Hill. In the morning a procession of the Foresters, Oddfellows, and Druids' Societies took place. The main streets were paraded, each society carrying its banner and headed by a band, the Foresters being led by the Verdin Brass Baud, the Druids by Frodsham Volunteer and the Parish Church Bible Class Bands, whilst the Frodstiam Military Band preceded the Odd- fellows. The company marched to the Parish Church, where a service was held by the Rev. H. B. Blogg (vicar), assisted by Rev. F. T. Dickinson (curate). An appropriate sermon was delivered by the Vicar. The various societies subsequently proceeded to the Vicar- age grounds where selections of music were rendered by the bands, alter which a move was made to their res- pective headquarters, where dinner was provided. CHANGE OF STATIONSIASTER.—Mr. Thomas Potts, who for 33 years has acted as stationmaster at Frodsham, has been superannuated, and his successor is Mr. Lam- bert, of Ellesmere Port. Mr. Potts who is verging upon 70 years of age, has spent 39 years in the service of the railway company, and of these as already stated the third of a century has been passed at Frodsham. He came there soon after the provision of a station. Dur- ing his lengthy term of office, he fulfilled his duties in a conscientious manner, and possessing genial character- istics, he was held in high esteem by all those who were brought in contact with him. Large numbers join in the wish that he may have in store many pleasant and happy years.

NEW POOR RATE-On Wednesday, at Frodsbam Police Court, Messrs. J. Handley and T. Rigby, affixed their signatures to a new poor rate of Is. in the 2, estimated to realise £392 16s. 9d.

A SALE OF WORE.—On Wednesday afternoon a sale of work in connection with the Trinity Wesleyan Sun- day School, was opened by Miss Dutton, of Moore. During the evening entertainments were given in an adjoining room, which were thoroughly enjoyed. The proceeds of the sale realised £3l 9s. 6d., and this is to go towards giving the scholars of the Trinity Sunday School a special treat in commemoration of the school's centenary. Although it is quite certain that the school was instituted in the year 1793, the exact time of the year has not as yet been ascertained.

SONS OF TEMPERANCE. The usual meeting of the Samuel Morley cadet section of the Sons of Temperance was held in the Co-operative Hall. on Wednesday evening. After the formal opening three members were initiated, and arrangements were made for the annual pic- nic to Micklehead Green, on Tune 3rd, and for the parents to accompany the children. A report was given of the National Division Competition, in which the name of Theo. Clare appeared aq hav- ing taken second prize for six best reasons why young people should not smoke.

SMALLPDX AT EARLESTOWN. A case of smallpox has been reported from Earlestown to the Medical Officer of Health, the victim being Mrs. Dickenson, wife of the manager of the Wellington Elotel, Earlestown. The unfortunate lady has only recently been confined, and the systoms of smallpox were developed last week end. On the case being reported, it was de- cided to remove the patient to Astley Hall at once, which was done on Monday night. On Tuesday the hotel was closed, and thoroughly fumigated, with a view to prevent the spread of the disease.
