GOOD TEMPLAR,.

The weekly session of the Hope of Earlestown lodge of Good Templars was held at the Co-opera- tive Hall on Tuesday night. Bro. E. Newman pre- sided and there was a fair attendance of members. One candidate was initiated to membership. An invitation to join the hope of Hindley Lodge in celebrating their 25th anniversary was received,but the consideration of it was deferred. Several other matters were brought 11D and considered. The prcgraintue of the evening was an entertainment by the new officers, the C.T. being responsible, and an interesting selection of songs and recitations was given. WHIT-MONDAT AT EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON. Monday was observed as a general holiday in Earlestown. The weather was beautifully fine and warm, notwithstanding the fact that threatening clouds overhung the district in the early morning. These, however, cleared away,and the sun shone out brilliantly the whole of the day. All the works were closed, add there was in consequence a large exodus of people for various destinations, a large contingent going to Southport. The annual pro- ,1 cession of Sunday school scholars took place and was witnessed by a large number of spectators, particulars will he found.in another column. At Newton everything was very quiet, the works of Messrs. McCorquodale and CO. running as usual. EARLESTOWN WESLEYAN BAND OF HOPE.

The first of a series of monthly open air meetings in connection with the above society, was held at Newton Common on Thursday evening. Mr. W. Pearson presided. and there was a large number of persons present. Addresses were delivered by the Chairman, Messrs. 0. Pickett and G. Bunting. A petition to the House of Commons in support of the Local Veto Bill, was adopted on the motion of Mr. T. Wheatley seconded by Mr. D. Robinson. Recita- tions were given by Mr. T. Picket. and several temperance hymns were sung. At the close of the meeting nine pledges were taken up, and altogether the meeting was considered to be a most successful one.

MEETING OF RECHABITES.

The monthly meeting of the Mizpah tent, of the 1.0. R. was held at the Co-operative Hall, on Thurs- day evening, Bro. E. J. George presiding over a large attendance. The meeting was opened in due form, and after the initiation of candidates and the proposal of others it was resolve'd to form a special fund for the assistance of members in distress, and for other purposes. The contributions to this fund to be a halfpenny per member per month, such fund to be added to by special efforts. The meeting was very enthusiastic in the adoption of this rule, it being pointed out how advisable it was that mem- bers in distress should be assisted in the payment of their contributions to keep them in benefit. Fur- ther steps were taken in the formation of the juvenile branch, of the order, which will shortly be - incorporated by a special meeting. It was stated that the Hon. T. W. Legli, M.P., had written to the effect that he had duly presented the petition of the tent in favour of the Local Control Bill.

SCARLET FEVER IN THE CHESHIRE

REGIMENT.

The Cheshire Regiment, stationed at Sheffield have been ordered to eo under canvass in consequence of the prevalence of scarlet fever in the barracks. There are nearly fifty cases of this disease, and it continues to spread. It has been found necessary to remove the or- dinary patients from the barracks hospital to a large marquee on the parade ground to make room for scar- let fever patients. A convenient camping ground has been selected near the barracks.

THE INSUBORDINATION AT ALDER- SHOT.

===== 0002601_18930527_art0194.txt | included=true | included =====
SENTENCES ON SOLDIERS.

Four more privates of the 20th Hussars concerned in the recent riotihg at Aldershot have received sentence. Privates Cockburn and Harwell were awarded twelve months' hard labour, Private Hartnett eleven months' imprisonment, and Private Saxby ten months' imprison- ment, and all are dismissed the service with ignominy. The restrictions against the cavalry brigade at Aldershot have now been withdrawn, and they have returned to barracks, and the order prohibiting men from taking part in the military tournament has been cancelled.

===== 0002601_18930527_art0195.txt | included=true | included =====
SEQUEL TO A SALVATION ARMY

MEETING. MYSTERIOUS DEATH AT WIGAN.

The Wigan police received intimation of a singular death yesterday morning. Mary Stevenson, of Long- lane, Ashton, after watching a Salvation Army meeting, returned home and entered into a heated argument on the merits of the army with her husband. She became excited and scratched his face. It is alleged that he jumped up and put her on the sofa. A short time after- wards she was seen in her nightdress by a man passing. She invited him in, and said," That was the man that did it," referring to her husband. She was found to be suffering from paralysis. Dr. Lotham attended to the woman till her death, and has expressed the opinion that death is due to hemorrhage of the brain, resulting from injury. The police are instituting inquiries.

===== 0002601_18930527_art0196.txt | included=true | included =====
THE INTERNATIONAL MINERS' CONFERENCE. AN IMPORTANT PROPOSAL.

BRUSSELS, FEILIAL—The Miners' Conference assembled this morning at ten o'clock. A long discussion arose as to a scheme put forward by M. Emile Lewy, a Belgian mineowner, for increasing the wages of miners. Event- ually, at the instance of the English delegates, who said the scheme seemed complicated, and rkquired close con- sideration, it was resolved to appoint an international committee to examine the proposals of M. Lewy, and to report to the next congress. The general principal of this scheme is the establishment of a universal interna- tional syndicate of mineowners, who, it is suggested, should advance the price of coal, and give the miners one-fourth of the profits. This would be tantamount to doubling their present wages. There are other provisions for securing a limitation of the output and the working off of stocks. Herr Schroeder, a German delegate, strongly urged that the next congress should be held in Germany, and expressed the belief that the Emperor would permit it to be held without interference. It was agreed that the International Committee should adopt this suggestion if possible.

===== 0002601_18930527_art0197.txt | included=true | included =====
TRAGIC AFFAIR AT NORTIIAAIPTON

A DOCTOR AND HIS SWEETHEART POISON THEMSELVES.

A Northampton correspondent telegraphs that Dr. Andrew Aikman was found dead at his house yesterday morning. Deceased was to have been married a fort- night ago, but on the eve of the wedding friends inter- fered and induced him to leave Northampton, and the lady who was to be his wife was so much upset that she took poison, and is still lying ill. Dr. Aikman, returned heme on Thursday night, and it is believed that in his trouble he, too, poisoned himself. A Northampton correspondent, telegraphing later, states that Dr. Wickman had up to Thursday been stay- ing with his parents in Edinburgh. On reaching Nort- hampton he drove at once to the house of Barratt,-the lady to whom he was to have been married, and was conducted by Miss Barratt's parents to her bedroom, where he fondly embraced and kissed her, and asked her mother to take great care of her. No words of approach were used by Miss Barrett, and after leaving her he drove to his own house, where he was joined by his brother Charles, and the Rev. T.Crossby Roberts, Baptist minister. The three had supper together, and deceased, who appeared fairly cheerful, afterwards partook of some whisky and then proceeded upstairs. On coining downstairs again he drank something from a glass, and appeared AS though he was about to burst out crying. He then threw out his arms and his fingers began twitching, and this so alarmed Er. Roberts and deceased's brother that they sent for medical assistance. Dr. Copley arrived in the course of a few minutes, but his efforts were of no avail as deceased sank and died very soon after Or. Copley reached the house. A bottle partially filled with poison was afterwards found in his bedroom.

Miss Barrett is the daughter of a shoe operative, and Dr. Aikman's friends are said to have objected to his marrying into such a humble family.

===== 0002601_18930527_art0198.txt | included=true | included =====
SOME USEFUL ELECTORAL STATISTICS.

Mr. J. W. Logan, M.P., speaking at Blaby, in the Ilarborough division, on Tuesday, said whilst Tories charged Mr. Gladstone with plotting the " dismember- ment of the Empire" they disinembared the Empire for the sake of an argument. They said that England, as separated from the rest of the United Kingdom, was opposed to Home Rule, but whilst the result of the general election gave some colour to the statement, it was qualified by the fact that out of 78 seats gamed by the Liberals in 1892 no less than 72 were in England alome. Lord Randolph Churchill had said :—" We must never forget that the Unionist party represents the majority of the British people," but the noble Lord was again speaking without notes, for as a matter of fact the Liberals polled 16,000 more British votes in 1892 than the Tories and Unionists combined. Replying to Lord , Salisbury's assertion that" if only 800 voters in England and Scotland bad voted differently there would have been no Homo Rule majority in the House of Commons," Mr. Logan said if 471 voters in England and Scotland had voted differently Mr. Gladstone would have had a majority of 78, and if 187 voters in England and Scot- land had voted differently Mr. Gladstone's niajority would have been 88. So far as contested elections were concerned, Mr. Gladstone had 658,554 more British votes behind his majority of 40 than Lord Salisbury had behind his majority of 116 in 1888. Taking the con- tested elections of the United Kingdom, Lord Salisbury's majority of 116 in 1886 was given him by a majority of 77,000 electors, and that was " the voice of the nation," whilst Mr. Gladstone's majority of 40 in 1892 was given him by a majority of 245,000 electors, but that was not the voice of the nation.

NESTING IN A LETTER-BOX.

REMARKABLE CASE IN CHESHIRE.

Some weeks ago one of the servants at the farmstead of Mr. Hewitt, Ilelmestone Hall,Oulten Lowe,Tarporley, made a strange discovery upon opening the letter-box at the gate, some distance from the hall. Ihe letter-box, intended for the reception of newspapers, letters, and small parcels, is of considerable size, about half-a-yard deep, over a foot wide, and six inches across. The slot is open and without a flap either inside or outside. To the astonishment of the servant a couple of:tom-tits were busily preparing a nest at the bottom of the box. Upon the discovery being reported to Mr. Hewitt, he ordered that nobody was to interfere with the tiny couple,but at the same time that the box must be open for use in the ordinarY way. The business of building now went on rapidly, and the box began to fill with moss, hair, Sce., day by day till there was a depth of four or five inches of the soft bedding. The next step was the forming of a nice round nest, just in the centre, and upon which one would suppose that all the letters and newspapers would drop down. However, the laying of eggs and the hatch- ing went on undisturbed, and when the box was ex- amined at the beginning of this week it was found that the hen had no fewer than fourteen young ones under her wings, " all alive and doing well," as the saying is.

JABEZ BALFOUR IN BUENOS AYRES

According to the London " Evening News" and " Post" the Buenos Ayres correspondent of a paper de- voted to South American interests thus refers to Jabez Balfour, whose extradition is being sought for by the British Goveinment :—" Liberator Balfour is with us, and his appearance is well-known in English circles. As the Argentine Government has refused to give him up he 1 takes life easy, and strolls about town ogling the girls. i His alias is ' Mr. Butler,' and a friend of mine who lodged in the same house with him when he first arrived (now he has taken a grand chalet at Flores), says' He is a very nice old gentleman, though such a horrid cad in appearance.*
